# PROSPERO

## ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

->crept into the subject of herbal materials, are detected;<-

# TO GIOVANNI
# MOCENIGO
## SON OF ANTONIO
### Most Illustrious Venetian Patrician.

## PROSPER ALPINI SENDS GREETINGS.

<image-desc>A small woodcut ornament of a winged cherub's head.</image-desc>

The knowledge of plants, most illustrious Giovanni Mocenigo <note>original: "Ioannes Maurocene"; the Mocenigo were one of the most prominent ducal families of Venice</note>, was first discovered by the great Chiron the Centaur, son of Saturn, or by Apollo, or finally by Aesculapius <note>The Greek god of medicine</note> (whom blind mortality once worshipped as Gods), as Pythagoras, renowned for his wisdom, and others have recorded. Afterwards, having perceived the admirable powers of these plants for the health of bodies among all people, many not only of the wisest Philosophers—among whom was the man of famous name, Democritus, who traveled through many regions out of love for this science—but even the greatest Kings (whose names, signifying this, are preserved in certain plants to this day <note>A reference to plants like Gentiana, named after King Gentius, or Lysimachia, after King Lysimachus</note>) have labored intensely in their study. It is, therefore, no reason for anyone to find it strange if I myself, concerning the plants which I formerly inspected and observed in the regions of Egypt, have now brought to light a book written by me

<summary>In this dedication, the physician Prosper Alpini addresses the Venetian nobleman Giovanni Mocenigo, tracing the divine and royal pedigree of botanical science from ancient mythology to the present. He justifies his own research in Egypt as part of a long tradition of philosophical and noble inquiry into the medicinal powers of nature.</summary>

<keywords>Giovanni Mocenigo, Prosper Alpini, history of botany, Egypt, medicinal plants, Chiron, Democritus, Venice, dedication</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedicatory preface of Prospero Alpini's work on Egyptian botany, transitioning from the general purpose of the research to the specific dedication to a noble patron.</meta>

...about to publish, I wished to dedicate this to you, a man illustrious for the nobility of your lineage and the brilliance of your singular virtues; especially since I know that you take the greatest delight in the study of all disciplines. For I have hoped that you would receive no small amount of pleasure from this new history of plants <note>original: "stirpium historia"</note>. There is also the fact that this Book of Plants <note>original: "Plantarum liber"</note>—which I had recently joined together with other books called *On the Medicine of the Egyptians* <note>original: "de medicina Ægyptiorum"</note> and dedicated to your most illustrious father—must now be published separately for the convenience of students. Therefore, it ought to be offered to you as the closest representative of your father, to whom, among others, it had first been destined. I have done this all the more gladly so that I might make my deep respect clearly seen toward you and your remaining brothers, to whom I have always been most devoted. Receive, therefore, most distinguished sir, this small gift from me with a cheerful countenance (for if I had anything greater or more excellent now, of which I know you are worthy, I would have offered it to you even more gladly); and I ask you again and again that you defend it from the tongues of detractors. Farewell, may you be long happy and mindful of me. At Venice, in the year of human salvation 1592.

<summary>In this concluding section of the preface, Prospero Alpini dedicates his botanical treatise to a noble patron, noting that while the work was originally paired with his writings on Egyptian medicine, it is now being issued separately for the benefit of scholars.</summary>
<keywords>Prospero Alpini, dedication, Egyptian medicine, botany, Venice, 1592, history of plants</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
### TO THE EXCELLENT READER.

<image-desc>Large decorative woodcut drop-cap 'P' featuring intricate scrollwork and floral motifs.</image-desc>

AFTER the books on the medicine of the Egyptians <note>original: "medicina Aegyptiorum"</note>, behold, excellent reader, we present to you this small volume, which is *On the Plants of Egypt* <note>original: "De plantis Aegypti"</note>. We had originally joined this work with those previous books, but now, looking out for your convenience, we have seen to it that it be printed separately. Receive, therefore, with a cheerful spirit, this small gift of mine. In this work, we seem at least to have achieved this much: that on account of the variety of subjects, the history of foreign plants <note>original: "stirpium historiam"</note>, and the likenesses <note>original: "effigies"</note> of the same—which are, in my opinion, excellently drawn—we believe we have written something not entirely unworthy of more refined ears. Farewell.

<page-num>I</page-num>
<header>ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT</header>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut headpiece with elaborate scrollwork, featuring two human-like figures among foliage and various animals, including a bird and a squirrel-like mammal.</image-desc>

# THE BOOK OF PROSPER ALPINUS

## ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

### BOOK I.

->WHY ONE MUST TREAT OF THE PLANTS<-
->growing in Egyptian locations. Chapter I.<-

->ALPINUS, GUILANDINUS.<-

<note>This work is written as a dialogue between Prosper Alpinus, the author and a physician who spent years in Egypt, and Melchior Guilandinus, the director of the botanical garden in Padua. This was a common literary device in the Renaissance to make scientific information more engaging.</note>

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut initial 'Q' containing a scene of a person seated on the ground near a tree in a landscape.</image-desc>

**ALPINUS:** It is now the fifth day since we began meeting in this garden for the sake of conversation. In this place, once I have brought the account of the medicine practiced among the Egyptians to its conclusion, I believe nothing else remains for us to say regarding their medical art. Therefore, we shall refresh our minds today with other discourses in the usual retreat of this most pleasant garden, sitting under the shade of these trees.

**GUILANDINUS:** What? Do there not still remain the plants of those places to be identified? You promised elsewhere that you would also speak of them. Indeed, because of their various uses in healing, their history seems to belong most closely to the discussions we have held until now concerning the medicine of the Egyptians. For we hope to receive no less usefulness and pleasure from these plants than from those medical topics, especially since no one until now has correctly observed or recorded them—particularly regarding their true appearances <note>original: "ichones," referring to the botanical illustrations</note>, which truly no one up to this point has known correctly or accurately. This is certainly evident in the case of the Egyptian Cassia <note>original: "Cassia Aegyptiaca"; likely *Cassia fistula*</note>, which the common sort of physicians call the "pipe" or "laxative cassia"; the *Paliurus* <note>The Christ's Thorn Jujube</note> of Athenaeus, which they call *Napca*; and from the *alcanna* of Avicenna—namely the Egyptian privet, called *Cypro* <note>original: "Cypro"; the Henna plant (*Lawsonia inermis*)</note> by some. It is also true of the balsam, the Acacia, the Lycium <note>A medicinal extract from thorny shrubs</note>, the Tamarisk, the Egyptian Bean, the *Stratiotes* <note>The "water soldier" plant</note>, and...

<summary>This opening page introduces a dialogue between Alpinus and Guilandinus in a Paduan garden, where they resolve to discuss the specific flora of Egypt following their previous talks on Egyptian medical practice.</summary>
<keywords>Prosper Alpinus, Melchior Guilandinus, Egyptian botany, Cassia, Henna, botanical illustration, Renaissance medicine</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Prosper Alpinus and Melchior Guilandinus from the previous page. Guilandinus has just asked Alpinus to share his knowledge of Egyptian plants, which Alpinus now prepares to do as they sit in the shade of a botanical garden in Padua.</meta>

<page-num>I</page-num>
<header>ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT</header>
<sig>A</sig>
<meta>catchword: trees</meta>

...that they are entirely unknown to many other students of plants of our time is clearly manifest. Therefore, a history of Egyptian plants, so desired by everyone, must not be passed over by you in silence now.

**ALPINUS:** Since it pleases you so much to hear from me about these plants which the Egyptian lands produce, why should I, who am most devoted to you, not clearly comply with your wish? I will attempt, therefore, on this day, as diligently as I can, to reveal them all to you, and moreover, what medicinal uses they have among those peoples.

**GUILANDINUS:** Indeed, I could never doubt your great kindness and benevolence toward me. Therefore, nothing more desirable or pleasing could have happened to me. I give you many thanks now; and this readiness of your mind toward me should now be extolled with much praise, which I will refrain from doing, lest I offend your great modesty. But let us leave these things aside now, and not waste today's time on them. Therefore, since we are seated in this pleasant retreat of the garden where the sun's rays cannot reach, under that shade, the hour suggests that you make a beginning of the history of those plants; and starting first from the account of trees, speak of the *cassia fistula*.

***

## *On the cassia fistula, called Chaiar xambar. Chapter II.*

->ALPINUS.<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap initial 'C' featuring a water bird, likely a swan or goose, among scrolling floral vine work.</image-desc> The *Cassia fistula* <note>The Golden Shower tree, known for its long, dark pods and medicinal properties</note>, which the Egyptians call *Chaiar xambar* <note>original: "Chaiarxambar," from the Arabic *khiyar shanbar*</note>, is a tree very similar to the walnut in its trunk, branches, and leaves. The trunk has a smooth, soft, ash-colored bark, exactly like what is seen on a growing walnut tree. The leaves are much more numerous than in the walnut, hanging attached to "wings" <note>term: wings (alae). Alpinus refers to the pinnate leaf structure where leaflets are attached to a central stalk</note>; on these wings, ten leaflets are seen, and nearly that many are observed hanging from almost every wing, five placed in order on each side. The leaves of this tree are also twice as long as walnut leaves and pointed at the tip. It bears many golden flowers, very similar to wallflowers <note>original: "leucoijs"</note> but much larger and more numerous, like the flowers of the bean-clover <note>original: "anagyridis"</note>, attached to and hanging from the wings; indeed, all the wings are seen to weigh down, heavy with almost innumerable flowers. These are also very fragrant, especially at sunrise. The Egyptians are accustomed to walk through the groves of Cassia trees quite often at dawn for the sake of the scent; for they are delighted by the breeze carried to them from the Cassias, which breathe out an extraordinary and most pleasing fragrance. For among them, when in bloom...

<summary>Alpinus agrees to describe Egyptian plants to Guilandinus, beginning with the Cassia fistula tree, which he describes as resembling a walnut tree but with fragrant golden flowers.</summary>
<keywords>Cassia fistula, Chaiar xambar, Egypt, botanical description, walnut tree, medicinal plants, fragrance</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues Prosper Alpinus's detailed botanical description of the Cassia fistula (Golden Shower tree) in Egypt, specifically focusing on its flowering cycle, the maturation of its pods, and the methods of preservation and trade used by the local population.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>BOOK I.</header>

[around these] trees the morning air perpetually gives off an exceptional and sweeter fragrance. Each flower has in its center many very thin filaments, similar to the flowerets of roses, which, growing little by little, are later changed into thicker tubes <note>original: *fistulae*. The author uses this term to describe the long, pipe-like seed pods of the Cassia tree</note>. At first, these very thin filaments are seen to be slanted and twisted; then, as they increase more, they become thicker and straighter; and finally, having reached their proper size, they become perfectly straight, like reeds <note>original: *calami*</note>. The larger pods <note>original: *fistulae*</note> are seen to be the size of a thicker cane and nearly two cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, approximately 18 to 22 inches, roughly the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</note> in length. The tree begins to flower in the month of June; it also flowers throughout the whole month of July in Alexandria and Cairo. However, in Pelusium <note>An ancient city on the eastern edge of the Nile Delta</note>, now called Damietta, I have even seen cassias flowering in the month of September. The canes or pods ripen over the space of a whole year, and they always remain on the tree throughout the year; the Egyptians collect them in Cairo only in the month of June, at which time many small ones, recently produced from the first flowers, are seen green, very similar to the pods of kidney beans <note>original: *phaeseolorum*</note>. Growing a little later, these gradually change their green color into black. These fresh, small, green pods they preserve in sugar, after first boiling them in water. A huge quantity of these thus preserved is exported to various regions. Those gathered from the trees in Pelusium have a thicker bark and contain little substance inside. But the Cairene and Alexandrian ones consist of a thinner bark and are full of much substance inside, for which reason these are preferred over all others for their quality. These, as has also been said elsewhere, are of two distinct kinds: for some others are reddish, which they call *Abes* <note>original: "Abes". Alpinus suggests the name refers to the skin tone of the *Abassini* or Abyssinians (modern-day Ethiopians)</note> from the color of those people; these are seen to be the best of all; and others are black. What some have said about cassias—that the best pods do not make a sound when shaken by hand—is observed there to be clearly false, since all fresh ones make a sound when moved; and even those living near the trees hear daily that the pods moved by the winds produce much sound from the seeds moving inside. This is something that sweet pods <note>original: *dulces siliquae*, likely referring to the carob tree (*Ceratonia siliqua*), common in the Mediterranean</note> also do. Those cassias which do not sound are criticized by the Egyptians, who think this happens because of watery moisture collected inside, causing the pulp to spoil. Nor are they gathered unripe, as some report. But they are very often bought by foreign merchants when they are worn out by age, since a huge quantity is often kept in houses prepared for that purpose for up to forty years. For once gathered from the tree, they immediately shut them in suitable places so that no air may enter, since cassias are spoiled by the slightest contact with air. For this reason, they are very often sent to us spoiled by age and even sour. On account of which, I would wish [they] were asked

<sig>A 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: all</meta>

<summary>Alpinus describes the morphology, seasonal growth, and commercial harvesting of Cassia fistula in Egypt. He details how the pods are preserved in sugar, how their quality is judged by sound and origin, and warns that imported cassia is often degraded by age or air exposure.</summary>
<keywords>Cassia fistula, Golden Shower tree, Alexandria, Cairo, Damietta, medicinal plants, Egyptian trade, botanical preservation, Abyssinian cassia, seed pods</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Prosper Alpinus's dialogue with Melchior Guilandinus regarding the medicinal properties and commercial selection of the Cassia fistula (Golden Shower tree) in Egypt. The previous page ended describing the maturation of the green pods into black ones.</meta>

<header>ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT</header>

...all pharmacists and physicians, so that they do not let themselves be deceived hereafter, and may always select fresh ones from among them, whose substance is somewhat sweet, and avoid the old ones that taste sour and astringent. The Egyptians do not use cassia <note>original: *cassijs*</note> when it is very fresh, but only after at least the fourth month; for the younger and fresher ones are observed to be useless and not free from harm. Indeed, they use—as our people do as well—the pulp of the pods <note>original: *fistularum*; literally "pipes," referring to the long, cylindrical seed pods</note> prepared either in boluses <note>A bolus is a large, soft pill or a rounded mass of medicinal substance</note> or in a drink. This pulp is temperate in heat and moist beyond the first degree <note>This refers to the Galenic system of humors, where medicines were classified by degrees of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness</note>; it draws out bile and also phlegm from the stomach and the mesenteric <term>mesentery|The fold of membrane that attaches the intestines to the abdominal wall</term> veins, and it tempers hotter blood and makes it purer. The variety called *Abisfina* <note>Abyssinian cassia</note> is preferred over the others in the city of Cairo for bilious and melancholic diseases, and especially for afflictions of the chest caused by the dripping of sharp humors, which it helps wonderfully. They are accustomed to use this pulp with white sugar and licorice as a secret remedy for faults of the kidneys and bladder: for it extinguishes the excessive heat of the kidneys, wipes away the humors from these parts, and expels them through the urine; hence, by frequent use of it, people protect themselves from stones <note>original: *calculis*; kidney or bladder stones</note>. They also use it for chronic cough, shortness of breath, asthma, and orthopnea <note>original: *orthopnoeam*; a condition where one can only breathe comfortably while standing or sitting upright</note> along with agaric <note>A type of medicinal fungus, often the Larch agaric, used as a purgative</note>. For hot pains of the joints, and especially in gout <note>original: *podagra*</note>, they apply it as a plaster to the aching parts; they also follow its use in the same way for hot inflammations. Indeed, they preserve the pods of the cassia—just like small green kidney beans—having first boiled them slightly in water and then dried them in the shade to remove moisture, with sugar or honey; they keep them for the use of children and delicate women. They administer them, at most, up to four ounces, and one ounce for children. And they use these for the same aforementioned conditions. 

**GUILANDINUS** <note>Melchior Guilandinus (c. 1520–1589), a Prussian physician and botanist who was a contemporary of Alpinus</note>: This history of the cassia has pleased me, but I desire that you show me the illustration <note>original: *figuram*</note> of both the cassia and the other plants you have observed, since I know for certain that you have sketched and painted them all properly—something no one has correctly provided for us before. Therefore, once I have seen the image <note>original: *ichone*</note> of the cassia tree, proceed to tell me about the other plants.

### CASSIA FISTULA, KHIYAR SHAMBAR <note>original: *Cassia fistula, Chaiarxamrar*. The Latin *fistula* means "pipe," referring to the shape of the seed pods; *Chaiarxamrar* is a Latinization of the Arabic name *khiyār shanbar*.</note>.

<image-desc>A botanical woodcut illustration of a Cassia fistula tree (commonly known as the Golden Shower tree). The tree is depicted with a single, textured trunk and a full crown of branches. It features pinnate leaves and numerous long, thin, cylindrical seed pods hanging vertically from the branches, alongside clusters of flowers. The tree is shown growing from a small mound of earth at the bottom of the frame.</image-desc>

<vocab>Pipe Cassia, Khiyar Shambar, Egypt, botanical illustration</vocab>

...all pharmacists and physicians, so that they do not let themselves be deceived hereafter, and may always select fresh ones from among them, whose substance is somewhat sweet, and avoid the old ones that taste sour and astringent. The Egyptians do not use cassia <note>original: *cassijs*</note> when it is very fresh, but only after at least the fourth month; for the younger and fresher ones are observed to be useless and not free from harm. Indeed, they use—as our people do as well—the pulp of the pods <note>original: *fistularum*; literally "pipes," referring to the long, cylindrical seed pods</note> prepared either in boluses <note>A bolus is a large, soft pill or a rounded mass of medicinal substance</note> or in a drink. This pulp is temperate in heat and moist beyond the first degree <note>This refers to the Galenic system of humors, where medicines were classified by degrees of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness</note>; it draws out bile and also phlegm from the stomach and the mesenteric <term>mesentery|The fold of membrane that attaches the intestines to the abdominal wall</term> veins, and it tempers hotter blood and makes it purer. The variety called Abyssinian <note>original: *Abisfina*</note> is preferred over the others in the city of Cairo for bilious and melancholic diseases, and especially for afflictions of the chest caused by the dripping of sharp humors, which it helps wonderfully. They are accustomed to use this pulp with white sugar and licorice as a secret remedy for faults of the kidneys and bladder: for it extinguishes the excessive heat of the kidneys, wipes away the humors from these parts, and expels them through the urine.

### *On the sweet pods, called carub by the Egyptians.*
#### Chapter III.

->A L P I N U S.<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'C' featuring scrolling vine and leaf motifs.</image-desc>
**CLOSE** to the Cassia is the tree called *carub* or *carnub* by the Egyptians; since it grows in Egypt, I was also obliged to speak of it. The tree is very well known to us, as there is a great abundance of these trees in many parts of Italy, such as those seen in the Neapolitan fields and in Apulia; indeed, they are cultivated most extensively in those places. From this tree, the Egyptians recognize only the use of the pods. They extract a very sweet honey <note>original: "mel"; here referring to a thick syrup or molasses</note> from them, which the Arabs use in place of sugar to preserve fresh and small laxative cassias, ginger, myrobalans <term>myrobalanos: a variety of dried plum-like fruits used in early medicine as a purgative</term>, tamarinds, and many other fruits. The use of this "honey" is also very frequent in place of true honey for enemas <term>Clysteres: a liquid injection used to clear the bowels</term>, and some even allow it to be taken by mouth to loosen the bowels; for by its use, the belly is moved to excretion no less than by the pulp of the cassia. They also use it for inflammations of the kidneys, applying it both internally and externally. For those with coughs or asthma, they administer it no differently than cassia, and it helps them greatly; a decoction <note>a concentrated liquor resulting from heating or boiling the plant substance</note> prepared from the pods provides these same benefits. But now let us speak of the Egyptian thorn, called acacia, which has remained unknown to many until now.

**GUILANDINUS.** Nothing could be more pleasing to me now than to hear you speak of this plant, as I know you have clearly discovered the truth of it in those regions.

## On the Acacia, Chapter IV.

->A L P I N U S.<-

The ACACIA, which the Egyptians call *Sant* <note>original: "Sant"; likely referring to the Acacia nilotica</note>, grows in locations in Egypt far from the sea; and these trees come forth most abundantly in the mountains of Sinai <note>original: "Synai"</note>, situated near the Red Sea. They grow to the size of a mulberry tree <note>original: "mori"</note>, and spread their branches quite widely upwards; a fact which Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, an ancient Greek physician whose work "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical reference for centuries</note> also mentions...

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical description of the Acacia tree, specifically identifying it with the "Egyptian thorn" mentioned by ancient authorities like Dioscorides. It transitions from Alpini’s physical description to a commentary by the botanist Melchior Guilandino regarding the trade of its medicinal extract.</meta>

ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

...continued <note>original: "nuit"; the suffix of a verb from the previous page, likely "meminit," meaning "he mentioned"</note>, saying that the tree does not raise itself straight up. The trunk is seen to be about the size of a **plum tree** and has a black, rough bark, armed with many very sharp spines. The trees that are observed in the aforementioned mountains appear quite formidable with their white spines, and the trunk is seen to have a very rough bark. It has small, oblong leaves that are very finely divided, most similar to **tansy** <note>a flowering herb with fern-like leaves</note>. The flowers are small, pale yellowish, or even white; they are round and imitate small tufts of wool, clearly similar in shape to the fruits of the **plane tree**, though much smaller than those. Indeed, the flower of this tree appears to be nothing other than a soft down forming a small round globe, and it possesses a not unpleasant scent. Those that are cultivated in **Cairo** <note>original: "Cayri"</note> have yellow or pale flowers, while those in the mountains of **Arabia** have white ones. 

From the flowers, pods resembling **lupines** are produced, though smaller than them; they appear green at first and soon turn black. The seeds are similar to the seeds of **sweet pods** <note>referring to the carob mentioned on the previous page</note> and are enclosed within the casings of the pods. The leaves, flowers, and fruit strike the palate with much harshness and astringency. From the immature green pods, they express a juice which they cook until it hardens. Some also prepare a juice from the leaves and undeveloped flowers, but this is inferior in quality. The **tanners of Cairo** consume a great quantity of that juice for blackening hides. 

Both male and female versions of this tree are found. The male is indeed bristling with spines and does not produce fruit. The female is filled with fewer and softer spines, and she does not have them among the inner branches, but only on the outside. This tree is very fertile and begins to bloom at the rising of the **Pleiades** <note>a star cluster whose appearance traditionally marked the changing of seasons</note> in the month of November, and again at their setting, namely in the month of March; thus it bears and ripens fruit twice a year. Without a doubt, it must be affirmed from these characteristics that this tree is the true **Acacia** of the ancients, or the **Egyptian thorn**; especially because no other thorny tree is found in Egypt that more closely corresponds to the traits attributed to the acacia, and even more so because they also call it *Kachia* <note>original: "Kachiam"; likely a transliteration of the Arabic 'al-qāqiyā'</note>.

->GUILANDINUS<- <note>Melchior Guilandino, a 16th-century Prussian physician and botanist who served as the director of the botanical garden in Padua</note>

From this, I cannot help but wonder greatly at the many who do not know that the true juice of the acacia is brought here from Egypt every day. For now, there are few **apothecaries** <term>pharmacopolae: historical term for those who prepared and sold medicinal drugs</term> in **Padua** and **Venice** who are unaware of that juice, which they call **Oriental Acacia**, to distinguish it from the juice that many substitute for acacia, which is prepared by some from **wild plums** <note>referring to the sloe, used as a common European substitute for the more expensive Egyptian acacia extract</note>. Nor do I wonder any less at some people, because they have not received knowledge of this plant from the physicians or apothecaries of the most illustrious Venetian consuls who lived in Egypt for many years, rather than... 

<column-break/>

# BOOK

...from Greece, which none of the ancient writers ever described as being fertile in acacia; indeed, many, because this plant lives in **Egypt**, named it the **Egyptian thorn** <note>original: "spinam Aegyptiam"</note> itself. Therefore, a genus of plant of this kind is not to be sought from Greece—as **Pietro Andrea Mattioli** <note>a famous 16th-century Italian doctor and botanist</note> says he obtained from **Byzantium** <note>modern-day Istanbul</note>, in which matter he was not a little deceived—but rather from Egypt, where all writers have affirmed that acacia grows most abundantly. And I think it is clearly known to you from what has been said that the image of that acacia which Mattioli painted is plainly false. 

**ALPINUS**: Another thing regarding the acacia must be noted, as many have erred so far as to think that the **gum** <note>referring to Gum Arabic</note>, which many recall this tree bearing, is certainly not found among us, nor is the true kind imported from Egypt, because **Dioscorides** <note>an ancient Greek physician and the father of pharmacognosy</note> reported that it consists of a **worm-like shape** <note>original: "forma vermiculari"</note>. They say that this gum, which they falsely call "Arabic," is instead similar to the gums of other trees, namely **cherries, plums, peaches, apples, and pears**. For this reason, they believe that the true Arabic or Egyptian gum, which is produced in the trunk of the acacia tree as herbalists have reported, is not found at all. 

**GUILANDINO**: What do you think about this matter? I truly think these claims are nonsense. 

**ALPINUS**: This much I certainly know: with these very hands in **Cairo** <note>original: "Cayri"</note>, in many gardens filled with acacia trees, I have very often collected much gum from their trunks. I did this especially in the garden situated near the house of the **Venetian consul**, in which there are many acacia trees from which gum flows. With all due respect to **Dioscorides** <note>original: "pace Dioscoridis"</note>, I would say that not all of it, but very little, is seen in a worm-like shape, nor is it all white and clear. For indeed, not all gums of the acacia tree are seen in a single color or a single shape. The gum takes such a form or shape as is dictated by the movement of the sap flowing from the bark; for sometimes when it is quite thick, it does not move far downward, but hardens almost immediately after being excreted, and it then becomes a **granular gum**. However, when a thinner and more fluid sap comes out, it sometimes produces an oblong gum. In a word, I say that I have seen acacia gums that are not at all unlike the gums of cherries or plums in shape and color. Furthermore, you should know that the entire quantity of gums brought to us from Egypt is from acacia trees, since there is no tree there except this one that produces gums. Indeed, Egypt and **Arabia** entirely lack plums, cherries, apples, and other gum-bearing trees. Consequently, the Arabs and Egyptians collect gums from no other tree than the acacia. 

**GUILANDINO**: You speak the truth, but pray tell me also of its **medical uses**, which you have properly observed among those people?

# ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

d

...you seen? **ALPINUS**: The Egyptians use the juice of the **acacia** tree, which is squeezed from the unripe pods, or even from the flowers, leaves, or the fruits themselves. Among them, the use of this juice is extremely frequent for strengthening the eyes and for protecting them against inflammations, which are very common in that region. It is also used for all ulcers of the mouth and ears, as well as for cracked lips. To strengthen and fix loose teeth, they use the juice reduced to a powder; after washing the area with a **decoction** <note>a concentrated liquor made by boiling medicinal herbs</note> prepared from the same juice, or from the leaves or flowers, they sprinkle the powder over the gums and rub it onto the teeth. 

To those spitting blood, they offer a **drachma** <note>a small unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams</note> dissolved in some liquid to be drunk every morning. It is well known among them that this has always had wonderful power for halting any kind of **flux of blood** <note>hemorrhage or bleeding</note>. I know for certain that, by this remedy alone, I have very often seen many women—who were nearly dead from excessive uterine discharge—revived and cured. In those who suffer from these excessive evacuations, they use **clysters** <note>an internal wash or enema</note> prepared from a decoction of acacia leaves and flowers, injected into the uterus; they also use the powdered juice dissolved and mixed into the aforementioned decoction. 

In cases of bowel flux <note>diarrhea or dysentery</note>, they frequently use clysters in the same manner, and also successfully administer the juice by mouth. For a **prolapsed** <note>a condition where an organ slips forward or down from its normal position</note> anus or uterus, they have nothing more excellent or more frequently used than the juice dissolved with a decoction of the leaves and flowers. For those who have weak joints from any cause, they consider a **fomentation** <note>a warm, medicinal compress</note> prepared from all these parts to be an immediate help—and not without reason, since acacia is endowed with a very powerful **astringent** <note>a substance that causes the contraction of skin cells and other body tissues</note> quality. 

Everyone uses a fomentation of it to protect the joints from the **downflow of humors** <note>referring to the ancient medical theory that diseases were caused by an imbalance or movement of bodily fluids</note>, and no less so when the joints are swollen and inflamed, especially at the beginning while the humors are still in motion. Hence, its use for **gouty pains** <note>original: "podagricos dolores"</note> is very frequent, and acacia has the greatest power to preserve men from attacks of **gout** <note>original: "podagra"</note>. 

There are some who use acacia for these evils not only externally as a fomentation or bath, but are also accustomed to taking a small amount of the juice by mouth for several days before the "time of flux" <note>the expected onset of a seasonal or recurring illness</note>, so that the internal passages, having been made narrower, may yield less to the course of the humors. Some also prepare a liniment from acacia juice and vinegar for smearing on weakened joints, by which they truly strengthen and firm them in a wonderful way. I have known many who were recently attacked by gout to have fully recovered through the constant use of acacia; every day, for at least an hour, they held their feet in a warm decoction of acacia and bathed them with it,

<meta>catchword: and feet</meta>

B O O K.

### On the Paliurus of Athenaeus, called Nabca. Chapter V.

->A L P I N V S.<-

There is a tree in Egypt which they call the **Nabca** <note>The Christ’s Thorn Jujube, *Ziziphus spina-christi*</note>. It is seen to be the size of a lotus tree, thorny in the manner of an acacia, bearing leaves very close to those of the jujube, though somewhat wider. Its flowers are also clearly similar to jujube flowers—white and small—from which burst forth little rounded fruits. These are fragrant, sweet, and very pleasant to the taste, rivaling the large so-called Marostican cherries <note>Marostica, in Italy, remains famous for its cherries today</note>; they are held in great value among the Egyptian and Turkish elites. The tree flowers and ripens its fruit twice a year, specifically in the spring and autumn, and is extremely prolific. However, the spring fruits do not ripen perfectly, nor are they as complete as those in the autumn; indeed, many of them rot before they can mature. In autumn, they turn out most perfect and at their best.

**GUILAND.** Based on the writings of Athenaeus <note>A Greek rhetorician and grammarian, author of the *Deipnosophistae*</note>, it is certain that this tree is the <term>paliurus</term> <note>A generic classical term for thorny shrubs, often applied to the *Ziziphus* genus</note> and what was once called the *connorum* by the people of Alexandria. Regarding this tree, he wrote as follows in Book 14: Agathocles of Cyzicus mentions them in these words in the third book of his *Histories of His Fatherland*:
<margin>Chapter 20.</margin>
> "When a lightning bolt struck a tomb, a tree sprouted from the mound which they call the *connorum*. It is a plant the size of an elm or a pitch-pine, and no smaller. It has numerous, long trunks and slightly protruding thorns. The leaf is tender and green, very similar to the *phya* <note>original: 'phyae'; an unidentified plant mentioned in Greek botanical fragments</note>. It produces fruit twice every year, namely in spring and autumn. This fruit is entirely sweet and similar to the fruit of the olive and the wild olive. It has flesh and a stone <note>original: 'os' (bone/pit)</note> similar to those, but it differs in the pleasure of its juice; its fruit is even eaten when green."

**ALPIN.** I certainly believe the aforementioned tree is the *connorum*, or the same *paliurus* that Athenaeus described. This is so even if the fruit and its stone do not seem entirely similar to the Nabca fruit and stone—since the Nabca are similar to small apples and their stones are round, rather than oblong like those seen in olives. However, I have never observed that they make a flour from those dried fruits anywhere in Egypt. Their primary use is as food while fresh and green; indeed, it is their custom to eat them just as we eat cherries. But now let us move on to explaining its use in medicine.

**GUILAND.** The use of the fruits seems to be very well known to the Arabs, about which Serapio <note>Serapion the Younger, an influential 12th-century Arab botanist and physician</note> wrote thus: There are two species of **Sadar** <note>The Arabic name 'Sidr', used for species of *Ziziphus*</note>, one is...

<summary>Alpinus describes the Nabca tree (Christ's Thorn Jujube) of Egypt, which Guilandinus identifies with the "connorum" mentioned in the classical texts of Athenaeus and Agathocles. They discuss its physical characteristics, its twice-yearly harvest, and its status as a delicacy before transitioning to its medicinal properties.</summary>

<keywords>Nabca, Ziziphus spina-christi, Christ's Thorn, Athenaeus, Agathocles of Cyzicus, botanical identification, Egyptian fruit, Serapio, medicinal plants</keywords>

# ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

,, ...the *Affri* <note>Likely a reference to a specific variety or local name for the *Ziziphus*</note>, and another is the *adhal*: but the *affri* does not have sharp thorns. The leaves of both species are broad and round. The *nabuch* <note>The fruit of the tree, commonly called nabukh</note> from the *adhel* are small; the best among them are those from that specific region which are offered to kings. These are sweeter than all others and have a better scent; they perfume the breath of the eater and even their clothing, just like other spices. The medicinal property of these fruits is cold and dry in the first degree <note>A classification in Galenic medicine where plants were rated 1–4 based on their effect on the four humors</note>. When perfectly ripe, they possess a certain amount of moisture. They are all astringent before they ripen, and they are frequently used to firm up a looseness of the stomach and bowels. For these ailments, the juice expressed from the unripe fruits is commonly used, whether taken by mouth or administered by <term>enemas</term> <note>original: "Clysteribus"</note>. They also use these same fruits when dried and infused in water. This infusion is used, as mentioned, for the relaxation of the intestines and even for intestinal ulcers. However, a decoction or infusion of the dried, ripe fruits is a very common remedy for all pestilential fevers; for they say these fruits have a wonderful efficacy against poisonous qualities and rot, and they vigorously strengthen the heart. The juice of the perfectly ripe fruits is best for purging bile from the stomach and from the primary channels of the veins. In all putrid fevers, they freely use an infusion prepared from them to settle the heat. These fruits are extremely pleasant to eat, especially when ripe; however, they provide little nourishment and, like cherries, they easily spoil in the stomach if one indulges in eating them immoderately, which causes the stomach to be upset."

**GUILANDINUS.** There are not many who—and not without merit—reckon this

BOOK.

# THE NABCA, BELIEVED TO BE THE PALIURUS OF ATHENAEUS

<image-desc>A detailed botanical woodcut of a small tree or shrub, identified as Nabca. The plant features a single, textured trunk that branches out into a full canopy. The leaves are oval-shaped with clear, parallel venation. Interspersed among the leaves are small clusters of blossoms and round, berry-like fruits. The entire plant is depicted growing from a small mound of earth represented by horizontal hatching.</image-desc>

# ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

...ripe.

The *Affri* <note>Likely a reference to a specific regional variety of the *Ziziphus* or Sidr tree</note>, and another is the *adhal*: but the *affri* does not have sharp thorns. The leaves of both species are broad and round. The *nabuch* <note>The fruit of the tree, commonly known today as nabq or sidr fruit</note> from the *adhel* are small; the best among them are those from that specific region which are offered to kings. These are sweeter than all others and have a better scent; they perfume the breath of the eater and even their clothing, just like other spices. 

The medicinal property of these fruits is cold and dry in the first degree <note>In the Galenic system of medicine, plants were classified by their "complexion"—a combination of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness—on a scale of 1 to 4</note>. When perfectly ripe, they possess a certain amount of moisture. They are all astringent before they ripen, and they are frequently used to firm up a looseness of the stomach and bowels. For these ailments, the juice expressed from the unripe fruits is commonly used, whether taken by mouth or administered by <term>enemas</term> <note>original: "Clysteribus"</note>. 

They also use these same fruits when dried and infused in water. This infusion is used, as mentioned, for the relaxation of the intestines and even for intestinal ulcers. However, a decoction or infusion of the dried, ripe fruits is a very common remedy for all <term>pestilential fevers</term> <note>A term for highly contagious, often fatal fevers, such as those associated with the plague</note>; for they say these fruits have a wonderful efficacy against poisonous qualities and rot, and they vigorously strengthen the heart. The juice of the perfectly ripe fruits is best for purging bile from the stomach and from the primary channels of the veins. In all putrid fevers, they freely use an infusion prepared from them to settle the heat. These fruits are extremely pleasant to eat, especially when ripe.

## *On the Sycamore, called Giumez.* Chapter VI.

### A L P I N V S.

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap 'A' featuring floral motifs and a figure.</image-desc>

**A** very vast tree called *Giumez* <note>The Arabic name *jummayz* refers specifically to the sycamore fig, *Ficus sycomorus*.</note> by the Egyptians grows in Egypt, which our people call the Sycamore or the Egyptian fig. Most wise Nature brought forth this tall tree in the sandy and sterile places of Egypt, so that under it, people might sometimes protect themselves from the harm of the sun’s burning rays. It is indeed a very large tree, and it spreads its branches so widely that it provides great shade for travelers—something that is eagerly sought by everyone in such hot, sandy places that are almost set on fire by the heat of the sun. GVILAND. <note>Melchior Guilandinus, a contemporary botanist and correspondent of Alpini.</note> in book 2 of *On the powers of foods*, Galen wrote that he saw a Sycamore in Alexandria that was similar to a small fig; for this reason, I believe he looked upon the size of a Sycamore that was still growing and young, and not yet an adult. It is strange that he did not notice so grand a tree, common to everyone. Dioscorides, describing it better, said:

> » Some also call the Sycamore *ficaminum*, that is, a mulberry, whose fruit 
> » they call Sycomorus because of its tasteless flavor. <note>original: "propter inefficacem gustum." The name Sycomore is derived from the Greek *sykon* (fig) and *moron* (mulberry).</note> It is a 
> » large tree, similar to a fig; leafy, abounding in much milky sap, with 
> » leaves like a mulberry. It bears fruit three or four times a year, not 
> » on the branches like a fig, but from the trunk itself <note>This botanical trait is called cauliflory, where fruit grows directly on the main stem or trunk.</note>, not unlike a 
> » wild fig. It is sweeter than unripe figs and has no seeds inside. 
> » The fruit does not ripen unless it is scratched with a fingernail or an iron tool. ALPIN. 
> » This description certainly expresses the true form of the Sycamore, but where 

he says it has "leaves of a mulberry," I would add that they are thicker, whiter, and rougher, because these leaves differ from fig leaves only in size and shape. GVILAND. Why do the Egyptians say this tree has such a great similarity to the fig and the mulberry that the form of both is seen in it? ALPIN. Many of them affirm it is a grafting of a fig tree onto a mulberry, just as some were persuaded that the <term>Musa (banana or plantain)</term> was also grafted onto the root of the <term>Colocassia (taro)</term> or the sugar-bearing reed <note>original: "canne saccharū ferentis" (sugar cane).</note>; however, I have not yet learned this to be true through experience. Without doubt, in this tree, the trunk, branches, bark, fruit, milky sap, and the roughness and color of the leaves appear like a fig; yet the shape and size of the leaves correspond to the mulberry. The fruits ripen slowly and not perfectly, and they have a watery and pleasant taste; hence they say...

the coldness of the mulberry <note>original: "mori." This continues the comparison between the Sycamore and the mulberry tree from the previous page.</note> prevents the fruits from achieving perfect maturation. This tree, as many have also recalled, is so fertile that it is never without many fruits, which grow from the trunk and not at all from the branches; nor does this tree produce fruit unless it is first frequently gashed by many strikes, so that even the bark of the trunk is wounded. From these wounds, milk <note>A reference to the milky latex or sap common to the Ficus genus.</note> immediately flows out, from which a small branch grows, bearing sometimes three, sometimes five, sometimes seven, and even more figs. This tree does not grow from seeds, since the figs lack seeds, but it is propagated by grafted branches; it is among those that grow quickly and, what seems marvelous, live for a very long time.

A certain Sycamore is seen in Matarea <note>Matareea (Al-Matariyyah), an area near Cairo famous in Christian tradition for "The Virgin's Tree."</note>, which is believed by the inhabitants to be so ancient that everyone holds it for certain that once, in a certain cavity of its trunk, the Most Blessed Virgin, fleeing the wrath of Herod from Jerusalem, took refuge there, and hid the Christ child our Savior for many days. Because of this, that tree is held in great veneration by many, and especially the hollow of its trunk that hid Christ, which they confess to have been the spirit of the great God. What Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century botanist and physician.</note> says is fanciful: that the trunks and branches of this tree never dry out unless they are first thrown into water and stay there for a little while. 

The use of the figs in food is harmful to the stomach, and it loosens, weakens, and upsets it. Truly, those who have become hot either from travel or from the sun eat those figs beneficially, needing cooling and moistening: for these fruits are moderately cold and very moist. They easily lubricate and relax the bowels. They heal all hot and hard tumors when used in the manner of a plaster.

<header>B O O K 10</header>

### Concerning the Date Palm. Chapter VII.

->A L P I N V S.<-

The PALM is a most well-known tree, not only to the Egyptians, Arabs, Syrians, and other Eastern peoples, but also to many peoples of Italy; for this reason, I think I should speak of it briefly. It is a tree with a single trunk, rising straight up to a height of twenty paces and more. It has leaves similar to swords, falling downwards in the manner of reeds. It flowers in the spring. For a branch—provided with a certain long casing <note>original: "inuolucro", referring to the spathe of the palm</note>, shaped like a bladder, which remains closed until spring and is then opened—sends forth small, white, fragrant flowers that sit and hang in clusters downward from the casing. Indeed, in the month of March, those casings attached at their "wings" <note>likely the leaf axils</note> are opened, from which a mass of infinite threads leaps out, and the flowers are seen hanging from these threads or filaments. From these, small green dates <term>dactili [from the Greek 'daktylos', meaning finger]</term> are produced, mimicking clusters of grapes, which ripen in Autumn. These fruits are observed to vary among themselves in size, shape, and color. For some are seen to be long, large, and thick, just like the white plums (which ripen in the month of July in the district of Bassano <note>Bassano del Grappa, a town in the Veneto region of Italy</note>). Others are small, long, and similar to the acorns of oaks; others are round and thick. Some are green, some white, others yellow, and some red; all are harsh and styptic <term>styptico [astringent or mouth-puckering]</term> in taste when unripe, but very sweet when ripe. Those that are nearly ripe, however, represent the taste of chestnuts, but sweeter and more pleasant. This tree produces more abundant fruit only in alternate years, nor (which seems a truly wonderful thing to say) do the females conceive and bear fruit unless someone mingles branches of the male with the branches of the female, and allows them, as it were, to kiss. Most people, in order to make the females fertile, scatter not the branches, but the powder <note>pollen</note> found within the male casing over the branches and the "heart" of the females; or others are accustomed to scatter the male flowers instead of the powder. For unless the Egyptians do this, without doubt the females will either bear no fruit, or those they do bear they will not retain, nor will these ripen.

G V I L A N D I N O. That "conception" of palms, which you say is performed by art, seems to be contradicted by the innumerable dates produced in the deserts of Arabia; in which forests of wild palms are found, which without human cultivation produce, retain, and ripen excellent and copious fruit, from which I judge—

<sig>C 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: judge [continuing onto the next page]</meta>

<summary>Alpinus describes the physical characteristics, flowering habits, and varieties of the date palm, including the ancient practice of artificial pollination (the "marriage" of palms). Guilandino responds with skepticism, citing the existence of productive wild palm forests in Arabia that lack human intervention.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Alpinus and his interlocutor regarding the reproductive nature of the date palm. The previous page ended with a discussion on the astringent nature of the fruit; here, Alpinus addresses theories on how the trees are fertilized and describes their physical resilience and vast utility in Egyptian life.</meta>

# ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

<meta>catchword: contactu (contact)</meta>

... I say that the females are moved to fertility not by branches, or pollen, or the flowers of the males, as Pliny and others have also recorded, but rather—not unlike what other trees are accustomed to do through the contact of male and female roots—they prepare themselves for fertility.

ALPINUS: The Arabs, who are experts in the cultivation of palms, will answer that the fertility of palms in the deserts of Arabia, even if it does not happen by art (since in these places these trees, as was said, produce fruit without any cultivation), is aided by the winds, which carry the pollen <note>original: "pulueres", literally 'dusts'</note> and flowers from the branches of the males to the branches of the females. I do not think it should be said that the mutual joining of the roots of both sexes of palms is the cause of conception, since all palms are endowed with such short roots that even if they stand almost together, no contact of the roots can exist between them. It is a wonderful thing to say: the very tall and vast trunks <note>original: "caudices"</note> of the palms can stand up in the air with such short, thin, and few roots, and despite their great weight, they do not fall to the ground even when violent winds often shake them. Added to this is the fact that the lowest part of the trunk nearest the ground, which supports the weight of the whole tree, is much more slender and weaker than the rest of the trunk. Hence many of the Egyptians, led by this miracle, have said that palms are not nourished and live by the earth drawn in by the roots, but by the air from the higher part of the tree. They persuade themselves of this by this conjecture: because they have observed that if the higher part of the palm, which they call the **heart**, is cut off, the entire plant immediately withers; this does not happen if the lower part of the roots is cut away.

There is no other tree more useful to the human race than the palm, since they make beams for houses from the trunks; from the branches, they make walls and many other wooden vessels for use, which they name <term>Cuffaz [from the Arabic 'qafas', a crate or basket made of palm frond midribs]</term>. From the leaves they make fans and baskets; from the bark of the trunk, they make ropes for ships. Indeed, the utility of the fruits is well enough known to everyone. But I will now also say what use they themselves have for healing. From the fruits there are three things especially which they use for the sake of health: namely the sheath <note>original: "spartum", the protective covering of the flower cluster</note>, the powder contained in the sheath or casing, and the dates themselves. Therefore, they use the membrane of the sheath both made into a powder and in a decoction prepared from it. The powder has the greatest use when taken by mouth for diarrhea, lientery <term>lienteria [a type of diarrhea where food passes through the body undigested]</term>, and dysentery, and no less for stanching all other unnatural evacuations, whether of blood or of another humor; but especially for a hepatic flux <note>a historical term for a chronic bloody discharge thought to be caused by liver weakness</note>, hemorrhoids, and bloody discharge from the uterus, as well as for the spitting of blood. They also use this powder to stabilize spreading ulcers, for the relaxation of the uvula <note>original: "vuæ", the small fleshy part hanging at the back of the throat; 'relaxation' refers to it being swollen or elongated</note>, and for the teeth...

<summary>Alpinus discusses the fertilization of date palms via wind-borne pollen rather than root contact and marvels at the tree's unique anatomy and extensive medicinal and practical uses in Egypt.</summary>
<keywords>Date Palm, Phoenix dactylifera, fertilization, Arabic botany, Cuffaz, medicinal plants, Egyptian architecture, diarrhea treatments, lientery</keywords>

...disturbance. They also use a decoction for all the aforementioned purposes, but often with the aforementioned powder mixed into it. It assists weak joints and those prone to <term>fluxions</term><note>The flow of humors or fluids to a specific part of the body, often causing swelling or pain.</note> in a marvelous way, by firming and strengthening them. They very commonly use the white powder found within the <term>sheath</term><note>original: "inuolucro", referring to the spathe or protective covering of the flower cluster</note> during the springtime—specifically when the palms begin to flower—mixed with sugar to treat hoarseness of the throat, coughs, and <term>ophthalmias</term><note>Inflammatory conditions of the eye.</note>. This powder is sweet and slightly astringent; women use it most frequently to stabilize their menstrual cycles and to preserve a pregnancy. 

Immature dates <note>original: "dactili immaturi"</note> serve the same purpose, whether eaten as food or prepared as a decoction, to restrain the spitting of blood or any other evacuation of blood, as well as for <term>lientery</term><note>A type of diarrhea where food is passed through the bowels undigested.</note>, diarrhea, and dysentery. They are also used for vomiting blood, drying out hemorrhoids, and thoroughly healing simple ulcers and wounds. For these recently mentioned conditions, they very frequently use a syrup prepared from immature dates. They also use these same dates when fully ripe, at which time they are quite sweet with a subtle astringency; for this reason, they frequently employ them for hoarseness, coughs, shortness of breath, pleurisy, and <term>peripneumonia</term><note>Inflammation of the lungs or pneumonia.</note>. Furthermore, a decoction of these is frequently used to assist the <term>eruptions</term><note>original: "excretiones"</note> of smallpox in children.

# BOOK I.

## On the Sebesten. Chapter VIII.

### ALPINUS.

Two kinds of the **Sebesten** <note>The Sebesten (*Cordia myxa*), also known as the Assyrian plum, is a tree valued for its sticky, medicinal fruit.</note> tree growing in Egypt are found: one is a wild variety similar to a plum, and the other is a domestic variety, differing from the wild one by its wider and thicker leaves. Both trees bear small white flowers, very similar to those of the **geranium**, from which fruits grow that resemble small, round plums. Their pits are observed to be triple-ribbed. The wild tree bears smaller fruits that ripen slowly and are less perfect than those produced by the domestic Sebesten, which are larger and better. However, fruits can be seen on the tree throughout the entire year. They bloom in the Spring and ripen their fruit in the Autumn. From the pulp of these fruits, they prepare the finest **birdlime** <note>original: *viscum*; a sticky substance typically used to trap birds, but here refined for medical use as an adhesive or plaster.</note>; indeed, they do this by crushing and washing the ripe Sebesten. There are some who prepare the birdlime by chewing them, using the tongue to spit the pulp away from the pit; they then boil a quantity of these fruits collected in a vessel until the watery moisture is consumed. Everyone uses this birdlime in the manner of a **plaster** <note>original: *emplastri*; a medical dressing spread on cloth and applied to the skin.</note> for all **scirrhous tumors** <note>Hard, fibrous swellings or growths.</note>. The substance itself is warm in the first degree and moist in the second <note>This refers to the Galenic system of medicine, which classified treatments by their 'complexion' to balance the body's humors.</note>, and it possesses an attracting, thinning, and resolving power; it resolves all tumors and softens every type of hardness. Some people take **boluses** <note>Large, rounded medicinal pills.</note> prepared from it with white sugar and licorice root powder over many days to treat a cough. The most familiar use of the fruits is for **coughs**, **shortness of breath**, **orthopnea** <note>A condition where one can only breathe comfortably when standing or sitting upright.</note>, **pleurisy**, and **pneumonia**; it is also used for hoarseness of the voice by preparing a **decoction** <note>A concentrated liquor resulting from heating or boiling plant matter.</note> from them. They are also accustomed to use this decoction for many fevers, especially when the chest is suffering from some ailment. But now, let us speak of the **Tamarisk**.

---

# THE DOMESTIC SEBESTEN.

<image-desc>A detailed botanical woodcut illustration of a Sebesten Domestica branch (likely Cordia myxa). # BOOK I.

## On the Sebesten. Chapter VIII.

### ALPINUS.

Two kinds of the **Sebesten** <note>The Sebesten (*Cordia myxa*), also known as the Assyrian plum, is a tree valued for its sticky, medicinal fruit.</note> tree growing in Egypt are found: one is a wild variety similar to a plum, and the other is a domestic variety, differing from the wild one by its wider and thicker leaves. Both trees bear small white flowers, very similar to those of the **geranium**, from which fruits grow that resemble small, round plums. Their pits are observed to be triple-ribbed. The wild tree bears smaller fruits that ripen slowly and are less perfect than those produced by the domestic Sebesten, which are larger and better. However, fruits can be seen on the tree throughout the entire year. They bloom in the Spring and ripen their fruit in the Autumn. From the pulp of these fruits, they prepare the finest **birdlime** <note>original: *viscum*; a sticky substance typically used to trap birds, but here refined for medical use as an adhesive or plaster.</note>; indeed, they do this by crushing and washing the ripe Sebesten. There are some who prepare the birdlime by chewing them, using the tongue to spit the pulp away from the pit; they then boil a quantity of these fruits collected in a vessel until the watery moisture is consumed. Everyone uses this birdlime in the manner of a **plaster** <note>original: *emplastri*; a medical dressing spread on cloth and applied to the skin.</note> for all **scirrhous tumors** <note>Hard, fibrous swellings or growths.</note>. The substance itself is warm in the first degree and moist in the second <note>This refers to the Galenic system of medicine, which classified treatments by their "complexion" to balance the body's humors.</note>, and it possesses an attracting, thinning, and resolving...

<summary>This page describes the domestic and wild varieties of the Sebesten tree in Egypt, focusing on its botanical features and the process of creating birdlime from its fruit for treating tumors.</summary>

## <term>WILD SEBESTEN<note>original: "Sebesten Sylvestris." The Sebesten, or "Assyrian plum" (*Cordia myxa*), was highly valued in Renaissance pharmacy for its mucilaginous fruit, used to soothe coughs and sore throats. The "Sylvestris" designation refers to the wild-growing variety as opposed to the cultivated garden variety.</note>##

<header>ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT</header>

### On the Tamarisk, called Atle. Chapter IX.

->ALPINI<-

The wild genus of **Tamarisk**, which <term>Dioscorides<note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD) was a Greek physician and botanist whose work *De Materia Medica* was the primary authority on herbal medicine for 1,500 years.</note></term> calls *myrica* and *tamarix*, is very well known in Italy. It grows near riverbanks and in dry, rocky, or sandy places, and preferably near stagnant waters, as Dioscorides taught. We do not speak of this one here, since Egypt is clearly without it. 

However, another "domestic" <note>In this context, "domestic" refers to the variety commonly found near human settlements or native to the region, specifically *Tamarix aphylla*, known in Arabic as *Athl*.</note> genus of tamarisk is seen in Egypt, quite different from the wild one in height and shape, as it grows to the size of a large olive tree. I also hear that further up the Nile in a place called <term>Sahit<note>original: "sahit". This refers to the Sa'id, the traditional name for Upper Egypt.</note></term> by those people, it grows to the size of a large oak. From its wood they make charcoal, which is used throughout all of Egypt and Arabia. From that same wood they also fashion many vessels for the use of many sick people. 

It bears leaves very similar to the wild tamarisk, yet they are longer and more slender; many green filaments appear, such as are seen in some <term>horsetails<note>original: "equisetis". A primitive vascular plant of the genus *Equisetum*, known for its jointed stems and needle-like leaves.</note></term>, hanging from the branches and remaining always green. It bears a few rare, green fruits the size of nuts; these are hard, woody, and without a kernel. They seem to be of the same substance as <term>galls<note>original: "gallis". Plant galls (especially oak galls) were highly valued in Renaissance medicine for their astringent properties, used to "bind" or stop the flow of fluids.</note></term>. All of these fruits differ from one another in shape, although a rough and unequal texture is observed in all of them—some being long, some short, others round, wide, thick, or slender. 

The use of the Tamarisk, both in Egypt and even more so in Upper Egypt where there is an abundant supply of these trees, is primarily for fuel, since they have almost no other wood available for burning. The leaves of this tree are used to treat a hardened, obstructed, or "cooled" <term>spleen<note>original: "lien". In the medical theory of the time, the spleen was thought to be susceptible to "cold" humors which caused it to swell or harden.</note></term>. 

As previously mentioned, vessels are crafted from the wood, which those suffering from spleen ailments are accustomed to use. Because of the great utility this wood provides for that type of disease, we should not wonder if <term>Pliny<note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist. Alpini often cites him but maintains a critical, scientific distance from his more fantastical claims.</note></term> said (though it is a fable) that swine accustomed to eating from vessels made of tamarisk wood would be found to have no spleen at all. 

Women use a decoction prepared from the bark, taken orally, to stop menstrual purging; they also prepare <term>hip-baths<note>original: "insessiones". A medicinal bath where the patient sits in a liquid extract to absorb its properties.</note></term> from it and inject the decoction into the uterus with clysters. They say the fruit performs the same function; indeed, they use them for the spitting of blood, for <term>lientery<note>original: "lienteriis". A form of diarrhea where food is passed through the body undigested.</note></term>, for menses flowing more than is right, and for hemorrhoidal...

<summary>Prospero Alpini describes the Egyptian Tamarisk (Athl), noting its significant size compared to European varieties and its diverse uses ranging from charcoal production to treating spleen disorders and internal bleeding.</summary>
<keywords>Tamarisk, Athl, Prospero Alpini, Egyptian medicine, spleen treatments, herbal decoctions, Upper Egypt, botany</keywords>

They also strengthen loose and falling teeth with a <term>decoction<note>A medicinal liquid made by boiling plant material in water to extract its essence.</note></term> of them, and they soothe the sharp pain stirred up within them. They also use it for the beginning stages of bleary eyes in <term>eye-washes<note>original: "colirijs". Medicated preparations for the eyes.</note></term>, and for mouth ulcers. The ash of the bark and fruits also cures ulcers. Indeed, many prepare a decoction from the wood, no differently than our physicians are accustomed to make from <term>Indian wood<note>original: "ligno Indo". Refers to Guaiacum, or "Holy Wood," which was the standard European treatment for syphilis at the time.</note></term> to cure the <term>French disease<note>original: "luem gallicam". A historical term for syphilis.</note></term>; they administer this to lepers, those with <term>scabies<note>A contagious skin disease causing itching and small raised red spots.</note></term>, and many others afflicted with pustules and ulcers, with very happy success.

# BOOK 15

### On the Tamarind, called *derelfide*. Chapter 10.
## ->ALPINUS.<-

**TAMARIND**, which they call *derelfide*, is a tree the size of a plum tree, with dense branches and leaves like those of the myrtle, very similar to the *sesban* <term>sesban: *Sesbania sesban*, a leguminous tree common in Egypt and tropical Africa</term>. It bears white flowers very similar to the flowers of the orange tree <note>original: "mali aurancij," literally "orange apple"</note>, from the middle of which emerge four very thin white threads. From these, thick pods are produced—green at first, and finally an ash-gray color when ripe. Inside, these contain several hard, thick, and irregular seeds, along with a black pulp that is sour to the taste. In Egypt, these trees are not particularly abundant, nor are they native; they are brought there from **Arabia Felix** <note>Arabia Felix: "Happy Arabia," the fertile southern part of the Arabian Peninsula, modern-day Yemen</note> and Ethiopia, and are kept in gardens. It is considered a miracle that one tree is said to live in the desert of Saint Macarius <note>Saint Macarius: A 4th-century monk; his "desert" refers to the Wadi El Natrun region of Egypt</note>, near the Monastery of the Syrians, in a soil where no other plant can survive. This plant possesses a wondrous quality: its leaves always follow the sun and are called "sun-followers." For at sunset they all close inward, and at sunrise they open. This movement is observed to have such power that while they have pods, at sunset the leaves close very tightly around them as if to hold them, and at sunrise they open and release the pods. I have noticed that this turning of the leaves is common to several other Egyptian plants, namely the acacia, the *Abrus*, the *Absus*, the *Sesban*, and the Tamarind.

The leaves of the Tamarind are used for killing worms in children, and an infusion or decoction of them is also used to loosen the bowels. The leaves are acidic and not unpleasant to the taste. The Arabs preserve the pods (or "reeds") of this tree with sugar or carob honey <note>original: "melle carnub," a syrup made from carob pods</note> while they are small and green, and also when they have grown larger and reached maturity. They preserve the pulp with sugar, which travelers carry with them on journeys through the desert regions of Africa. By this remedy, those who are often overheated and inflamed by the heat and the journey, and are suffering from great thirst, are cooled and find relief. It extinguishes their thirst by purging and evacuating many "hot humors" <term>hot humors: In the medical theory of the time, bodily fluids like yellow bile that were thought to cause heat and fever when out of balance</term> through the bowels. Indeed, in pestilential fevers and all others arising from putrid, burning conditions, they drink water in which a great quantity of Tamarinds has been infused with sugar. This drink is most pleasant for those thirsty from the heat; it cools them greatly and quenches their thirst. Finally, for all putrid fevers stirred up by bilious and scorched humors, and for the heating and inflammation of the liver and kidneys—and even for gonorrhea—they frequently use Tamarinds, which they call *derelfide*.

<summary>Prospero Alpini describes the Tamarind tree's physical characteristics, its "miraculous" heliotropic leaf movements, and its vital role in Egyptian and Arabic medicine as a cooling agent and laxative.</summary>
<keywords>Tamarind, Egyptian botany, Arabic medicine, heliotropism, Saint Macarius, carob honey, medicinal syrups, hot humors, fever treatments</keywords>

# BOOK 15

### On the Tamarind, called *derelfide*. Chapter 10.
## ->ALPINUS.<-

**TAMARIND**, which they call *derelfide*, is a tree the size of a plum tree, with dense branches and leaves like those of the myrtle, very similar to the *sesban* <term>sesban: *Sesbania sesban*, a leguminous tree common in Egypt and tropical Africa</term>. It bears white flowers very similar to the flowers of the orange tree <note>original: "mali aurancij," literally "orange apple"</note>, from the middle of which emerge four very thin white threads. From these, thick pods are produced—green at first, and finally an ash-gray color when ripe. Inside, these contain several hard, thick, and irregular seeds, along with a black pulp that is sour to the taste. In Egypt, these trees are not particularly abundant, nor are they native; they are brought there from **Arabia Felix** <note>Arabia Felix: "Happy Arabia," the fertile southern part of the Arabian Peninsula, modern-day Yemen</note> and Ethiopia, and are kept in gardens. It is considered a miracle that one tree is said to live in the desert of Saint Macarius <note>Saint Macarius: A 4th-century monk; his "desert" refers to the Wadi El Natrun region of Egypt</note>, near the Monastery of the Syrians, in a soil where no other plant can survive. 

This plant possesses a wondrous quality: its leaves always follow the sun and are called "sun-followers" <note>original: "heliotropia," from the Greek for "sun-turning"</note>. For at sunset they all close inward, and at sunrise they open. This movement is observed to have such power that while the tree has pods, at sunset the leaves close very tightly around them as if to embrace or protect them, and at sunrise they open and release the pods. I have noticed that this turning of the leaves is common to several other Egyptian plants, namely the acacia, the *Abrus*, the *Absus*, the *Sesban*, and the Tamarind.

<summary>This page describes the botanical characteristics of the Tamarind tree, noting its non-native status in Egypt and its "miraculous" growth in the desert. It specifically highlights the phenomenon of nyctinasty, where the leaves close at night and open during the day.</summary>

HEADER.

## On the Agiahalid. Chapter XI.

### A L P I N V S.

<image-desc>An ornamental drop cap 'A' featuring floral and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>
A TREE is seen in the garden of a certain Turkish Eunuch, which is located a short distance from the house of the Venetian Consul; it was brought there from Ethiopia. It is the size of a wild pear tree, with sparse branches—not many in number—which extend upward unevenly. These branches are arranged with very sharp points and are covered in many thorns. It bears leaves very similar to those of the boxwood tree <note>Latin: *buxi*</note>, though they are wider, few, sparse, and grow either singly or in pairs with equal spaces between them. It has small, white flowers, similar to the flowers of the oriental Hyacinth but smaller, from which come black fruits resembling those of the dwarf elder <note>Latin: *ebuli*; *Sambucus ebulus*</note>, with a somewhat bitter and astringent taste. The leaves strike the palate with an acidic flavor and some astringency. I have heard that in Ethiopia, the use of this tree's leaves is very common for treating children troubled by worms. What do you think? Should we not consider this tree to be the <term>Lycium</term> <note>original: *licium*; a medicinal extract or the plant providing it, often associated with box-thorn</note>, since Dioscorides <note>A famous Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note> wrote the following about it: "Lycium, which some call <term>pyxacantha</term> <note>meaning 'box-thorn'</note>, is a thorny tree with branches three cubits long, or even longer, with dense leaves like the boxwood, and a black, smooth, bitter fruit like pepper; it has a thick, pale bark—not unlike soaked lycium—and many slanted, woody roots."

### G V I L A N D.

The Agiahalid, according to your description which I have seen, appears to be a tree rather than a shrub. However, we have learned from Dioscorides that <term>Lycium</term> is more of a shrub than a tree. Furthermore, that shrub is observed to have dense leaves, not sparse ones like those that exist on your Agiahalid.

### ALPINI.
