### On Uzeg; and whether it is Indian Lycium. Chapter XII.

->ALPINUS<-

Perhaps that tree called *agihalid* <note>likely the Desert Date, *Balanites aegyptiaca*</note> should not be rejected in place of Lycium. But now I will reveal the true Indian Lycium to you: a shrub which I found far from the city of Alexandria, above the banks of the branch of the Nile called *calig* <note>original: "calig"; referring to a canal or waterway</note>, ten miles away; the inhabitants call it **Uzeg**. It is a shrub with many very straight branches rising from numerous hard, woody roots that spread out at an angle. It grows to a height of three cubits <note>a cubit is roughly 18 inches, making the plant about 4.5 feet tall</note> and more, and on the branches are many long and very sharp thorns, some of which are seen to be covered with small leaves. The leaves of this shrub are similar to those of the olive, yet whiter and narrower, or less broad. It has flowers clearly similar to the *Agihalid*, being small and white, rivaling the form and size of the Oriental Hyacinth; from these, small black fruits are produced, entirely like the fruits of the dwarf elder, smooth to the touch, and bitter and astringent to the taste.

**GUILANDINUS.** Who would doubt that this tree should be judged as the Indian Lycium <term>Lycium: a medicinal extract used in antiquity for eye ailments and skin conditions, usually derived from species of Berberis or similar thorny shrubs</term>, provided we believe Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician and pharmacologist</note>, who certainly described it in these very words, saying:

<margin>» » » » »</margin>
> They say that Indian Lycium is made from a shrub called *Lonchitis* <note>original: "Lonchitide"</note>; it is classified among the thorn-bearing plants, having straight branches three cubits in length, or larger, thicker than a bramble from the root. The bark of the fruit turns red, and the leaf is like that of the olive. From the roots of this shrub, perhaps they collect that true juice we desire, and I think it is not lacking to them for their use.

**ALPINUS.** Although a great abundance of Lycium is traded there, I do not know if they actually prepare it from this shrub, since I have neither found this plant to grow abundantly in Egypt, nor do I know if it is familiar to the people there. Indeed, I judge the Lycium seen among them to be false and adulterated. For this juice, which is brought from parts of Arabia, is hard and black on the outside, very similar to the juice of the acacia. When broken open, the inside seems to show the color of aloes, with a faint, not unpleasant odor, and a slightly sweet, astringent taste, having no bitterness at all. It is viscous and sticks to the fingers when handled. Because of these things, I do not believe it to be true Lycium, especially since we observe that it neither has bitterness, nor does it produce a reddish foam when set on fire or ignited, as many authorities have handed down concerning the nature of true Lycium.

<summary>Alpinus describes a thorny shrub called Uzeg found near the Nile and debates with Guilandinus whether it is the true Indian Lycium described by Dioscorides, noting the differences between local specimens and the adulterated resins sold in markets.</summary>
<keywords>Uzeg, Indian Lycium, Alpinus, Guilandinus, Dioscorides, Nile, Agihalid, Lonchitis, medicinal plants, botany, Egypt, Arabia</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the botanist Prospero Alpini and Melchior Guilandinus. They are discussing the plant "Uzeg" (identified by Alpini as the source of the ancient medicinal extract Lycium) which Alpini encountered while traveling along the Nile.</meta>

# ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

**GUILANDINUS.** But why did you not strive to seek out the true juice from the Uzeg, and by that path hunt for a certain knowledge of the juice of Lycium?

**ALPINUS.** It happened that I saw this plant only when I was returning to my homeland; for when I was sailing from the city of Cairo <note>original: "Cayri"</note> to Alexandria via the Nile river, I inspected some shrubs near this city upon the banks of the same river (as has already been said) which were almost forming a hedge, with flowers and fruits already ripe. From these I plucked a branch and brought it with me to Alexandria. Having considered this shrub sufficiently, I recognized it to be Lycium itself. Nor, once I had recognized it, did time remain for me to test its juice expressed from the roots. Indeed, as soon as I arrived in Alexandria, I was forced to board the ship waiting for us for the journey, and so I could not test the juice which is circulated as Lycium. They <note>referring to ancient medical authorities like Galen or Dioscorides</note> establish its nature as a medium between hot and cold, but dry beyond the first degree. It is astringent, and therefore they use it as a wash for all ulcers, especially for ulcers of the mouth, ears, nose, anus, and intestines. Its greatest use is in eye-salves <term>collyria: medicinal preparations, often semi-liquid, applied directly to the eye</term> to ward off inflammations of the eye <term>ophthalmias: a general term for severe eye infections or inflammation</term>; for women have a prepared liniment made of Lycium and antimony <note>likely "kohl," a traditional eye cosmetic and medicine made from stibnite</note>, with which they smear their eyes daily to defend them from flows of humors <note>In Galenic medicine, "humors" were bodily fluids; an excess "flow" was thought to cause disease</note>. They also use it for a flux of the belly <note>diarrhea</note>, and no less for dysentery. They use it with the greatest hope for the spitting of blood, and no less for restraining uterine discharge by inserting it as a pessary. They also frequent its use for ulcers of the private parts, especially for decay, for corroded and ulcerated gums, and also for the loosening of teeth. In cases of lientery <note>a type of diarrhea where food is passed undigested</note>, dysentery, and hepatic flux <note>a discharge attributed to the liver</note>, they are accustomed to use it in enemas, and no less by drinking it dissolved in some liquid.

->There is no doubt that when applied, it powerfully<-
->defends any part from the flow of humors.<-
->From this it has been found to<-
->hold back the pains of<-
->gout. They<-
->call it Uzeg.<-

## UZEG, BELIEVED TO BE INDIAN LYCIUM

**GUILANDINUS.** But why did you not strive to seek out the true juice from the Uzeg, and by that path hunt for a certain knowledge of the juice of Lycium?

They <note>referring to ancient medical authorities like Galen or Dioscorides, who established the pharmacological properties of plants</note> establish its nature as a medium between hot and cold, but dry beyond the first degree. It is astringent, and therefore they use it as a wash for all ulcers, especially for ulcers of the mouth, ears, nose, anus, and intestines. <meta>This page continues the botanical dialogue between Prospero Alpini and Melchior Guilandinus. # ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

### On Egyptian Privet, called Elhanne, or Tamarhendi. <note>While *tamarhendi* usually refers to the tamarind tree in modern botanical terms, Alpini notes here that it was a name used locally for the henna plant.</note>
### Chapter XIII.

#### GUILANDINUS:

<image-desc>Decorative initial V with an illustration of a coastal or riverside town with buildings and trees, set within a square frame.</image-desc>
DID YOU see the Egyptian privet there, which the Egyptians call *elhanne* and *tamarhendi*? For I hear that there is a very great profit <note>original: "reditum"</note> from it there. ALPINUS: I inspected it very often; there, that tree is both most abundant and very well-known to everyone. Indeed, a great trade is conducted there from the leaves, for they use them for dyeing with a golden color. In truth, this tree appears similar to the privet, except for the flowers and fruits, in which some dissimilarity is seen between *elhanne* and the privet; for even if they scatter flowers in clusters <note>original: "racematim"</note> just like the privets, nevertheless they differ from them in color, shape, and scent. Indeed, the color of the *elhanne* flowers is not white, but ash-colored, and the flowers appear closest in shape to elderberry <term>sambuci: the elder or elderberry tree, known for its small, clustered white flowers</term> flowers, yet they are smaller than those. The scent of the *elhanne* is also quite sharp, while that of the privet is sweeter. They differ no less from one another in their fruits, since the fruits of the privet are berries similar to juniper, and the seeds of the *elhanne* are round, imitating coriander <term>coriandros: the coriander plant, whose round seeds were a common point of botanical comparison</term>. It also bears leaves less wide than the privet, narrower and whiter. The fruit, as I have already said, contains many small black seeds in a little pod <note>original: "folliculo"</note>, such as are seen in the seeds of grapes. GUILANDINUS: What? Do you doubt that this shrub is privet? Certainly, it cannot be denied that there is some difference between the privet and this plant. This could happen even from a diversity of soil; since it is well-known to everyone how much the same plants born in different places differ from one another in shape, and often even in their power <term>facultate: the medicinal or "virtuous" strength of a plant</term>. This is seen in rhubarb <term>rhabarbaro: the true medicinal rhubarb, highly valued in the Renaissance</term> and rhapontic <term>rhapontico: "False Rhubarb," a similar but less potent root often used as a substitute or adulterant</term>, whose roots appear the same to the sight and taste, yet it is found that they clearly differ in power. But concerning the *elhanne*, although in some way the shape of the shrub, its flowers, and its fruits differ from the privet, nevertheless it is known to be effective against the same diseases which Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical authority for over 1,500 years</note> and others have recorded that the privet helps. But why say more? Since Dioscorides most clearly wished this tree to be the privet, as is plainly evident from his own words, which are these:
<margin>„</margin> Privet is a tree which bears leaves around its branches
<margin>„</margin> very close to the olive, but wider, softer, and greener

### On Balsam, Called *Balassan* by the Egyptians.
### Chapter 14.
### GUILANDINI.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial N featuring a landscape scene with architecture and foliage.</image-desc>
Does the balsam plant still live in Egypt? ALPINI. Not at all, but rather in Arabia Felix <note>original: "Arabia foelici," or "Happy Arabia," a term used by the Romans to describe the fertile southern regions of the Arabian Peninsula, modern-day Yemen and Oman</note>, which both the Egyptians and the Arabs declare to be fertile with Balsam. GUILANDINI. Therefore, Dioscorides, Theophrastus, Pliny, Justin, and many others among the ancient writers—who recorded that Balsam once lived only in Egypt and Judea—were deceived. ALPINI. Without a doubt, I believe they were deceived not only regarding the native location of Balsam, but even concerning the tree itself, its juice, its fruits, and other matters; from which one might even suspect that they had never seen a living balsam tree anywhere. GUILANDINI. You speak of something quite remarkable, which physicians will not easily believe with certain faith; I ask, do you not think Dioscorides, Theophrastus, Pliny, Serapion, and other very grave writers should be trusted more regarding Balsam than yourself? They are preferred over others in teaching the science of herbs and plants, and are considered more worthy of trust. ALPINI. Although I believe I made the truth of Balsam plainly manifest in the dialogue I once wrote in Cairo <note>original: "Cayri"</note>, nevertheless, since you have now provoked me to narrate the true history of Balsam so that I may converse and comment upon it with you, I cannot now escape. Therefore, it will not trouble me to repeat the same things I once wrote, since the matter is of such great importance. And so as not to prolong the matter further, I say: it can easily be inferred that Arabia Felix was always the native home of Balsam, and that Egypt and Judea were never its native land. This is because all who have mentioned the Balsam plant recorded that it was kept only in gardens and was not at all born spontaneously in other uncultivated places, as Theophrastus, Pliny, Solinus, and Strabo have individually recorded. Pliny says: "But Balsam is preferred above all fragrances, granted to the single land of Judea, formerly in only two gardens, both royal; one no more than twenty acres <note>original: "iugerum," a Roman unit of land measurement</note>, the other even smaller." And Strabo, writing about the Balsam of the Valley of Jericho, said: "Where there is both a palace and a Balsam garden; the tree is aromatic and shrub-like, similar to the *cytisus* <term>cytisus: a genus of flowering shrubs in the pea family, often called brooms</term>..."

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>In this dialogue, Prospero Alpini challenges the classical consensus on the origins of Balsam, arguing it is native to the Arabian Peninsula rather than Judea or Egypt. He asserts that ancient authors only saw the plant in cultivated royal gardens, leading to their geographic misconceptions.</summary>
<keywords>Balsam, Balassan, Arabia Felix, Judea, Egypt, Prospero Alpini, Melchior Guilandini, Pliny the Elder, Strabo, botanical history, medicinal plants</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Prospero Alpini and Melchior Guilandini regarding the Balsam tree. Alpini is arguing that the plant is not native to Egypt or Judea, but was imported from Arabia, specifically citing the historical account of the Queen of Sheba and recent botanical failures in Egyptian gardens.</meta>

-> ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT <-

...and is very similar to the terebinth tree. No one doubts that foreign plants are kept with the greatest care only in pleasure gardens, and that native plants are observed to grow spontaneously not only in gardens or orchards, but in many other uncultivated places. But why do I strive to persuade you that Balsam plants were foreigners in Judea, when Josephus <note>Flavius Josephus, the 1st-century historian, in his work "Jewish Antiquities"</note> in Book 5 of *Jewish Antiquities* made the matter quite clear? He recorded there that the Queen of Sheba first brought a Balsam plant from Arabia as a gift to King Solomon, from which others were later propagated. 

The same must be said about the Balsam of Egypt; it is clearly established by the accounts of all Egyptians that this plant lived there in a garden near the place called Mataria <note>original: "el Mattharia," a site near Cairo famous for the "Virgin’s Tree" and a miraculous well, traditionally where the Holy Family rested</note>, kept perpetually with the greatest diligence. All ancient and more recent writers admit this, and it was never found growing in any other uncultivated place in Egypt. All Cairenes <note>original: "Cayrini," the inhabitants of Cairo</note> affirm that it perished there more than once and was brought back again from Arabia, grafted, and renewed. 

In the year 1575 from the birth of the Most Blessed Virgin, the Governor of Egypt—a eunuch named Mesih Pasha <note>original: "Messinor," likely referring to Mesih Mehmed Pasha, the Ottoman governor</note>—while visiting certain parts of Egypt, and especially Mataria (which place he was accustomed to visit and pray in out of honor and respect for the Blessed Virgin every Friday, the day which they [Muslims] sanctify, just as we Christians do Sunday), found himself in the garden. Seeing that all the Balsam plants had withered and died, he took care to have forty plants brought from Mecca. These were indeed grafted in the same garden, but within a few years they perished again through the negligence of the caretakers. 

Therefore, the Egyptians have always obtained Balsam from Arabia. This fact is not difficult to ascertain there, since many people often come to Cairo from Mecca by way of the Red Sea. The journey from Cairo to Mecca is indeed very easy and very frequent. Truly, it would not be useless for me now to subjoin the history of the pilgrims who visit those places every year for the sake of religion; for in this way you will be able to have more certain information about the place from which Balsam now comes, as well as where the Sultan <note>original: "Rex Turcarum," the King of the Turks</note> obtains so much balm of Gilead <term>opobalsami: the precious resin or sap extracted from the balsam tree, highly prized in medicine</term>.

**GUILANDINI.** It has greatly pleased me to hear from you that Arabia Felix <note>original: "Arabia foelicem," meaning "Happy Arabia," the fertile southern region of the Arabian peninsula</note> is now, and has always been, fertile with Balsam, according to the testimony of the Egyptians and Arabs. I consider this most true, even though Dioscorides, Theophrastus, Pliny, Solinus, Serapion, Sethus, and other writers have left it recorded that Balsam was once granted only to Egypt and Judea. In my judgment, full faith should perhaps not be entirely granted to these writers, since many others among the ancients [noted that] Arabia...

<summary>Alpini argues that Balsam is native to Arabia, not Egypt or Judea, citing Josephus's account of the Queen of Sheba and a 1575 attempt by the Governor of Egypt to replant specimens from Mecca. He explains that the plant only survives in Egypt through constant human intervention and re-importation.</summary>
<keywords>Balsam, Mataria, Queen of Sheba, King Solomon, Cairo, Mecca, Mesih Pasha, opobalsamum, Arabia Felix, botanical history</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues Prospero Alpini’s argument that the Balsam plant is native to Arabia, not Egypt or Judea. He cites several ancient authorities to support his claim before beginning a detailed description of the annual pilgrimage from Cairo to Mecca and Medina.</meta>

...that they left behind testimony that Arabia was also once fertile with Balsam.

<margin>»</margin>
For Diodorus Siculus <note>A Greek historian of the 1st century BCE</note>, in the second book of his histories, handed down to memory that Balsam grows in a certain valley of Arabia, and nowhere else. And Strabo <note>The Roman-era Greek geographer</note> in the 16th book of his *Geography* says: "Next to these is the most blessed land of the Sabeans <note>The Kingdom of Saba, or Sheba, in modern-day Yemen</note>, which is a very great nation; among them grow frankincense, myrrh, and cinnamon, and on the coast also Balsam, and another very fragrant little herb." Also Pausanias <note>The 2nd-century CE Greek traveler</note> in Book 9 wrote: "I have heard a different account concerning those vipers which live in Arabia among the Balsam trees."

But why should we believe Dioscorides, Theophrastus, and others regarding Balsam more than the Egyptians and Arabs born near the Balsam groves? Especially since I am uncertain whether those ancients ever encountered or inspected a living Balsam plant, whereas these people were born in the places where Balsam grows. Since it is most firmly established by the writings of all authors, both ancient and of this age, that Egypt nourished Balsam plants, how easily could these locals have seen and truly recognized those plants? Since it is clear that Egypt obtained them from Arabia, why should we doubt any longer that Arabia is the native soil of the Balsam? This matter requires no proof among the Egyptians and Arabs, since they all know for certain that not only in the past, but also in the present age, innumerable Balsam plants brought into Arabia are living, and all those which Egypt has had until now were exported from there and have very often been renewed in that manner.

Therefore, for my part, there will no longer be any doubt that Dioscorides and others who asserted that Balsam was once granted only to Egypt and Judea were greatly deceived; they recorded this in their writings based more on the reports of others than on true knowledge. But you, however, do not forget to recount now the history of the Egyptian pilgrims you mentioned, which will be most pleasing to me. **ALPIN.** In Arabia Felix <note>original: "Arabia foelici," the Latin name for the fertile southern part of the Arabian Peninsula</note> there is a primary city called Mecca, which is situated near the Red Sea. Distant from it by an eight-day journey is another called Medina, in which is the tomb of Muhammad, their false prophet <note>original: "pseudoprophetæ," reflecting the common 16th-century European religious bias</note>. Every year, twelve or fifteen thousand pilgrims travel there from Egypt to perform sacrifices and offer burnt offerings. A certain Commander is put in charge of these people, who, with a guard of three hundred soldiers, conducts all those pilgrims to Mecca and Medina, and soon brings them back safely to Cairo from the attacks of the Arabs, who not infrequently invade, kill, and plunder them in these deserts. For these Arabs live by seeking their sustenance through robbery; they are most fierce and daring, and they have very fast horses which they use, and it is even sanctioned by law...

<summary>Alpini argues that Arabia is the true native home of the Balsam tree, citing ancient geographers and local knowledge, then begins describing the dangerous annual pilgrimage from Cairo to Mecca.</summary>
<keywords>Balsam tree, Arabia Felix, Diodorus Siculus, Strabo, Pausanias, Mecca, Medina, Muhammad, Pilgrimage, Cairo, Sabeans</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues Prospero Alpini’s account of the Arabian origins of the Balsam plant, focusing on the political and religious geography of the Hejaz region during the annual pilgrimage (Hajj) to Mecca and Medina.</meta>

# ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

[They] live in such a way that they can never settle in one place, but are continually on horseback, living under tents. Therefore, the pilgrims, under the leader they call the <term>Hamirag</term> <note>original: *Hamirag*, a corruption of the Arabic *Amir al-Hajj*, or Commander of the Pilgrimage</note>, proceed directly to Mecca; then, on their return, they set out for the tomb at Medina, a journey which they complete in forty days. They stay at Medina and Mecca for another twenty days. The King of the Turks <note>The Ottoman Sultan</note> sends two governors to Medina, one of whom is in charge of sacred matters, and the other of secular and military affairs. But at Mecca, which does not entirely obey the Ottoman Empire, an Arabian prince presides, whom they call the <term>Seriph</term> <note>original: *Seriph*, the Sharif of Mecca</note>; they call by this name all who are descended from Muhammad and show written proof of their kinship to him. This prince has ten thousand armed cavalry and also twenty thousand infantry. Fearing that the Turk might lay a trap for him during this pilgrimage, as soon as he hears the pilgrims are approaching his lands, he withdraws from Mecca and retreats into the mountains. There he stays until the pilgrims have departed, and he threatens them daily that unless they leave after twenty days, he will cut off their water, since all the water flows down from the mountains.

The King of the Turks sends these gifts to this Sharif: namely, a golden cloak, and one hundred and fifty thousand gold coins to his sons and brothers every year. In exchange for these gifts, the Sharif himself sends the King of the Turks a gift of four hundred pieces of very fine Indian linen, of the sort that may be considered the <term>byssus</term> <note>A fine, costly fabric mentioned in the Bible, often translated as "fine linen"</note> mentioned in the Sacred Scriptures. Furthermore, along with that fine linen, he also sends the King three or four pounds of <term>opobalsamum</term> <note>The pure resin or "juice" of the balsam tree</note>, a pound to the Governor of Cairo, and half a pound to the *Hamirag* of the pilgrims. There are also two other leaders or *Hamirag* with many pilgrims—one from Damascus and the other from parts of Arabia Felix <note>Southern Arabia, modern-day Yemen</note>—who come to visit Mecca and Medina, to all of whom he gives a little balsam. After all the pilgrims of Egypt, Damascus, and Arabia leave Mecca, they travel to a certain mountain they call Arafat, at the foot of which is a place they call Maure <note>Likely referring to the hills of Marwa or the valley of Mina</note>, where they sacrifice in memory of the sacrifice made by Abraham, believing that to be the place of the sacrifice. In the middle of the return journey, there is a sandy mountain where they claim many balsam plants grow; they assert these were born on that barren mountain by a miracle of Muhammad.

Furthermore, so that you do not doubt from this account that the Sharif possesses so much balsam, know that the balsam production there is usually leased out by the Sharif. They pay, as I hear, several thousand gold coins each year; and those who hold the lease from him sell the balsam to whomever they wish. And this is the account of the pilgrims' jour-

<meta>This page continues Prospero Alpini’s dialogue with Guilandus. Having just discussed the political geography of the Hejaz and the Sharif of Mecca, Alpini now transitions to the specific botanical characteristics of the balsam plant and the production of its valuable resin, confirming that true balsam comes from Arabia rather than Egypt.</meta>

# ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

...stay, so that from this you may also know from where so much <term>balsam sap</term> <note>original: *opobalsami*, the resinous juice of the balsam tree</note> comes to the Turks, and also so that you may hold your own opinion on this matter: namely, that <term>Arabia Felix</term> <note>original: *Arabiam foelicem*, "Happy Arabia," referring to the fertile southern parts of the Arabian Peninsula</note> is the proper soil for balsam, while Egypt is foreign to it.

**GUILAND.** It was most pleasing to me to hear from you a diligent history of the journey of the Egyptian pilgrims, and also that the King of the Turks <note>The Ottoman Sultan</note> obtains balsam sap only from Arabia; but now you must describe the plant itself.

**ALPIN.** Balsam is a shrubby little tree, which grows to the height of <term>henna</term> <note>original: *elhanne*</note>, or privet, or broom; it bears very few leaves, similar to those of rue, yet not, as <term>Dioscorides</term> <note>A famous 1st-century Greek physician and botanist</note> used to say, whiter than those, but rather they appear similar to the leaves of the <term>mastic tree</term> <note>original: *lentisci*</note>, specifically green in color, somewhat whitish, and evergreen. Its wood is gummy, and the exterior appears a light reddish color; its branches are conspicuous with the same color—long, straight, and slender, sparsely and irregularly filled with leaves. These leaves grow together in groups of three, five, or seven on a stalk, in some way imitating the leaves of the mastic tree. 

The branches are fragrant, gummy, and when touched with the fingers, they stick to them tenaciously. It bears small white flowers very similar to acacia flowers, hanging in threes from each stalk, almost in the shape of an <term>umbel</term> <note>an umbrella-like flower cluster</note>; they are extremely fragrant, though the scent dissipates in a short time. From these come yellow seeds contained in blackish-reddish pods, which are very fragrant. Inside, they contain a yellow moisture very much like honey, with a somewhat bitter and slightly sharp taste that strikes the tongue, smelling of balsam sap. These seeds imitate the shape and size of the fruits of the <term>terebinth</term> <note>the turpentine tree</note>, being pointed at the ends and thick in the middle. 

The balsam sap distills in summer from the scarred bark of the trunk; immediately when drawn out, it is white in color, and afterwards it turns green, thirdly to gold, and finally to the color of honey. When first distilled it is clear, but it soon becomes turbid and thickens; then a third time it becomes very thin and very clear, and finally, when aged, it thickens like turpentine. When recently distilled, it smells with a very sharp and extraordinary odor, which often causes a headache for many, and in some even provokes a nosebleed. Indeed, that sharpness and intensity of odor, such as occurs in <term>civet</term> <note>original: *Zebet*, a musk-like scent from the civet cat</note>, is blunted over time and becomes more pleasant; but when aged, hardly any odor is felt in it, and for that reason Dioscorides said that old balsam was useless. It is also very light at the beginning and very easy to dilute, so much so that if it is dropped from a height into water or milk, it will not settle at the bottom of the vessel. But once it has turned clear and golden and is thoroughly purged of all dregs, it possesses a moderate lightness, so that when dropped into water, even if it seeks the bottom...

<summary>Alpini provides a detailed botanical description of the balsam tree, its leaves, flowers, and seeds, while explaining how the precious resin (opobalsamum) changes in color, consistency, and scent as it ages.</summary>
<keywords>balsam, opobalsamum, Arabia Felix, botany, plant morphology, resin harvesting, Dioscorides, henna, mastic tree, civet</keywords>

<meta>catchword: ipsa</meta>

...sinks, yet it is soon carried back again to the surface of the water, and is quickly dissolved with it. The older it is, the more obscure <note>meaning darker or less clear</note> it becomes. And for this reason, <term>Dioscorides</term> <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and author of the foundational herbal *De Materia Medica*</note> rightly said that the best <term>balsam sap</term> <note>original: *Balsami succum*, referring to the liquid resin or opobalsamum</note> is fresh, of a strong scent, pure, not turning toward sourness, easily diluted, smooth, astringent, and moderately biting to the taste. 

**GUILAND.** A doubt has arisen concerning its color, since you said that <term>sap-balsam</term> <note>original: *opobalsamum*</note> is white in color immediately after being drawn out, and that this color changes in a short time into an oily green, and soon into golden, and finally into the color of honey; yet Dioscorides—whom you would hardly deny has inspected true sap-balsam—said that it has a milky color. 

**ALPIN.** Dioscorides left it written that balsam sap, when dripped and dissolved in water, takes on the color of milk. This is most true; indeed, this is an inseparable and unique sign, which more faithfully reveals the true sap of the balsam compared to the other adulterated substances accepted as sap-balsam by many. Many things could be said by us regarding the signs of pure and adulterated sap-balsam, and no less concerning <term>fruit-balsam</term> <note>original: *carpobalsamo*, the dried berries of the tree</note> and <term>wood-balsam</term> <note>original: *xilobalsamo*, the twigs and branches</note>, about which almost all more recent physicians are in doubt. But because we have elsewhere written most extensively about all these in our *Dialogue on Balsam*, and since I know for certain that you have long since read and considered all those things, I do not wish to consume more time on them, but rather to turn our conversation to other matters. Therefore, those who desire to hear and recognize a complete and perfect account of the plant, its sap, its fruits, and its branches, should not be reluctant to read that dialogue, to which I also refer you for the remaining things that could be said about balsam. 

The most illustrious Senator <term>Giacomo Contarini</term> <note>original: *Iacobus Contarenus*, a Venetian patron of the arts and sciences</note>, the noble Englishman <term>John Wroth</term> <note>original: *Ioannes Vothus*</note>, the Frenchman <term>François Ferrier</term>, the German <term>Joachim Jungermann</term>, the Venetian pharmacist <term>Marco Fenari</term>, and many others have seen the very true balsam plant living at my house, and they even saw the true sap-balsam drip from the axils of its leaves. This was a great marvel to many, since it does not appear anywhere else that Italy has ever possessed this plant. Nevertheless, I will now endeavor to reveal and report to you the uses in medicine which sap-balsam, fruit-balsam, and wood-balsam have among the Egyptians, taking our starting point from the virtues of sap-balsam.

**GUILAND.** It must be thought that <term>Galen</term> <note>A 2nd-century Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for 1,500 years</note> knew few uses of Balsam in medicine from this fact: that in his sixth book *On the Properties of Simple Medicines*, while copiously teaching the uses of all simple medicines, he brought forth nothing on balsam regarding this matter, as is evident from these words of his: 
<margin>”</margin> "Balsam dries <note>original: *Balsamum desiccat*</note> 
<margin>”</margin> and heats in the second degree <note>referring to the system of four humors and their "degrees" of intensity</note>. It is, moreover, of such fine parts 
<margin>”</margin> that it is fragrant. But its liquid is of even finer parts than..."

# BOOK I.

<meta>catchword: supposito</meta>

...the plant itself; however, it is not as hot as some believe, having been deceived by the fineness of its parts. Furthermore, its fruit is very similar in its nature and power, but it is far inferior in the fineness of its parts. But come now, tell us of its common uses among the Egyptians. 

**ALPIN.** There is certainly no medicine there that those people use more often or more frequently for healing than **balsam-sap** <note>original: *opobalsamum*</note>, which they apply to almost all diseases and symptoms—especially those that are stirred up by a simple cold and moist temperament, by cold and moist humors, or by poisons. They use it with the highest praise for all wounds, which are cured by it in a miraculous way. I learned this through experience with **Pietro Mariano**, a Venetian merchant; one night his head was terribly wounded by the Turks with wooden clubs, even down to the bone. When those wounds were anointed with balsam for only a day, they almost entirely knit back together, and within a very few days—as he himself can testify—he was perfectly healed. They also use it for foul ulcers, which are cleansed and healed by it. 

In cases of poisoning, or bites from serpents, or stings from scorpions, it is of the greatest help both when drunk and when applied to the skin. In times of **plague**, so that they may be defended from its contagion, they have nothing greater, more useful, or safer to use than swallowing half a **drachm** <note>A unit of weight roughly equal to 3.5 grams</note> of it every day. They also cure many putrid fevers by drinking it, which causes a copious sweat to break out. In chronic fevers caused by raw and cold humors and obstructions of the internal organs, they find nothing more useful or excellent than administering two **scruples** <note>A small unit of weight, about 1.3 grams</note> or a drachm of balsam-sap by mouth daily; for it powerfully frees the organs from obstructions and, by fostering natural heat, it ripens and digests the raw humors. 

Hence, its use for all "cold" diseases and symptoms is most frequent among them. Dropped into the eyes, it restores lost vision, and in the ears, it restores hearing and heals earaches. Furthermore, it is used for those suffering from convulsions caused by moisture and cold, for those with vertigo, epilepsy, paralysis <note>original: *resolutos*, referring to a loss of function or "resolution" of the nerves</note>, and tremors; for a cough caused by cold humors, **orthopnea** <note>a condition where breathing is only possible while sitting or standing upright</note>, and all difficulty in breathing; for fluxes of the lungs, moisture, and pains; for coldness of the stomach, indigestion, and all troubles of gas; for the cold and moist imbalance of the uterus, for suffocations, pains, sterility, and **white discharges** <note>original: *albos fluores*, likely referring to leucorrhea</note>; for the suppression of urine due to cold, gas, or stones; and for powerfully breaking up and clearing away stones in the kidneys—the use of balsam-sap is most well-known among all those people, and they find it a most excellent remedy for all these ills. Indeed, Egyptian women who are sterile, by both drinking it and...

<summary>Alpini describes balsam-sap (opobalsamum) as a near-universal remedy in Egyptian medicine, detailing its use in treating wounds, the plague, fevers, and various internal obstructions. He emphasizes its effectiveness through a personal anecdote involving a wounded Venetian merchant.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Prospero Alpini and Melchior Wieland (referred to as Guilandinus). In the previous section, Alpini began detailing the medicinal uses of balsam-sap (opobalsamum) in Egypt, specifically its effectiveness for wounds, plague, and fevers. Here, the discussion shifts to its use for fertility, its intensive application in 16th-century "anti-aging" skincare routines, and the medicinal properties of the plant's seeds and twigs.</meta>

<header>ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT</header>

...applied as a suppository, or as a fumigant, they often use it with great success to remove **sterility**. They use it no less for creating a beautiful face, as the skin is protected from wrinkles when anointed with it. I also hear that some women have used it quite effectively to preserve the beauty and youth of their faces.

**GUILAND.** But how do they use it to preserve youth? For although this use is on many people's lips, I have nonetheless been unable to learn the method from anyone.

**ALPIN.** So that women can permanently preserve the skin of their face while they are young, they follow the use of **balsam-sap** <note>original: *opobalsami*</note> in this manner: the woman to be "balsamed" (if I may put it that way) first stays in a hot bath until she is very warm; at which time she anoints her skin many times and copiously with balsam-sap. After this anointing, she remains for an hour or more in the very hot bath until the skin has properly soaked up the balsam-sap and it has dried; she then leaves the bath and performs her usual duties. She remains thus, with her face and breast anointed with balsam-sap, for at least three days, during which she never washes or cleans the anointed skin. 

On the third day after the anointing, she goes to the bath again in the same way already described, and anoints her face and breast, now thoroughly warmed, many times with plenty of balsam-sap. There are some women who anoint the skin even ten times in one day, observing a careful interval so that the anointed skin may properly soak up all the balsam-sap and dry a little, so that it can more readily draw the juice deeper within. 

They carry out this anointing of balsam-sap in this way many times—for at least thirty days. During this time they never wash or clean the skin; for fifteen days or even more, they rest with the skin thus anointed with balsam-sap until it shows no liquid and has been thoroughly dried by it. At that time, they wash themselves with **distilled bean water** <note>original: *aqua fabarum stillatitia*; historically used as a cosmetic wash to clear the complexion</note>, having first applied a small amount of **bitter almond oil** <note>original: *olei amigdalarum amararum*</note> to those parts, and they wipe the skin for many days. And let these things be said regarding the powers of balsam-sap.

The use of the **seeds** and **fresh twigs** is also common among them. Indeed, they use them for almost everything they use balsam-sap for, and especially for bringing strength to the **stomach**; for they administer its powder with a decoction of **spikenard** <note>original: *spicae nardi*; an aromatic plant used in ancient perfumes and medicines</note>, in doses of up to two **drachms** <note>A unit of weight roughly equal to 3.5 grams</note>. The same decoction serves to cherish, increase, and strengthen the natural color of the skin. They also use it for difficulty breathing, a **cold cough** <note>In the medical theory of the time, illnesses were categorized as "hot" or "cold"; a cold cough was often one involving excess phlegm</note>, and other diseases arising from coldness. The powder and decoction are most frequently used to dry out the moisture of the **womb** and to revive its heat. They frequently use a fumigant made from these for all diseases of the womb caused by...

<meta>catchword: coldness (frigida)</meta>

B O O K. 25
Concerning the Calaf or Ban. Chapter 15.

### ALPINUS.

A small shrub-like tree is found in Egypt which, in my own opinion, is a species of Willow. It grows in damp places, with leaves somewhat similar to the Willow, but much larger—indeed, both longer and wider. Its flowers are certain oblong, white, and downy globules, growing almost at the base of the leaf stems; the tree produces as many flowers as it has leaves. This small tree grows to the size of a <term>Chaste tree<note>Latin: *vitex*. A Mediterranean shrub often used as a reference for size and habit in historical botanical texts.</note></term> with many branches or straight shoots. From these flowers, I am told, they distill a water called *macahalef* <note>original: *macahalef*; likely a transcription of the Arabic *ma' al-khalaf*, meaning "willow water."</note>, which they claim possesses remarkable powers for drying up <term>putrefaction<note>In the medical context of the 16th century, this referred to the "rotting" of bodily humors, which was thought to cause infection and fever.</note></term>, as well as for resisting poisons and reviving the heart. In pestilential fevers, this water is held by them as a secret remedy. They also provide the water in which many flowers have been infused for some time, or in which they have been boiled, for headaches and for the restoration of strength; they have experienced that this water also strongly resists decay and poisons. The decoction and infusion of these flowers prepared in water is in the greatest use among them for anyone suffering from a fever.

<meta>This page continues the botanical and medicinal discussion of plants found in Egypt and Arabia, specifically focusing on the coffee plant, referred to here as Bon or Ban.</meta>

->BOOK 26 / On Coffee. Chapter XVI.<-

### ALPINI

I saw a tree in the garden of Halybey the Turk, an image of which you will now see, from which those seeds—very common there and called <term>Bon</term> <note>an early name for the coffee bean, derived from the Arabic 'bunn'</note> or <term>Ban</term>—are produced. From these, both Egyptians and Arabs prepare a very popular decoction <note>original: "decoctum"</note> which they drink instead of wine; it is sold in public taverns just as wine is among us. They call it <term>Caoua</term> <note>coffee, from the Arabic 'qahwa'</note>. These seeds are imported from <term>Arabia Felix</term> <note>Southern Arabia, literally "Happy Arabia"</note>. The tree I mentioned observing was seen to be similar to the spindle-tree <note>original: "euonomo"</note>, yet it had thicker, harder, and greener leaves that remain green forever. 

The use of these seeds is very well known to everyone for preparing the aforementioned decoction. I have described how it is prepared by them elsewhere. However, they use this decoction to strengthen a "colder" stomach <note>In the medical theory of the time, a "cold" stomach was thought to cause poor digestion and sluggishness</note> and to aid digestion. It is used no less to clear obstructions from the internal organs. In cases of cold swellings of the liver and spleen and long-standing obstructions, they find success by using this decoction for many days. 

It also seems particularly beneficial for the uterus, for it warms it and removes obstructions from it. Indeed, it is in common use among all Egyptian and Arabian women so that they may assist their menstruation <note>original: "menses"</note> by slowly sipping a great deal of this hot decoction while they are flowing. To promote them when they are suppressed, the use of this decoction for many days after the body has been purged is most useful. 

**GUIL.** <note>Melchior Guilandini, a botanist and contemporary of Alpini</note> Avicenna <note>The influential Persian physician Ibn Sina (980–1037)</note> mentions these seeds and recorded uses for them similar or identical to those you have related. He classified the seed itself as hot in the third degree and dry in the second degree <note>This refers to the Galenic system of grading the potency of medicines from one to four</note>. However, this does not seem to be true, since the seed has a sweetish taste with a little bitterness and displays no sharp pungency. Nonetheless, he taught that it contributes much toward clearing obstructions of the organs and cold swellings of the liver or spleen. But he says it may stir up nausea in the stomach and purge phlegm <note>original: "pituitam"</note>. I have learned through experience among the Egyptians that these seeds provide many other benefits. And this is the tree that I once examined in Cairo <note>original: "Cayri"</note>.

<sig>G 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: BON</meta>

<summary>This page provides one of the earliest European descriptions of the coffee plant and its beverage, detailing its physical characteristics, its social use in taverns as a wine substitute, and its medicinal applications for digestion and women's health.</summary>

# On the Baobab. Chapter XVII.

->ALPINUS<-

**B**AHOBAB is a fruit the size of a citron <note>original: "mali citri"; the citron is a large, thick-rinded citrus fruit</note>, similar to a gourd <note>original: "cucurbitae"</note>. Inside are black, hard seeds, with their ends curving toward one another almost into a semi-arch; they possess a substance similar to that of gourds, which in fresh specimens is moist, red, and acidic in taste

<summary>This page introduces the Baobab tree, providing a botanical description of its fruit and seeds, comparing them to citrons and gourds.</summary>
<keywords>Baobab, botany, Egypt, fruit, seeds, Alpinus, citron, gourd</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Baobab fruit from Chapter XVII. The previous page ended describing the seeds and pulp; this page details the taste, medicinal applications in Ethiopia and Cairo, and the appearance of the tree itself.</meta>

# BOOK I.

...not unpleasant; the fruit, recently cut from the tree, is very pleasing to the taste. In the burning regions of Ethiopia, with its acidic flavor—which the wealthy temper with sugar <note>original: "saccharo"</note>—it powerfully extinguishes thirst and cools the body significantly. There, as I hear, they use it for all heat-related ailments and all putrid fevers, especially for those of the plague, both by eating its pulp with sugar and drinking the pressed juice with sugar, or by taking a syrup prepared from it.

In Cairo <note>original: "Cayri"</note> as well, where the fresh fruit is not available, they use its pulp prepared as a powder, which is a red earth, striking the palate with an astringent and acidic taste such as is observed in Lemnian earth <note>Terra Lemnia was a medicinal clay from the island of Lemnos, used since antiquity as an astringent and antidote</note>. Among many people, the use of that earth is very common for pestilential fevers, as well as for coughing up blood, lientery <term>lientery: a form of diarrhea where food passes through the body undigested</term>, dysentery, a bloody flux from the liver, and also for regulating menstrual flow. Some administer a drachm <note>original: "dragmam"; a unit of apothecary weight</note> of this earth, reduced to a very fine powder and dissolved in plantain water <note>original: "aqua plantaginis"</note>, for these same purposes; others use it as a decoction, and others as an infusion. I myself saw the tree that bears these fruits in a certain garden; its leaves are very similar to those of orange trees <note>original: "aurancijs"</note> in size and shape.

<image-desc>A faint botanical woodcut illustration of a Baobab tree branch with palmate leaves and a large, hanging oval fruit occupies the lower background of the page. In the center, below the text, is a decorative typographical ornament consisting of mirrored scrollwork motifs.</image-desc>

->BAOBAB<-

ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

## *On the Cotton Tree, called Gotne Msegiar.* <note>original: "Gotne Msegiar"; likely from the Arabic *qutn mushajjar*, meaning "tree cotton."</note>
### Chapter XVIII.

->GUILANDINO.<-

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'M' showing a landscape with buildings, trees, and a mountain in the background.</image-desc> Do the Egyptians gather a great abundance of cotton there? For Syria and Cyprus produce a very great amount of it. 

->ALPINI.<- 

The Egyptians import cotton from elsewhere for their own use, for that herbaceous plant <term>herbaceous plant: a soft-stemmed plant that grows and dies back annually, like common field cotton</term> from which the Syrians and Cypriots gather cotton does not grow among them. Instead, they have only a woody shrub <note>This refers to *Gossypium arboreum*, a perennial cotton species.</note> which grows only in certain gardens. It lives for many years and differs from the herbaceous type only in its height and the shape of its trunk, branches, and leaves. It grows to a height of ten cubits <note>A cubit is roughly 18 inches, making the tree about 15 feet tall.</note>, with a hard, woody stem and branches. 

->GUILANDINO.<- 

What do I hear? That a cotton tree is seen there? This is certainly a new plant to all herbalists, never before seen or known. But tell me, I pray, do many of those cotton plants grow there, from which they might fulfill the use of that most common downy fiber? 

->ALPINI.<- 

Only a few of those cotton trees are seen, and only in gardens. They bear the same down as the herbaceous type, but because of the small quantity available to them, it is not in common use; rather, cotton is imported from elsewhere. The Egyptians use it in place of linen bandages—which our people use exclusively—to heal all ulcers and wounds. Indeed, they do not use linen bandages for wounds and ulcers as we do. They also apply it, as we do, to stop the flow of blood. There is also a very frequent use of the seeds' mucilage <note>The jelly-like coating of the seeds.</note> for all burning fevers and for poisons that cause erosion in the stomach and intestines. For those suffering from coughs caused by the dripping of sharp or salty fluids <note>In the medicine of the time, coughs were often attributed to "catarrh" or fluids dripping from the head into the chest.</note>, cotton is of great use to them. In Arabia, they prepare those very thin cloths from this cotton down (which they call *sessa* <note>original: "sessa"; likely referring to *shash*, a fine muslin cloth</note>, and many are convinced it is the byssus <term>byssus: an ancient term for exceptionally fine, precious fabric, often debated by Renaissance scholars as being either fine linen or cotton</term> of the ancients), which are certainly praised by everyone for their beauty.

<meta>catchword: COTTON</meta>

<summary>In this dialogue, Alpini describes a rare, perennial 'Cotton Tree' found in Egyptian gardens, contrasting it with the common annual cotton of Syria. He details its medicinal uses for wounds and fevers, and its role in producing fine Arabian fabrics.</summary>
<keywords>cotton, tree cotton, Gossypium arboreum, medicinal plants, Egyptian medicine, textiles, byssus, Melchior Guilandino</keywords>

<summary>In this dialogue, Alpini describes the rare tree cotton (Gossypium arboreum) found in Egyptian gardens, distinguishing it from the common herbaceous cotton of Syria and Cyprus. This was a significant botanical observation at the time, as the perennial tree-like variety was unfamiliar to European herbalists.</summary>

# ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

### Concerning the Arabian <term>Sambac</term><note>The Arabian Jasmine (*Jasminum sambac*), known for its intense fragrance.</note>, or Arabian Jasmine. Chapter XIX.

#### ALPINUS.

<image-desc>Large decorative drop cap 'C' with floral and vine patterns.</image-desc>IN CAIRO, there is a climbing shrub which raises itself quite high by creeping with its shoots. Its leaves are very similar to those of the <term>bitter orange tree</term><note>original: "mali aurancij"</note>, and it produces white flowers endowed with eight petals. These are entirely similar to common jasmine flowers, though they differ by having more petals, a larger size, and a much more powerful and sweeter scent. The use of these flowers is intended more for the decoration of the body than for health. Nevertheless, they prepare an oil from them which women use in baths to warm and relax the womb. They have found from experience that it is especially beneficial for <term>scirrhous tumors</term><note>A historical term for hard, chronic swellings or masses.</note>, and they use it to make childbirth easier. They both drink this oil warm and smear it upon the external parts of the womb. They also use it externally by anointing the region of the chest, while also drinking it warm by mouth, to treat a cough, difficulty breathing, and even for lethal <term>pleurisy</term><note>An inflammation of the lining of the lungs.</note> in which sputum is brought up with the greatest difficulty. It is further used for <term>pneumonia</term><note>original: "peripneumoniam"</note> and for severe pains of the stomach, intestines, and womb. Our <term>perfumers</term><note>original: "vnguentarij"; these were specialists who prepared scented oils, ointments, and medicinal salves.</note> prepare this from sweet almonds or even from sesame seeds; after these seeds have been infused with the scent of the jasmine flowers, they express a highly fragrant oil from them.

## ARABIAN JASMINE (SAMBAC).

<image-desc>A detailed botanical woodcut of a flowering shrub, likely Arabian Jasmine (Jasminum sambac). The plant is shown growing from a small patch of earth with a thick, textured, and slightly twisted woody stem. The branches are dense with ovate, pointed leaves. <vocab>Sambac, Jasmine (Lesmin), Arabian</vocab>

# ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

### Concerning the Arabian <term>Sambac</term><note>The Arabian Jasmine (*Jasminum sambac*), known for its intense fragrance and use in teas and perfumes.</note>, or Arabian Jasmine. Chapter XIX.

#### ALPINUS.

<image-desc>Large decorative drop cap 'C' with floral and vine patterns.</image-desc>IN CAIRO, there is a climbing shrub which raises itself quite high by creeping with its shoots. Its leaves are very similar to those of the <term>bitter orange tree</term><note>original: "mali aurancij"; specifically the Seville or sour orange tree.</note>, and it produces white flowers endowed with eight petals. These are entirely similar to common jasmine flowers, though they differ by having more petals, a larger size, and a much more powerful and sweeter scent. The use of these flowers is intended more for the decoration of the body than for health. Nevertheless, they prepare an oil from them which women use in baths to warm and relax the womb. They have found from experience that it is especially beneficial for <term>scirrhous tumors</term><note>A historical term for hard, chronic swellings or masses, often thought to be precursors to cancer or deep obstructions.</note>, and they use it to make childbirth easier. They both drink this oil warm and smear it upon the external parts of the womb. They also use it externally by anointing the region of the chest, while also drinking it warm by mouth, to treat a cough, difficulty breathing, and even for lethal <term>pleurisy</term><note>An inflammation of the lining of the lungs that causes sharp chest pain.</note> in which sputum is brought up with the greatest difficulty. It is further used for <term>pneumonia</term><note>original: "peripneumoniam"</note> and for severe pains of the stomach, intestines, and womb. Our <term>perfumers</term><note>original: "vnguentarij"; these were specialists who prepared scented oils, ointments, and medicines, often acting as both pharmacists and cosmeticians.</note> call this plant the Jasmine, though this Arabian variety is far more potent than those known in Europe.

### Concerning the black bean called <term>Lablab</term><note>Commonly known today as the Hyacinth bean (*Lablab purpureus*).</note>. Chapter XX.

->ALPINUS.<-

THIS IS a woody climbing plant which grows to the size of a grapevine; its shoots extend and spread out in the manner of a vine, and its leaves are entirely similar in shape to the common <term>bean</term><note>original: "phaseolo"; referring to the common kidney bean or similar legumes.</note>. It bears flowers twice a year, in spring and in autumn, which are somewhat similar to the flowers of common beans. From these flowers, one sees long pods, very broad like those of broad beans, which contain black seeds clearly similar to beans, some of which appear black and others reddish. This "tree" <note>Alpini uses the word *arbor* (tree), though he describes a woody vine.</note> lives for a hundred years or more and is evergreen. The Egyptians frequently eat these beans, and they are no less sweet to our own taste. Women use a decoction 
->with saffron to stimulate menstruation.<-
->Furthermore, it is useful for a cough,<-
->and for shortness of breath, and for<-
->promoting the flow of suppressed<-
->urine.<-

<meta>This page continues Prosper Alpinus's 16th-century botanical survey of Egypt, transitioning from his discussion of the Tamarind to another leguminous plant, the Abrus.</meta>

<header>ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT</header>

### On the Red Bean, called Abrus. Chapter 21.

->A L P I N V S .<-

THERE is also a shrubby tree, twiggy and twining, which creeps in the manner of the *lahlab* <note>The Arabic term *lablab* refers to climbing vines or the hyacinth bean</note>, raising itself quite high and spreading out wide. Its leaves are clearly similar to those of the *derelside* <note>Likely a local name for a species of Sesbania or Cassia</note> or the Tamarind, but much smaller. They even move toward the Sun, just as was described regarding the tamarind leaves; indeed, when the Sun sets, they close and remain completely shut until the Sun rises again, at which time they open once more <note>This describes nyctinasty, the "sleep" movements of plants</note>. I hear that this tree bears flowers very similar to those of kidney beans, as well as pods which contain seeds that are small, round, red, and hard, entirely like beans. Indeed, to everyone they look exactly like the coral beads <note>original: *corallorum uniones*</note> that young girls are accustomed to wearing around their necks. They have the taste of beans, and they are used for food; the Egyptians are accustomed to eating them boiled in broth. However, they are more difficult to digest than all others, for which reason they are very troublesome to the stomach, cause much flatulence, and are observed to be very harmful to those suffering from "hypochondria" <note>In early modern medicine, "hypochondriasis" referred to physical ailments of the upper abdomen, often linked to melancholy and digestive distress</note>.

->stomach with the greatest<-
->difficulty and even<-

ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

### *On the Mauz, or Banana. Chapter 22.*

### ALPINUS.

The plant called **Mauz** <note>original: *Mauz*; the Arabic name for banana, from which the scientific name *Musa* is derived</note> by the Egyptians grows in many places in Egypt, and especially in Damietta—once called Pelusium—reaching the size of a pomegranate tree, but it lacks branches. Instead, it spreads its leaves like a reed; these are so long and wide that in some cases they have been seen to be six or seven cubits <term>cubit: an ancient unit of length based on the forearm, roughly 18 to 20 inches</term> long, and nearly two cubits wide. Many say that this plant was originally grafted from sugarcane onto the root of the **colocasia** <term>colocasia: also known as taro; a root vegetable with large "elephant ear" leaves</term>, and truly an image of both is seen in it: it has attained the length of the canes and the width of the colocasia in its leaves. It remains green forever, and its leaves never fall. 

This plant bears many fruits, similar to small cucumbers but hanging in many clusters. Their color on the outside appears golden, and the peels breathe out a very sweet and aromatic scent. The substance of the fruits is somewhat similar to cucumbers, yet it is soft, fatty, sticky, sweet, and especially pleasant to those accustomed to eating it; it lacks any seeds or pits. It weighs heavily on the stomach, is difficult to digest, provides much nourishment, and generates a thick and sticky humor <term>humor: in pre-modern medicine, one of the bodily fluids thought to govern health and temperament</term>, which can obstruct the internal organs, especially the liver. However, it is a help to those suffering from coughs and shortness of breath caused by heat. They use a decoction <term>decoction: a medicinal liquor made by boiling plant material in water</term> of it effectively for coughs caused by the dripping of hot humors, for inflammation of the pleura <note>the membrane surrounding the lungs</note> and the lungs, for inflamed kidneys, and for those who have difficulty urinating. They also say that the fruit, whether eaten or taken as a decoction, increases sexual desire. They claim the dried peels of the fruits have great power to strengthen all the internal organs, and a decoction prepared from them in warm water aids the heart. The use of this decoction is said to be very useful among the Egyptians for pestilential fevers; they also drink the powder of these same peels dissolved in warm water for ailments of the heart.

**GUILANDINO.** This plant was known to Theophrastus <note>a Greek philosopher and the "father of botany"</note> under the name of a palm, and also to Serapion <note>a 9th-century Arabic physician whose works were standard medical texts in Europe</note>, who said nearly the same things about its medicinal benefits as you have recounted. However, I have known no one who confirmed what you have now brought forth from the reports of the Egyptians: namely, that this plant was grafted from the sugar-bearing cane onto the root of the colocasia. Nevertheless, in the length and width of the leaves, they do seem to reflect the appearance of the foliage of both plants. Let these remarks suffice.

<summary>This page describes the banana plant (Mauz), detailing its physical appearance, a local Egyptian legend about its origin as a graft of sugarcane and taro, and its various medicinal uses for respiratory and heart issues.</summary>
<keywords>banana, Mauz, Damietta, sugarcane, colocasia, taro, Galenic medicine, digestion, aphrodisiac, Egyptian botany, Prospero Alpini</keywords>

## *On the Sesban.* Chapter XXII.

### ALPINI.

SESBAN is a shrub found there the size of a <term>myrtle<note>Myrthus: a common Mediterranean evergreen shrub known for its aromatic leaves.</note></term>, with leaves like those of the <term>hatchet-vetch<note>original: "securidacæ"; likely Securigera securidaca, a plant in the pea family.</note></term>, but longer and narrower, having tender, green branches. It bears saffron-colored flowers, very similar to those of the <term>stinking bean-trefoil<note>original: "anagyridis"; refers to Anagyris foetida, a shrub with yellow pea-like flowers.</note></term>, with many hanging in clusters from the leaf axils or small branches. From these, long pods are produced, very similar to <term>fenugreek<note>original: "fænugreco"; a herb commonly used in both ancient and modern medicine and cooking.</note></term>, inside of which are seeds that are likewise quite similar to the seeds of fenugreek. The Egyptians use these shrubs for weaving hedges to mark the boundaries of their fields. I have learned that these seeds have the following medicinal uses: indeed, they say they dry and strengthen a "moist" stomach, and they also employ them both in decoctions and in powders to restrain any <term>flux of the belly<note>original: "alui fluxum"; a general historical term for diarrhea.</note></term>. Furthermore, they find them significantly useful for stopping the immoderate flow of menstruation; and to put it briefly, they use those seeds most frequently for strengthening the body during all types of excessive <term>evacuations<note>original: "vacuationibus"; referring to the loss of bodily fluids or discharges.</note></term>.

<image-desc>A large ornamental tailpiece woodcut featuring a central grotesque mask with a stylized beard and a crown-like floral crest. The face is flanked by symmetrical, elaborate scrollwork and foliage, with hanging swags of fruit or leaves at the bottom.</image-desc>

## *On the Sesban.* Chapter XXII.

### ALPINI.

**SESBAN** is a shrub found there the size of a <term>myrtle<note>original: "myrti"; a common Mediterranean evergreen shrub known for its aromatic leaves and symbolic associations with love and peace.</note></term>, with leaves like those of the <term>hatchet-vetch<note>original: "securidacæ"; likely Securigera securidaca, a plant in the pea family named for its hatchet-shaped pods.</note></term>, but longer and narrower, having tender, green branches. It bears saffron-colored flowers, very similar to those of the <term>stinking bean-trefoil<note>original: "anagyridis"; refers to Anagyris foetida, a shrub with yellow pea-like flowers known for its strong, unpleasant odor.</note></term>, with many hanging in clusters from the leaf <term>axils<note>The angle between the upper side of a leaf or stem and the supporting stem.</note></term> or small branches. From these, long pods are produced, very similar to <term>fenugreek<note>original: "fænugreco"; a Mediterranean herb whose seeds have been used for millennia in cooking and to soothe inflammation.</note></term>, inside of which are seeds that are likewise quite similar to the seeds of fenugreek.

The Egyptians use these shrubs for weaving hedges to mark the boundaries of their fields. I have learned that these seeds have the following medicinal uses: indeed, they say they dry and strengthen a <term>"moist" stomach<note>In the medical theory of the time, an excess of the "moist" humor in the stomach was thought to cause poor digestion and lethargy.</note></term>, and they also employ them both in <term>decoctions<note>A medicinal liquor made by boiling herbal materials.</note></term> and in powders to restrain any <term>flux of the belly<note>original: "alui fluxum"; a general historical term for diarrhea or dysentery.</note></term>. Furthermore, they find them significantly useful for stopping the immoderate flow of menstruation; and to put it briefly, they use those seeds most frequently for strengthening the body during all types of excessive <term>evacuations<note>original: "vacuationibus"; referring to the loss of bodily fluids or discharges, which were believed to weaken the patient's "vital spirits."</note></term>.

<summary>This page describes the Sesban (Sesbania sesban), an Egyptian shrub used for field hedges and as a medicinal treatment for digestive issues and excessive fluid loss.</summary>

# OF THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

## *On the Sophera.* Chapter XXIV.

### ALPINI.

**SOPHERA** is a shrubby plant about a <term>cubit<note>original: "cubitalis"; an ancient unit of measurement based on the length of the forearm, approximately 18 inches (45 cm).</note></term> in height, very closely related to <term>goat’s rue<note>original: "galegæ"; refers to Galega officinalis, a plant in the legume family used historically in herbal medicine.</note></term>. It has hard branches and many leaves similar to those of the myrtle but thinner, which are arranged in orderly rows on both sides of the stem like wings. It bears saffron-colored, scentless flowers that rival the flowers of the <term>cassia<note>original: "cassiæ"; a genus of yellow-flowering plants often used for their laxative properties.</note></term>. From these flowers, small pods emerge containing four or five seeds, which are entirely similar to those of the Sesban described previously. It is said that these seeds are poisonous to humans.

# OF THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

## *On the Sophera.* Chapter XXIV.

### ALPINI.

The **SOPHERA** <note>identified as *Senna sophera*, a relative of the pea and bean family</note> is a shrubby plant reaching about a <term>cubit<note>original: "cubitalis"; an ancient unit of measurement based on the length of the forearm, approximately 18 inches (45 cm).</note></term> in height, and it is very closely related to <term>goat’s rue<note>original: "galegæ"; refers to *Galega officinalis*, a plant used historically in herbal medicine.</note></term>. It possesses hard branches and an abundance of leaves similar to those of the myrtle, but thinner; these are arranged in orderly rows on both sides of the stem like wings <note>this refers to a pinnate leaf structure, where leaflets grow opposite each other</note>. It bears saffron-colored, scentless flowers that rival the beauty of the flowers of the <term>cassia<note>original: "cassiæ"; a genus of yellow-flowering plants often used in the Renaissance for their laxative properties.</note></term>. From these flowers, small pods emerge containing four or five seeds, which are entirely similar to those of the Sesban described in the previous chapter. It is said that these seeds are poisonous to humans.

# OF THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

## On the Beidelsar. Chapter XXV.

### ALPINI.

**BEIDELSAR** <note>identified as *Calotropis procera*, commonly known as the Apple of Sodom or Giant Milkweed</note> grows in damp places near Alexandria along a branch of the Nile called the <term>Khalig<note>original: "Calig"; refers to the ancient canal system in Egypt.</note></term>, and in Cairo in a location near <term>el-Matariya<note>original: "el Mattharia"; an area famous in the Renaissance for its gardens and the "Balsam of Gilead" trees.</note></term> which is almost always damp, and nearly marshy, from the water of the Nile that remains there for a long time. It is a herbaceous, shrubby plant, raising itself up with many straight shoots from the root to a height of nearly two <term>cubits<note>approximately 3 feet or 90 cm.</note></term>. It has broad, oblong, rounded leaves, like the leaves of <term>Aloe<note>referring to the succulent, thick quality of the leaves.</note></term>, thick and white, from which a white milk flows when they are cut. Indeed, the entire plant abounds with milk <note>sap or latex</note>. It bears saffron-colored flowers very similar to those of <term>buttercups<note>original: "ranunculi".</note></term>, from which, in place of fruit, two "testicles" <note>referring to the twin seed follicles characteristic of this plant family, which resemble the anatomy suggested by the name</note> hang bound together, quite resembling human testicles. Inside these are contained white down <note>silky seed fibers</note> and small, broad seeds, rivaling those of lettuce. 

The leaves, crushed either raw or boiled in water, are used in the manner of a plaster for "cold" tumors and pains <note>in Galenic medicine, "cold" ailments were believed to be caused by an excess of phlegm and required warming treatments</note>. From the downy fibers, they prepare bedding which they use. They also use it as <term>tinder<note>original: "esca, ac pabulo"; literally "food and fodder" for a flame.</note></term> to catch fire struck from flint. The whole plant teems with a very hot and burning milk, which many collect in certain vessels to strip the hair from hides; for if they remain in that milk for any length of time, they are denuded of their hair. The dried milk also powerfully loosens the bowels, and even causes lethal dysenteric flows <note>a warning regarding its high toxicity if ingested</note>. It is also a most excellent remedy for curing <term>impetigo<note>a contagious skin infection characterized by sores and crusts.</note></term> and for healing many other skin infections, if the parts are anointed with it.

# On *Achaouan abiat*, that is *white wormwood*. Chapter XXVI.

## ALPINUS.

There grows in many places in Cairo a certain shrubby plant, dense with branches, which rises to a height of up to three cubits <note>A cubit is approximately 18 inches or 45 centimeters; thus, the plant grows to about 4.5 feet.</note>. It has the leaves of **artemisia** <note>wormwood</note>, which are dark on the inner side and white on the outer; the entire plant is downy or hairy, and it bears many golden flowers in the manner of an **umbel** <note>a flat-topped flower cluster</note>, very similar to **groundsel** <note>original: "senecioni"; a common flowering weed</note>, which finally turn into downy seeds. The flowers and leaves have little scent, and a sweetish taste with some astringency. Many of the locals maintain that this is the "greater and white Achaouan," indeed a species of wormwood, which is why they call it **Achaouan el chebit abiat** <note>original: "Achaouam el chebit abiat"; likely a transliteration of the Arabic *al-uqhuwan al-shibat al-abyad*, meaning white wormwood chamomile</note>. Other herbalists of our time call this plant **cineraria**, and others **marine Jacobæa** <note>known today as *Jacobaea maritima* or Dusty Miller</note>. The Egyptians use it for many things; indeed, they say a decoction prepared from the leaves is powerful against stones of the kidneys and bladder, and also for healing old obstructions of the internal organs. It is used especially for uterine obstructions, "coldness" of the womb, **strangulation** <note>original: "strangulationem"; a historical medical term for "suffocation of the womb" or hysteria</note>, sterility, swelling, and for recalling interrupted menstruation. Consequently, hysterical women are greatly helped by **hip-baths** <note>original: "insessionibus"; a medical bath where the patient sits in a decoction up to the waist</note> prepared from the leaves and flowers in water.

## GUILANDINUS.

->Unknown to the ancients,<-
->unless one were to<-
->classify it under the<-
->species of wormwood.<-

### A C H A O V A N   A B I A T.

# On *Achaouan abiat*, that is, *white wormwood*. Chapter XXVI.

## ALPINUS.

There grows in many places in Cairo a certain shrubby plant, dense with branches, which rises to a height of up to three cubits <note>A cubit is a traditional unit of measurement based on the length of the forearm, approximately 18 inches or 45 centimeters; thus, the plant grows to about 4.5 feet.</note>. It has the leaves of **artemisia** <term>wormwood; a genus of bitter plants in the daisy family used for medicinal purposes</term>, which are dark on the inner side and white on the outer; the entire plant is downy or hairy, and it bears many golden flowers in the manner of an **umbel** <term>a flower cluster where individual stalks spring from a common center, like the ribs of an umbrella</term>, very similar to **groundsel** <note>original Latin: "senecioni"; a common yellow-flowering plant often considered a weed</note>, which finally turn into downy seeds.

The flowers and leaves have little scent, and a sweetish taste with some astringency. Many of the locals maintain that this is the "greater and white Achaouan," indeed a species of wormwood, which is why they call it **Achaouan el chebit abiat** <note>original: "Achaouam el chebit abiat"; likely a transliteration of the Arabic *al-uqhuwan al-shibat al-abyad*, meaning white wormwood chamomile</note>. Other herbalists of our time call this plant **cineraria** <note>literally "ash-colored," referring to the silvery-grey foliage</note>, and others **marine Jacobæa** <note>known today as *Jacobaea maritima* or Dusty Miller, a common coastal plant with silver leaves</note>.

The Egyptians use it for many things; indeed, they say a decoction prepared from the leaves is powerful against stones of the kidneys and bladder, and also for healing old obstructions of the internal organs. It is used especially for uterine obstructions, "coldness" of the womb, and **strangulation** <note>original Latin: "strangulationem"; a historical medical term for "suffocation of the womb," a condition once thought to cause respiratory distress and anxiety, often associated with hysteria</note> of the uterus.

<summary>In this chapter, Alpinus describes a species of white wormwood found in Cairo, identifying its physical characteristics and local Arabic names. He notes its classification by European herbalists as Cineraria and details its use in Egyptian medicine for treating kidney stones and reproductive ailments.</summary>

<keywords>Achaovan Abiat, white wormwood, Cairo, Egyptian botany, Artemisia, Cineraria, kidney stones, women's health, uterine obstructions</keywords>
