BOOK.

## On Bammia. Chapter XXVII.

### ALPINUS.

**BAMMIA** <note>Also known as okra (*Abelmoschus esculentus*).</note> is a plant the size of the **marshmallow** <term>Althaea officinalis; a plant in the mallow family known for its mucilaginous properties</term>. It has the leaves and flowers of a **mallow** <term>Malva; a genus of flowering plants</term>, though they are golden in color; from these, fruits are produced that resemble certain small cucumbers, which contain small, blackish, round seeds. These are used as food among those people [the Egyptians]. From these seeds, they prepare dishes in the same way our people do from **kidney beans**, **peas**, and other legumes. They eat these dishes very frequently, most often prepared in a meat broth.

They say that the leaves and fruits are **cold and moist in the first degree** <term>a classification in Galenic medicine where plants were ranked by their temperament to treat bodily imbalances</term>, and possess a power that is resolving and softening. Therefore, they are suitable for the chest and lungs; they soften the belly and make it slippery <note>acting as a mild laxative</note>. Applied in the manner of a compress or a plaster, the leaves resolve inflammations; they ease pains, soften tumors, and promote their **suppuration** <term>the process of a wound or tumor forming pus to heal</term>.

Women use the leaves and fruits with great benefit for hardness of the uterus, applying them as compresses, plasters, or in **sitz-baths** <term>insessionibus; a medicinal bath where one sits in warm herbal water</term>. Indeed, for inflammation of the kidneys and for stones, a decoction prepared from them and taken by mouth with **candy sugar** <note>original Latin: "saccharo candido"; highly refined sugar used in medicine</note> for several days is considered a most excellent remedy. For **ophthalmias** <term>eye inflammations</term>, once the discharge has ceased, they frequently use the decoction as an **eye-wash** <gloss>colirij</gloss>, and with it they are accustomed to
->foment the aching side<-
->in those with pleurisy.<- <note>Pleurisy is an inflammation of the linings of the lungs which causes sharp chest pain.</note>

<sig>D: 3</sig>

BOOK I.

## On Bammia. Chapter XXVII.

### ALPINUS.

**BAMMIA** <note>Also known as okra (*Abelmoschus esculentus*).</note> is a plant the size of the **marshmallow** <term>Althaea officinalis; a plant in the mallow family known for its mucilaginous (sticky/thick) properties</term>. It has the leaves and flowers of a **mallow** <term>Malva; a genus of flowering plants</term>, though they are golden in color; from these, fruits are produced that resemble certain small cucumbers, which contain small, blackish, round seeds. These are used as food among those people [the Egyptians]. From these seeds, they prepare dishes in the same way our people do from **kidney beans**, **peas**, and other legumes. They eat these dishes very frequently, most often prepared in a meat broth.

They say that the leaves and fruits are **cold and moist in the first degree** <term>a classification in Galenic medicine where plants were ranked by their temperament—hot, cold, moist, or dry—to treat bodily imbalances</term>, and possess a power that is resolving and softening. Therefore, they are suitable for the chest and lungs; they soften the belly and make it slippery <note>acting as a mild laxative</note>. Applied in the manner of a compress or a plaster, the leaves resolve inflammations; they ease pains, soften tumors, and promote their **suppuration** <term>the process of a wound or tumor forming pus as part of the healing process</term>.

Women use the leaves and fruits with great benefit for hardness of the uterus, applying them as compresses, plasters, or in **sitz-baths** <term>insessionibus; a medicinal bath where one sits in warm herbal water to treat the pelvic area</term>. Indeed, for inflammation of the kidneys and for stones, a decoction prepared from them and taken by mouth is said to provide great relief.

<meta>This page continues Prospero Alpini's botanical survey of Egypt. Having just finished Chapter XXVII on Bammia (okra), Alpini now begins Chapter XXVIII, describing Melochia (Jute mallow), another staple plant of the Egyptian diet and pharmacy.</meta>

BOOK I.

## On Melochia. Chapter XXVIII.

### ALPINUS.

**MELOCHIA** <note>Known today as Jute mallow or *Corchorus olitorius*; the leaves are the primary ingredient in the traditional Middle Eastern soup, Molokhia.</note> is a plant very similar to **amaranth** <note>original: *blito*; referring to *Blitum* or species of the Amaranth family</note>, though it has narrower, longer, and sharper leaves. Its flowers are small and yellow, smaller than those of the **wallflower** <note>original: *leucois*</note>; from these flowers are produced small, black seeds very similar to **black cumin** <term>Nigella sativa; a spice known for its pungent, bitter taste and medicinal properties</term>, which are enclosed in certain curved pods.

Among the Egyptians, the seeds are in very frequent use for all conditions for which **marshmallow** <term>Althaea officinalis; a plant valued since antiquity for its soothing, "slippery" mucilage</term> seeds are found to be helpful. Indeed, Melochia appears to have properties entirely similar to marshmallow: therefore, its greatest use is for ripening or maturing, resolving, and softening hard tumors. Its mucilage also possesses a greater stickiness than that of marshmallow.

Regarding food, nothing is more common or more pleasing to the Egyptians than this plant: they boil it in water or meat broth, just as we are accustomed to boiling **beets**. Banquets lacking dishes of Melochia are held in low esteem by them. Indeed, Melochia is the most familiar food for those people, though many suffer ill effects from it; it provides little nourishment and generates a sticky juice, which causes those who eat it frequently to suffer from difficult obstructions of the internal organs. Nevertheless, they do not omit Melochia from their meals, and everyone greedily eats it, especially when it produces a stickier mucilage.

However, a decoction of the whole plant, and especially of the leaves, is friendly to the chest, for it moistens it; thus, when taken with **rock candy** <note>original: *saccharo candido*</note>, it is a ready 

->remedy for dry and rough coughs, as well as for those<-
->breathing with difficulty. The seeds,<-
->taken in a weight of two drachms<-
->[about 7 grams], very copiously<-
->purge all<-
->humors.<-

MELOCHIA.

<summary>Alpini describes Melochia (Jute mallow), noting its extreme popularity in the Egyptian diet despite its tendency to cause internal "obstructions" due to its slimy texture. Medicinally, he compares its soothing, mucilaginous properties to marshmallow, recommending it for tumors and respiratory issues.</summary>

BOOK I.

## Chapter XXVIII: On Melochia.

### ALPINUS.

**MELOCHIA** <note>Known today as Jute mallow or *Corchorus olitorius*; the leaves remain the primary ingredient in the traditional Middle Eastern soup, Molokhia.</note> is a plant very similar to **amaranth** <note>original: *blito*; referring to *Blitum* or species of the Amaranth family commonly used as potherbs</note>, though it has narrower, longer, and sharper leaves. Its flowers are small and yellow, smaller than those of the **wallflower** <note>original: *leucois*; likely referring to the *Matthiola* or *Cheiranthus* genus</note>; from these flowers are produced small, black seeds very similar to **black cumin** <term>Nigella sativa; a flowering plant whose pungent seeds are used as a spice and medicine</term>, which are enclosed in certain curved pods.

Among the Egyptians, the seeds are in very frequent use for all conditions for which **marshmallow** <term>Althaea officinalis; a plant valued since antiquity for its soothing, "slippery" mucilage used to treat inflammation</term> seeds are found to be helpful. Indeed, Melochia appears to have properties entirely similar to marshmallow: therefore, its greatest use is for ripening or maturing, resolving, and softening hard tumors. Its mucilage <note>A thick, gluey substance produced by the plant</note> also possesses a greater stickiness than that of marshmallow.

Regarding food, nothing is more common or more pleasing to the Egyptians than this plant: they boil it in water or meat broth, just as we are accustomed to boiling **beets**. Banquets lacking dishes of Melochia are held in low esteem by them. Indeed, Melochia is the most familiar food for those people, though many suffer ill effects from it; it provides little nourishment and generates a sticky juice <note>referring to the viscous, gelatinous texture the leaves develop when cooked</note>, which causes those who eat it frequently to suffer from difficult obstructions of the internal organs. Nevertheless...

## Chapter XXIX: On Zatarendi.

### ALPINUS.

They call a certain herb **Zatarendi** <note>Derived from the Arabic *za’atar*, a general name for various aromatic herbs like thyme, oregano, or savory. Alpini notes it was imported from India to Egypt.</note> (because it is brought there from India). It grows very little, staying low to the ground, and possesses thick, round, white leaves that are full of juice and highly fragrant. It produces neither flowers nor fruit. Many people eat it dried as a seasoning <note>original: *obsonio*; referring to a relish or side-dish that adds flavor to a meal</note>, much like **oregano** <note>original: *origanum*</note>. I hear that among many people it is used to treat a **cough** <note>original: *tussim*</note> and difficulty in breathing; it is also used to promote the flow of urine and to stimulate **menstruation** <term>emmenagogue; a substance that stimulates or increases menstrual flow</term>.

ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

### Chapter XXX: On Long Pepper.

->ALPINUS.<-

There is a certain herb cultivated in some gardens with leaves like those of rushes, but thicker, from which, when they are cut, a yellow juice flows. This juice is extremely sharp and burning; indeed, the entire plant is hot in the fourth degree <term>fourth degree; in Galenic medicine, the highest and most potent level of a quality (heat, cold, moisture, or dryness), indicating a substance so strong it can blister or destroy tissue</term>, burning even more than common pepper. They use this plant applied as a plaster for pain in the hip <term>sciatica; nerve pain that radiates from the hip down the leg</term>, as well as for "cold" pains in the joints <note>In ancient medicine, "cold" pains were thought to be caused by a lack of vital heat or an accumulation of phlegm in the joints</note>. To prevent injury, they temper its excessive heat with rose oil or myrtle oil.

->L O N G  P E P P E R.<- <note>original: *Felfel Tavil*; from the Arabic *filfil tawil*, meaning "long pepper."</note>

## ABSUS.
### ALPINUS.

THERE is also a certain small herb growing to the size of a palm <note>a "palm" (Latin: *palmus*) was a traditional unit of measurement based on the width of a human hand, roughly 3 to 4 inches</note>, or slightly larger, with leaves like those of a trefoil <note>original: *trifolij*; referring to plants like clover which typically have three leaflets</note>, but rounder, with four leaflets attached to each stalk. It bears small, white flowers and seeds very similar to the seeds of a pear. This is the plant which the Egyptians call **Absus**. <note>The plant is known today as *Chamaecrista absus*, or "Jasmine Flowering Senna." Its seeds were historically used in Egypt to treat eye diseases like ophthalmia.</note>

## On Sesame or Sempfen. Chapter XXXII.

-> ALPINUS <-

**SEMPFEN**, called sesame by our people, is a plant with a single straight stalk that rises to a height of one cubit <note>An ancient unit of measurement, approximately 18 inches, the length of a forearm from elbow to fingertip.</note>. It is clothed throughout the stalk with densely arranged leaves similar to those of the nightshade <note>original: *solatro*</note>. It bears small white flowers, from which are produced small, four-ribbed pods containing yellow seeds similar to flax seeds, which are sweet to the taste. From these seeds, they produce an oil they call *sitis* <note>Alpinus likely refers to the Arabic word *seyt* or *zeyt*, which generally means oil</note>, which is very frequently used for food and is sold there at a higher price than olive oil. The leaves, seeds, and oil are found to have nearly the same properties—namely, a temperate warmth and a moisture approaching the second degree—acting to resolve <note>to reduce swelling or inflammation</note> and soften. 

They use a decoction of the plant as a wash applied to the eyes to resolve ophthalmias <note>a general term for severe eye inflammation or infection</note>. It is also used for coughs, difficulty breathing, pleurisy, pneumonia, and hard, scirrhous <note>hard, fibrous tumors or swellings</note> tumors. Women frequently use sitting-baths <note>original: *insessiones*; a therapeutic soak for the pelvic region</note> made from the plant and seeds with a little honey for hardness of the uterus. They use the same preparation for skin defects, specifically for dandruff of the head, bruising of the face, and black spots arising from congealed blood, by frequently applying it as a warm wash. From the plant and seeds boiled in water with a little honey, they prepare plasters for scirrhous tumors and for "dried-out" nerves <note>likely referring to tendons or ligaments that have become stiff or wasted</note>, which they are accustomed to using effectively. 

These are also frequently used for burned parts, no less than for resolving or drawing out pus from hot inflammations. They even employ the decoction in enemas for colicky pains and to soften the bowels. Women keep the decoction and water-infusion as a secret aid to induce menstrual flow; those whose menstruation is blocked due to the thickness of the blood or obstruction of the uterine veins are not disappointed in their hope. 

The use of the seed for food is an ancient practice among all the inhabitants of Egypt; however, it offers little nourishment, providing instead an oily and thick sustenance. From this, it is easily understood why women become fat from eating them frequently. Indeed, those who desire their bodies to become plump eat the seeds after they have been cleaned of their husks. The oil expressed from the seeds is even more fattening, and most fattening of all is the dregs of the oil called <term>thaine</term> <note>tahini; a paste made from toasted, ground, hulled sesame seeds</note> by them. To drink the oil and its dregs,

<summary>Prospero Alpini describes the physical characteristics and diverse uses of the sesame plant in Egypt, noting its superiority to olive oil in the local market. He details its medicinal applications for respiratory, skin, and gynecological issues, as well as its use as a dietary supplement for weight gain.</summary>

<keywords>Sesame, Sesamum indicum, Sempfen, Tahini, Egyptian medicine, botanical oil, ophthalmia, pectoral health, women's health, nutritional history</keywords>

to drink it, moreover, so that they might become plump; women in the baths use it as a very common practice. The Egyptians have also been accustomed to use the oil for many other things, especially for skin pustules, roughness, and every blemish provoked by the melancholic humor <note>In ancient medicine, "black bile," one of the four humors believed to cause various physical and mental ailments if out of balance</note>, whether it is drunk, used frequently in food, or even when the oil itself is rubbed onto the affected parts. Among certain people, there is a secret remedy for abolishing skin itches: administering four ounces of it to be drunk for many days. They also use it for difficulty of breathing, for desperate cases of pleurisy in which the patients cannot expectorate <note>cough up phlegm</note> anything, and for pneumonia. Furthermore, it is used to induce menstruation and to soothe the violent torments of stomach, intestinal, and uterine pains, being applied both internally and externally. 

**GUILANDINUS:** I give you many thanks for telling me of so many and such great medical uses of this plant discovered by those people. I judge these uses to be worthy of the ears of your own doctors as well, since these things were not manifest to the Egyptians alone; many other most distinguished physicians have passed down almost the same things for the memory of posterity, such as Rhazes, Avicenna, Haly, Serapio <note>Famous Persian and Arab physicians—Al-Razi, Ibn Sina, Haly Abbas, and Serapion—whose works were central to Renaissance medical education</note>, and others. But now it remains for us to look at the illustration of the plant, and whether it is the one which some have depicted, who seem to have been deceived in their uncertainty by the reading of Theophrastus <note>An ancient Greek philosopher known as the "Father of Botany"</note>. They call this plant **sempsem**.

->CONCERNING THE PLANTS OF EGYPT<-

# S E M P S E M  S E S A M E <note>original: "Sempsem Sesamum." 'Sempsem' is a Latinization of the Arabic word 'simsim,' meaning sesame.</note>

A detailed botanical woodcut illustration of a sesame plant (*Sesamum indicum*), identified in the text as Sempsem Sesamum. The drawing shows the entire plant from the fibrous roots at the bottom to the budding top. The central stalk branches out into several stems adorned with lanceolate, serrated leaves. Along the upper portions of the stems, elongated, upright seed pods (capsules) are growing from the leaf axils. The illustration style is characteristic of 16th or 17th-century herbals.

<meta>catchword: Of</meta>

### BOOK 2.

...to drink it, moreover, so that they might become plump; women in the baths use it as a very common practice. The Egyptians have also been accustomed to use the oil for many other things, especially for skin pustules, roughness, and every blemish provoked by the melancholic humor <note>In ancient medicine, "black bile," one of the four humors believed to cause various physical and mental ailments if out of balance.</note>, whether it is drunk, used frequently in food, or even when the oil itself is rubbed onto the affected parts. Among certain people, there is a secret remedy for abolishing skin itches: administering four ounces of it to be drunk for many days. They also use it for difficulty of breathing, for desperate cases of pleurisy in which the patients cannot expectorate <note>cough up phlegm</note> anything, and for pneumonia. Furthermore, it is used to induce menstruation and to soothe the violent torments of stomach, intestinal, and uterine pains, being applied both internally and externally. 

**GUILANDINUS:** I give you many thanks for telling me of so many and such great medical uses of this plant discovered by those people. I judge these uses to be worthy of the ears of your own doctors as well, since these things were not manifest to the Egyptians alone; many other most distinguished physicians have passed down almost the same things for the memory of posterity, such as Rhazes, Avicenna, Haly, Serapio <note>Famous Persian and Arab physicians—Al-Razi, Ibn Sina, Haly Abbas, and Serapion—whose works were central to Renaissance medical education.</note>

### Concerning Culcas or Colocassia. Chapter XXXIII.

### ALPINUS.

NO plant is better known to the Egyptians than the **colocassia** <note>The Taro plant (*Colocasia esculenta*), a staple root crop in tropical and subtropical regions.</note>, which they call **Culcas** <note>original: "Culcas." A Latinization of the Arabic word 'qulqas.'</note>. It is also no plant more widely used, since they constantly eat the roots of this plant boiled in broth and prepared as food in many other ways, just as our people eat turnips. Fields full of colocassias are seen there, and—what seems more marvelous—none of those people has ever seen any of these plants endowed with flowers or fruit. Out of the countless living specimens I have often inspected, I have never been able to see any flowering, nor with any fruit, nor even with a stalk.

Because of this, it would seem doubtful that this plant is the **Egyptian bean** <note>original: "faba Aegyptia." This was a point of major botanical confusion in the Renaissance; the "Egyptian bean" of the ancients was actually the Sacred Lotus (*Nelumbo nucifera*), not the Taro.</note> described by the Greeks. This plant is seen there with leaves like those of the *nenuphar* <note>original: "nenupharis." The water lily.</note>, but longer and very close to those of the *arum* <note>The 'lords-and-ladies' plant, related to taro.</note>; it lacks a stalk, flower, and fruit. Its root is seen to be large and thick, similar to **China roots** <note>original: "chinę radicibus." *Smilax china*, a root from East Asia that was a popular medicinal import in the 16th century.</note>, with a slightly bitter, acrid taste and a certain viscosity, which becomes sweet when cooked. Daily, everywhere and throughout the entire year, countless roots are sold through the city at a very cheap price.

Hence, I have truly always wondered greatly that **Dioscorides** <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and author of *De Materia Medica*.</note> recorded that the Egyptian bean bears flowers and fruit, and also claimed that it was the same as the colocassia. The Egyptians also call this plant by the name **culcas**. I have neither seen it bloom anywhere nor seen it give any fruit, nor have I ever heard of anyone who has ever seen it; I very gladly leave the resolution of this dispute to others. They eat it both raw and cooked, most willingly when **indulging in the acts of Venus** <note>original: "veneri indulgentes." A poetic reference to sexual intercourse.</note>, both because they believe it produces much seed <note>original: "semen." In this context, referring to reproductive fluids.</note> and because it powerfully stirs up desire.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut tailpiece ornament featuring a central ornate urn or vase filled with foliage, flanked by two symmetrical winged cherubs or putti holding garlands.</image-desc>

<meta>catchword: CULCAS</meta>

### Concerning Culcas or Colocasia. Chapter XXXIII.

### ALPINUS.

No plant is better known to the Egyptians than the **colocasia** <note>The Taro plant (*Colocasia esculenta*), a staple root crop in tropical and subtropical regions.</note>, which they call **Culcas** <note>original: "Culcas." A Latinization of the Arabic word *qulqas*.</note>. It is also no plant more widely used, since they constantly eat the roots of this plant boiled in broth and prepared as food in many other ways, just as our people eat turnips. Fields full of colocasias are seen there, and—what seems more marvelous—none of those people has ever seen any of these plants endowed with flowers or fruit. Out of the countless living specimens I have often inspected, I have never been able to see any flowering, nor with any fruit, nor even with a stalk.

Because of this, it would seem doubtful that this plant is the **Egyptian bean** <note>original: "faba Aegyptia." This was a point of major botanical confusion in the Renaissance; the "Egyptian bean" of the ancients was actually the Sacred Lotus (*Nelumbo nucifera*), not the Taro.</note> described by the Greeks. This plant is seen there with leaves like those of the **water lily** <note>original: "nenupharis."</note>, but longer and very close to those of the **arum** <note>The 'lords-and-ladies' plant, related to taro.</note>; it lacks a stalk, flower, and fruit. Its root is seen to be large and thick, similar to **China roots** <note>original: "chinę radicibus." *Smilax china*, a medicinal root from East Asia used at the time to treat various ailments including gout and syphilis.</note>.

## On the Egyptian Lotus called Nuphar. Chapter XXXIV.

### ALPINUS.

Having recognized the error regarding the **Taro** <note>original: *Colocasia*</note> in the previous section, we now take notice of another error concerning the **Egyptian Lotus**, since this plant is clearly the same as what our people call the **water lily** <note>The white water lily, *Nymphaea lotus*.</note> and the Arabs call **Nuphar**. Indeed, in the entire Nile river as it washes through Egyptian lands, no other plant has been found that exhibits the characteristics of the lotus other than the water lily itself. This plant is certainly similar to the **Taro** or **Egyptian Bean**, as Dioscorides affirms of the Lotus; it bears a white flower (I speak of the larger water lily) very similar to a lily. Theophrastus expressed this more clearly when he said: "and the flower is white, closest to lilies in the narrowness of its petals." For truly, this is the flower of the larger water lily, which is closed at sunset and hidden under the water, and at sunrise rises above the water, emerges, and opens.

This is observed in this plant not only in Egypt, but also in many places in Italy, especially in all the marshy or swampy areas around Venice. The valleys of Marghera and Mestre indeed support many of these plants, in which anyone can clearly observe what is described about the Lotus by Theophrastus, Dioscorides, and many others. And what of this? Is there not also produced from the flower a head very similar to the heads of poppies? In this head, many tiny seeds are contained, just like those of millet, and the root is thick. I cannot help but greatly marvel at Theophrastus, Dioscorides, and others, who all separated the Egyptian lotus from the water lily into distinct chapters, when both are clearly one and the same plant. Furthermore, the marvelous movements toward the sun celebrated in accounts of the lotus are the same as those noticed by everyone in the common water lily. For at sunset it closes its flower, then the whole plant with its leaves is hidden under the water, and at sunrise, it first pushes the flower out of the water, opens it, and a little later rises above the water with all its leaves. The Egyptians eat the raw stalks with their heads during the heat of summer; for they are somewhat sweet and moist, and they cool and hydrate the body to a degree. They call these **Razelnil** <note>original: *Razelnil*. From the Arabic *ra's al-Nil*, meaning "Head of the Nile."</note>.

---

<margin>Book 13, chapters 17 and 18.</margin>
**GUILANDINUS.** <note>Melchior Guilandini (c. 1520–1589), a Prussian physician and botanist who preceded Alpinus in studying Egyptian flora.</note> Indeed, I marvel not a little at the diligence of Pliny, who wrote these things. It is a wonder what is handed down beyond these things: [that] at the sun set- <note>original: *occi-dente*</note>

<meta>catchword: -ting (dente)</meta>

<summary>In this chapter, Alpinus identifies the ancient "Egyptian Lotus" described by Dioscorides and Theophrastus as the common white water lily (Nymphaea), correcting contemporary botanical misconceptions. He describes its solar-tracking behavior and its local use as a cooling summer food known as Razelnil.</summary>

<keywords>Egyptian Lotus, Nuphar, Water Lily, Nymphaea, Theophrastus, Dioscorides, Razelnil, Solar movement, Botany, Marghera, Venice</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical description of the Egyptian Lotus (Nymphaea). The previous page ended while citing ancient authorities like Theophrastus and Dioscorides regarding the plant's movement in response to the sun.</meta>

[at sun]set, these poppy-like heads are compressed and covered by leaves. At sunrise, however, they open until they reach maturity, and the flower, which is white, falls away. They report further that in the Euphrates river, both the stalk itself and the flower are submerged in the evening until midnight, sinking so deep into the water that they cannot be found even if one reaches out a hand. Then the plant turns, gradually rises, and at sunrise emerges from the water, opening its flower; it continues to rise until it is clearly high above the water. Since in our common water lily—as you said—all these things are clearly observed in the stagnant fresh waters near the place called Marghera, and near the city of Mestre in all those marshy valleys. Now, proceed to the medicinal uses of the water lily <note>original: *nymphaea*</note> or Egyptian lotus known to the people. 

**ALPINUS.** They use the crushed flowers and leaves for all "hot" inflammations <note>In the humoral medicine of the time, "hot" diseases were treated with "cold" plants like the water lily.</note>, and also the juice, especially for soothing pains arising from a hot cause, mixed with milk and rose oil. In the same way, they use an oil prepared from the flowers for hot pains, inflammations, burns, ulcers, and headaches in those suffering from fevers or insomnia; they apply crushed leaves externally, or they rub the forehead and temples with its juice mixed with oil and vinegar. To induce sleep, they frequently use a syrup prepared from it, and they give patients a powder made from both the seeds and the root along with a decoction <note>A medicinal liquor made by boiling the plant.</note> of the same. 

The use of the root and seeds is held as a secret by many for treating gonorrhea, for "strengthening" women against white discharge <note>original: *album profluuium*; a reference to leukorrhea</note>, and for dysentery. Many, however, avoid the use of this plant, choosing others instead, for fear that both their sexual desire and their potency might be diminished. For it is common knowledge among all of them that the use of the seeds and root makes men very "cold" and sluggish toward Venus <note>original: *ad venerem*; a common euphemism for sexual activity</note>. Because of this, I hear that many holy hermits there follow this practice so that they can more easily lead a celibate life. 

But now our conversation must turn to the **papyrus**, since this plant also grows in the Nile river. But since I know you have already fully dealt with everything pertaining to the knowledge and use of this plant in your commentary on Pliny's chapter on papyrus, I think I should deal with it only briefly here.

# On the Stratiotes, called Watery Houseleek. Chapter 25.
<note>original: *De Stratiote, Haihalem maoui vocato*</note>

## A L P I N V S.

Pelusium, now called Damietta, is a city situated in the lower lands of Egypt. It happens that because the Nile River flows past it (discharging into the Mediterranean Sea at a distance of nearly five miles), its soil is more extensively flooded and irrigated at will. For this reason, many gardens—more lush and pleasant than any others found in all of Egypt—are seen near this city, planted with cassia, lemons, bananas <note>original: *musis*; the banana was often called "Musa" in early botanical texts</note>, and vines. These gardens are divided, in the Italian fashion, by ditches filled with flowing river water, a feature found nowhere else in Egypt.

In those ditches, a certain herb grows in great abundance, floating on the surface like duckweed <note>original: *aquae lenticulae palustris*</note>. It has no stem, and its leaves are like those of Hound's-tongue <note>original: *Cynoglossi*</note>, but shorter, wider, thicker, harder, hairier, and whiter; from these leaves hangs a small and sparse downy growth instead of roots. This plant is similar to the great houseleek or stonecrop <note>original: *sedo, vel semperuiuo*</note>. They call it the Watery Houseleek <note>original: *Hayhalem elmaoui*</note>, as if they were saying "watery sedum." It possesses no scent, except for that which it absorbs from the water. However, it strikes the tongue with an astringent and dry flavor, not unlike the effect of acacia. 

They use this plant for the same ailments for which plantain is known to be effective. For this reason, the country women, whom they call Bedouins <note>original: *Bedoui*</note>, daily consume its decoction, juice, or powder by mouth in the weight of one drachm to stop the eruption of blood from the uterus and other parts. The rural folk also use the crushed leaves applied topically to heal wounds, and they heal them marvelously. They believe the plant to be cold to the first degree and dry beyond the second degree.

## G V I L A N D.

I believe this plant to be the true *Stratiotes* <note>The "Water-soldier," likely *Pistia stratiotes* or Water Lettuce in this Egyptian context</note> of the ancients, about which Dioscorides provided us these details: *The Stratiotes growing in water is called by some the River-Stratiotes; the Egyptians call it Tibus, and the Magi call it "Cat's Blood"* <note>original: *Aeluri sanguis*</note>. *It floats on the waters and lives without a root, from which it draws its name. It is an herb similar to the houseleek, though it has larger leaves.* Dioscorides also writes that it excels at the same ailments for which you said the Egyptians now use it. Therefore, there should be no doubt that this herb is the *Stratiotes*. Because...

<header>ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT</header>

## Chapter XXXVI.
->*On the Papyrus called Berd.*<-

->A L P I N V S.<-

PAPYRUS, which the Egyptians call *berd* <note>original: *berd*; likely a variation of the Arabic *birdi*</note>, is a plant of the Nile river, rising six or seven cubits <note>a cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches; thus, about 9 to 10 feet</note> above the water with two or more very straight stalks. At the tops of the stalks, a flower-head <note>original: *scapus*; referring to the tufted umbel of the papyrus</note> is seen, woven from countless long and straight filaments. It also consists of many straight, sword-shaped leaves, somewhat similar to the cattail <note>original: *tiphæ*; plants of the genus *Typha*</note>, which are triangular and soft. Surgeons use the pith <note>original: *medulla*</note> of these stalks to dilate the openings of ulcers. From the burnt ash of the flower-head, they treat fresh ulcers; if this ash is frequently sprinkled on them, it also prevents malignant growths from spreading. From the fresh stalks they distill a water that is extremely beneficial for cataracts <note>original: *suffusiones*</note>, dimness, and obscurities of the eyes.

<header>ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT</header>

## Concerning Cyperus. Chapter XXXVII.

->A L P I N U S.<-

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'C' containing a figure in a landscape with foliage, framed by a square border.</image-desc>
ROUND CYPERUS <note>original: *Cyperus rotundus*; commonly known today as nut grass or purple crowfoot</note>, which the Egyptians call *hodueg* <note>Alpini’s transcription of the Arabic *su'ad*</note>, grows most abundantly in the Nile River and in many of Egypt's lakes or marshy places. It produces leaves like those of a leek, though longer and more slender, and a stem a cubit <note>approximately 18 inches</note> long and angled. At the top of the stem are several leaves radiating like a star, from which emerge certain grass-colored spikes containing seeds. The roots are black, round, and large, appearing the size of an olive; many of them are seen hanging together, clinging to a single hairy root-fiber. They are fragrant and have a sharp taste. These roots have a warming effect and dry the body without causing irritation; they "cut through" <term>incidunt: a medical term for breaking up thick or sticky humors like phlegm</term> and thin [fluids], and open the mouths of the veins.

Among the Egyptians, these roots have the greatest number of medical uses. They are a very common remedy especially for healing ulcers of the mouth and the private parts, used both as a powder and as the ash from the burnt roots. A decoction or powder prepared from them—when taken over several days—greatly warms, dries, and strengthens the stomach, brain, lungs, womb, nerves, and joints, and bolsters the natural heat <note>the innate vital warmth believed in Galenic medicine to drive life and digestion</note>. Because of this, they are a most effective aid against all diseases excited by a cold and damp temperament of the brain, as well as those caused by the "distillation" <note>dripping or leaking</note> of cold humors. They are no less effective for indigestion <note>original: *stomachic cruditatibus*</note>, excess dampness, gas, difficulty breathing, orthopnea <term>orthophneę: a condition where one can only breathe comfortably while standing or sitting upright</term>, and coughs. They are also used for all women suffering from "hysterical" conditions <note>illnesses then believed to be caused by a "cold and damp" womb</note> and other diseases arising from those same uterine causes, such as joint pains. It is a well-proven remedy there for suppressed menstruation, as they bring on the menses very effectively. The Egyptians very frequently use the decoction and powder to break up stones in both the kidneys and the bladder and to provoke the flow of urine; they also employ it just as often in all chronic fevers and other long-lasting illnesses.

<meta>catchword: CYPERVS</meta>

<summary>This chapter describes the physical characteristics and extensive medicinal applications of Cyperus rotundus (Nut Grass) in Egypt, highlighting its use for respiratory, digestive, and uterine ailments.</summary>
<keywords>Cyperus rotundus, Nut Grass, Egyptian medicine, Prospero Alpini, Nile flora, Galenic humors, pharmacology, 16th-century botany</keywords>

# BOOK.

## CYPERUS HODVEG.

<image-desc>A detailed botanical woodcut of a Cyperus plant, likely representing a species of nut-grass. The illustration depicts a cluster of long, narrow, lanceolate leaves at the base. Three vertical stalks (culms) rise from the center, each ending in a large, branching umbellate inflorescence of small spikelets. The root system is shown with several distinct, rounded tubers connected by rhizomes. The plant is positioned above a series of horizontal hatching lines that represent water or marshy soil.</image-desc>

## Concerning Cyperus. Chapter XXXVII.

->A L P I N U S.<-

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'C' containing a figure in a landscape with foliage, framed by a square border.</image-desc>

ROUND CYPERUS <note>original: *Cyperus rotundus*; commonly known today as nut grass or purple crowfoot</note>, which the Egyptians call *hodueg* <note>Alpini’s transcription of the Arabic *su'ad*</note>, grows most abundantly in the Nile River and in many of Egypt's lakes or marshy places. It produces leaves like those of a leek, though longer and more slender, and a stem a cubit <note>approximately 18 inches</note> long and angled. At the top of the stem are several leaves radiating like a star, from which emerge certain grass-colored spikes containing seeds. The roots are black, round, and large, appearing the size of an olive; many of them are seen hanging together, clinging to a single hairy root-fiber. They are fragrant and have a sharp taste. These roots have a warming effect and dry the body without causing irritation; they "cut through" <term>incidunt: a medical term for breaking up thick or sticky humors like phlegm</term> and thin [fluids], and open the mouths of the veins.

Among the Egyptians, these roots have the greatest number of medical uses. They are a very common remedy especially for healing ulcers of the mouth and the private parts, used both as a powder and as the ash from the burnt roots. A decoction or powder prepared from them—when taken over several days—greatly warms, dries, and strengthens the stomach, brain, lungs, womb, nerves, and joints, and bolsters the natural heat <note>the innate vital warmth believed in Galenic medicine to drive life and digestion</note>. It also provokes the menstrual flow, promotes urination, and is known to expel stones from the kidneys and bladder.

<meta>Concerning...</meta> <note>original: *De*; a catch-word at the bottom of the page indicating the first word of the following page.</note>

<summary>This page describes the Round Cyperus (Nut Grass), detailing its physical appearance in the Nile marshes and its extensive use in Egyptian medicine to treat ulcers and internal obstructions.</summary>
<keywords>Cyperus rotundus, Nut Grass, Egyptian flora, Nile, herbal medicine, ulcers, Galenic pharmacology, Prospero Alpini</keywords>

ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

# Concerning the Chate, the Abdellawi, and the Watermelon.
## Chapter XXXVIII.
### ALPINI.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'I' set within a square frame, featuring decorative scrolling foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>
THE Egyptians are in the habit of using a certain kind of cucumber called *Chate* <note>original: *Chatè*; likely the Armenian cucumber or hairy cucumber, *Cucumis melo* var. *flexuosus*</note>. This plant does not differ from the common cucumber in any other respect except in size, color, and softness. The Egyptian cucumber has smaller, whiter, softer, and rounder leaves, and it produces fruits very different from our own, as they are longer and greener than ours, and are seen with a smooth, soft, and uniform skin. To the taste, they are sweeter and easier to digest <term>concoctu: a Renaissance medical term referring to the "cooking" or processing of food in the stomach</term>. The use of these as food, with the bodies of those eating them whether raw or cooked, is believed by all the inhabitants of those places to be healthy. Some physicians there even allow them to be eaten raw by those suffering from certain fevers, and especially by those afflicted with the plague; for some reason (I know not which), everyone is persuaded that eating these cucumbers raw is highly beneficial for them. That they are useful when cooked in "burning fevers" <note>acute fevers characterized by high heat and thirst</note> is confirmed by reason, since they cool and moisten the body. In hot diseases of the kidneys and bladder, they consume these cucumbers cooked with milk for many days, not without great benefit. They also frequently take the "milk" <note>an emulsion made by grinding seeds with liquid</note> of the seeds themselves by mouth for the same ailments. For hot inflammations of the eyes and other parts, they use the crushed pulp of the cucumbers mixed with milk. And to soothe gouty pains stirred up by a "hot cause" <term>causa calida: according to Galenic medicine, gout could be caused by an excess of hot humors like blood or yellow bile</term>, they follow the practice of anointing the parts with the juice mixed with milk and oil of roses. They advise patients to drink a distilled water <note>original: *stillatitiam aquam*; water produced by a still to concentrate the plant's "essential" cooling properties</note>, up to a pound, for many days to correct heat of the liver and to heal kidneys that are either inflamed or troubled by stones; experience has found that this water benefits them sufficiently. There are some who, for these same maladies, are accustomed to use the distilled water mixed with the milk of the seeds of these same cucumbers. The Egyptians also have certain melons quite different from our own, which they call *Abdellawi* <note>original: *Abdellaui*; a variety of Chate melon, *Cucumis melo* var. *adana*</note>, to distinguish them from others which they also call *Chaiar* <note>original: *Chaiar*; from the Arabic *khiyar*, referring to the common cucumber</note>. Their use as food is perceived as not very pleasant, and they are observed to be more insipid than all the others and with a softer pulp. They have, however, seeds which [differ] from all...

# BOOK I.

[...they believe] it is far superior for cooling. For this reason, everyone uses these more frequently than others, placing little value on the seeds of other melons. The plant does not differ much from our own [European] ones, but it bears fruits that are oblong, pointed, and very thick in the middle and at the ends, very similar to the Bahobab <note>original: *Bahobab*; likely referring to the fruit of the Baobab tree, which Alpini had recently described</note> fruits recently mentioned. They also have watermelons <note>original: *Angurias*</note> which differ from our own only in size and appearance. Ours are indeed larger, with a yellower rind; these contain only seeds and a very sweet water inside. They drink this water generously to ward off thirst and to correct excessive heat in the stomach, liver, or kidneys. For burning fevers and tertian fevers <note>fever that recurs every third day, common in malaria</note>, the use of that water is highly praised and frequently practiced by them. There are some who extract a thinner water through distillation for those who are thirsty, those with bilious stomachs, or those with hot livers and kidneys troubled by stones <term>calculis: kidney or bladder stones</term>. They certainly find this to be of great utility. The Turks, Egyptians, and Arabs who lead a more luxurious life frequently use the water of these watermelons as a drink during the peak heat of summer; some even mix in a small amount of rose water, musk, and amber. It is the custom of the Egyptians to honor visitors with these, offering them to be eaten. They preserve them throughout the year, keeping them uncorrupted in straw; sometimes they are sold there at such a high price for the sick that I have known one to be sold for a silver or gold coin. Indeed, its

### *On the Achaouan, or Odorless Feverfew:* Chapter 39.

->A L P I N I<- <note>original: *Alpinus*; referring to Prospero Alpini (1553–1617), a Venetian physician and botanist who spent several years in Egypt.</note>

<image-desc>A faint ghost illustration of a botanical specimen, featuring a large leaf and stem, is visible in the background, likely bleed-through from the verso of the leaf.</image-desc>

ACHAOVAN <note>original: *Achaouan*; likely a species of *Anthemis* or *Matricaria*, plants in the chamomile family.</note> is a herbaceous plant very close to chamomile, which, however, does not grow quite so tall, but instead spreads out more widely just above the ground. It has leaves like those of <term>feverfew</term> <note>original: *parthenij*; *Tanacetum parthenium*, a traditional medicinal herb used to treat fevers and inflammation.</note>, though they are smaller and more densely packed, and flowers that rival those of chamomile. The herb is found to be devoid of taste and smell, but the flowers breathe out a heavy and very faint scent. They use a decoction of the flowers and leaves to remove obstructions of the internal organs, and especially for those suffering from <term>jaundice</term> <note>original: *Ictericos*; from the Greek *ikteros*, referring to the yellowing of the skin caused by liver or bile dysfunction.</note>.

->GUILANDINUS<- <note>original: *Guilandinus*; Melchior Guilandinus (c. 1520–1589), a Prussian physician and botanist who served as the director of the Botanical Garden of Padua.</note>. If a sharp taste were perceived in this plant, it would, without doubt, be the <term>stinking chamomile</term> <note>original: *cotula*; likely *Anthemis cotula*, known for its pungent odor and acrid taste.</note> described by Serapion <note>Serapion the Elder, a 9th-century Syrian Christian physician whose works on medicinal plants were highly influential in the Renaissance.</note>.

->ALPINI.<- And these are the plants that I was able to observe as being native to Egypt.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut vignette featuring a winged putto or cherub seated among stylized foliage and fruits.</image-desc>

<summary>This page describes the Achaouan, an Egyptian herb similar to chamomile used medicinally to treat internal obstructions and jaundice. It concludes Alpini's observations on the native flora of Egypt with a brief scholarly comparison to the works of Serapion.</summary>
<keywords>Achaouan, chamomile, feverfew, jaundice, medical botany, Prospero Alpini, Melchior Guilandinus, Egyptian flora, medicinal decoctions</keywords>

## On the Neiemelmsalib, or Grass of the Cross. Chapter 41.

->A L P I N V S<- <note>original: *Alpinus*; referring to the author, Prospero Alpini, who is describing his observations in Egypt.</note>

A certain slender grass grows here, with creeping white roots that are jointed <note>original: *geniculatis*; referring to the "knees" or nodes on the roots and stems.</note> and sweet, though they possess a certain sharp pungency. These roots are very similar to common grass. The plant reaches the height of a finger, rising with jointed stalks and small branches, with leaves similar to common grass but smaller. These small branches bear four spikes <note>original: *spicas*; the flower or seed-bearing heads of the grass.</note>, resembling those of <term>Ischaemum</term> <note>original: *Ischæmi*; likely referring to a species of *Digitaria* or finger-grass, named from the Greek for its supposed ability to stop bleeding.</note>, or the spikes of common grass...

...resembling blood-grass <note>original: *ischaemi sanguinarij*; likely referring to *Digitaria sanguinalis*, a grass whose spikes radiate like fingers.</note>, forming a perfect cross, which they all shape in exactly the same way. For this reason, the Egyptians call it *neiem el* <note>original: *neiem el*; the full name is *Neiem-el-msalib*, where *salib* means cross in Arabic.</note>, as if they were saying "the grass of the cross." Within these spikes are very tiny seeds, similar to the common seeds of grass. These seeds are of the greatest use among all people, specifically for those suffering from stones <note>original: *calculis*</note> in both the kidneys and the bladder, and for breaking up stones formed in the bladder. This type of disease is very common there—almost endemic—for reasons described elsewhere. Women frequently use a decoction of the root for children afflicted by smallpox <note>original: *variolis*</note> and measles <note>original: *morbillis*</note>, as well as for themselves when they wish to call back suppressed menstrual cleansings. There are some who hold as a secret remedy a decoction prepared from moderately crushed seeds, used to assist with the rashes <term>exanthemata</term> <note>original: *exhantemata*; skin eruptions or spots appearing during a fever</note> in pestilential fevers, which our people call <term>petechiae</term> <note>original: *peticulas*; small purple or red spots on the skin caused by minor hemorrhaging, often associated with serious fevers like typhus.</note>. The whole plant, and especially the roots, is of no small use among them for the treatment of wounds and ulcers.

### On the Egyptian Melilot called Alchimelech. <note>original: *Alchimelech*; likely a corruption of the Arabic *al-malik* ("the king") or *Iklil al-malik* ("The King’s Crown"), a traditional name for Melilot.</note>
### Chapter 41.

### ALPINUS.

The MELILOT <note>original: *Melilotus*; a genus of legumes in the bean family, often called sweet clover.</note> which the Egyptians use has a very small, round seed, somewhat blackish, though leaning toward red. It is not entirely without scent and possesses an astringent <term>stiptic</term> <note>original: *stiptico*; a substance that causes body tissues to contract, often used to stop bleeding or reduce secretions.</note> taste with a certain bitterness. The plant that produces this is a small herb spread out upon the ground, moderately creeping, lifting itself into the air hardly at all, or only very little. It features the leaves of a pod-bearing trefoil <note>original: *trifolij siliquosi*; a three-leafed plant, like clover, that bears distinct seed pods.</note>, though smaller, and small flowers...

<meta>Catchword: ALCHI-</meta>

...with many long [stalks], arranged in the manner of a cluster <note>original: *racemi*; like a bunch of grapes</note>, saffron-colored, fragrant, from which come small, slanted pods <note>original: *siliquæ*</note> containing the seeds described earlier. Only the seeds of this plant are in use among those people. They consider them to be temperate in regards to heat and cold, but they believe they possess a drying and dissolving quality, and that they strengthen through their <term>astringency</term> <note>original: *stipticitate*; a quality that contracts body tissues</note>. For this reason, their use for tumors of the liver <note>original: *hepatis*</note> is very frequent among them, because it digests and dissolves without causing any laxity.

They also use fomentations <note>warm, medicinal compresses</note> made from a decoction of the seeds for all pains occupying any part of the body, especially for pleurisy <note>original: *pleuritim*; inflammation of the lung lining</note>, pneumonia <note>original: *peripneumoniam*</note>, and for pain of the liver, colon, and stomach <note>original: *uentriculi*</note>. To those suffering from such pain, they give a pound of the decoction to drink warm, twice a day. Women also find relief for "strangulation of the womb" <note>original: *vteri strangulationibus*; a historical term for various respiratory or nervous symptoms attributed to uterine disorders</note>, gas, and pains through its use, especially when prepared as <term>sitz-baths</term> <note>original: *insessionibus*; medicinal baths where one sits in the liquid up to the hips</note>. These baths are administered even more effectively for hard tumors of the womb and other parts when prepared with fenugreek <note>original: *fœnugreci*</note>, linseed <note>original: *lini*</note>, and chamomile flowers, and they are also used to induce delayed menstruation. They use the decoction most profitably to strengthen the stomach and to open obstructions of the veins.

**GUILIELMUS:** <note>original: *GVIL.*; William Quackelbeen, a physician and colleague of the author</note> Now I understand from you that it is true that among the Arabs, the use of Melilot <note>original: *meliloti*; sweet clover</note> consists of the seeds, whereas the Greeks admit not the seeds, but the flowers into use. Whether this plant is the true Melilot, or rather the one which our people call "urban lotus" <note>original: *lotum vrbanum*</note> and "field garland" <note>original: *sertulam campanam*; a common name for species of Melilotus</note> (which is a large, shrubby plant), we shall explain elsewhere, for this does not seem to be the place for that dispute. However, I will only say this: I am more inclined toward the opinion of the Egyptians.

-> illustration be placed. <-

Having so far identified the plants unique to Egypt known to us, there are several others common to many other places which certainly should not be overlooked before we conclude this discourse. Most important among these is the plant called *Kellù* by the Egyptians—known by some as *Antyllis* <note>original: *Antyllis*; likely a species of the pea family, often used for wound healing</note>—as well as *psyllium* <note>fleawort; its seeds are still used today as a source of fiber</note>, *Melanthium* <note>original: *Melanthium*; also known as black cumin or Nigella sativa</note>, the caper bush, the nettle, sleep-inducing nightshade <note>original: *solanum somniferum*; likely Ashwagandha or a related species of Physalis</note>, coriander, and clove-scented basil <note>original: *ocymus goriophyllatus*; a variety of basil with a distinct clove-like aroma</note>

# ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

white henbane, fleabane, thorn apple <note>original: *Stramonium*; likely *Datura stramonium*, known for its toxic and medicinal properties</note>, Secamone <note>a climbing plant in the dogbane family</note>, and the castor oil plant. Let us, therefore, discuss something regarding these very plants. **GUIL.** I believe you have something worth knowing to say about them; therefore, speak as you please. **ALPIN.** *Kellù*, which our people also call *Kalli* <note>Glasswort or saltwort, plants of the genus *Salsola* or *Salicornia*</note>, is found in Egypt in three distinct species. When all of these are burned, they produce ashes used for glass, soap, and many other things. The first species is the same plant that our people call "jointed Kalli" <note>original: *Kalli geniculatum*</note>. The second is what some call "white Kalli." The third is more unique to the Egyptians; it consists of a few leaves very similar to those of *ajuga* <note>original: *aiugę*; likely Bugleweed</note>, but much longer, and a single stem that is not entirely straight, from which three or four small stalks arise. These grow straight upwards, and each of them at the tip forms a head composed of five or more leaves very similar to *chamaepitys* <note>original: *chamepythios*; Ground-pine</note>, but curved back and drooping in an arc; from the middle of these, many small flowers similar to roses emerge. All these plants strike the tongue with a salty and sharp taste. **GUILAND.** Without a doubt, both of these last plants you described should be classified under the second genus of *Anthyllis* <note>a genus of the pea family</note>. Indeed, Dioscorides <note>an ancient Greek physician and botanist</note>, when teaching about *ajuga*, wrote thus: <margin>,,</margin> An herb creeping on the ground, curved, with leaves like the smaller *sempervivum* <note>original: *sempervivi minoris*; likely a type of Stonecrop or Houseleek</note>, <margin>,,</margin> but much thinner, hairy and fatter, crowded around the branches; in the chapter on *Anthyllis*, he established that it is very close to the leaves of *ajuga*. But please, continue to describe the rest. **ALPIN.** From these three plants, once they have been thoroughly dried in the sun and then burned, ashes are prepared. These are transported from there to Venice for the manufacturing of glass and other products. Many people say that the leaves of these three plants—but especially the second and third types of *Kalli*—when reduced to a powder and taken by mouth, purge phlegm and burnt bile <note>in ancient medicine, "burnt bile" or black bile was one of the four humors thought to cause illness if out of balance</note>. They say the juice expressed from the leaves does the same.

<meta>catchword: KALLI.</meta>

<summary>Alpini describes three varieties of "Kalli" (glasswort) found in Egypt, explaining how they are burned to produce the soda ash essential for the Venetian glass industry and their uses in humoral medicine.</summary>
<keywords>Kalli, glasswort, soda ash, Venice, glassmaking, soap, Dioscorides, ajuga, medicinal purges, Egyptian botany</keywords>

# BOOK

<warning>Significant bleed-through from the reverse side of the page is visible in the upper half of the sheet, partially obscuring the header.</warning>

<image-desc>A detailed botanical woodcut of a Psyllium plant. The illustration shows several long, slender stems emerging from a central base, with narrow, lanceolate leaves that are occasionally recurved. At the terminal ends of the stalks are small, lily-like flowers with pointed petals and prominent stamens. The root system is depicted as a thick, gnarled rhizome situated on a small patch of earth.</image-desc>

**Psyllium** <term>Psyllium: Also known as Fleawort (*Plantago afra*), this plant is famous for its seeds, which produce a soothing gel when soaked.</term> grows there in great abundance. Its seeds are used among the local people, particularly the **mucilage** <term>mucilage: A thick, gluey substance extracted from plants, used in traditional medicine to soothe internal and external irritation.</term> prepared by them with **rose water**, for all **bilious** <note>bilious: Refers to illnesses thought to be caused by an excess of "yellow bile," one of the four humors in ancient medicine, often associated with heat and digestive distress.</note> and burning fevers, and for inflammations of the chest, such as in **pleurisy** <term>pleurisy: Inflammation of the membranes surrounding the lungs, causing sharp chest pain.</term> and **pneumonia**. However, they also extract it with **barley water** <note>original: *aqua hordei*</note> and administer it with **white sugar**. They use this Psyllium mucilage extracted in rose water for bilious **diarrhea** and **dysentery**, as well as for all external inflammations, applying it in the form of a **plaster** <term>plaster: A soft, medicated mass spread on a cloth and applied to the skin.</term> and a **poultice**.

## ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

...in the manner of a plaster, they use the whole plant, both the green leaves and the seeds of the **psyllium** <note>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on *Plantago afra*.</note>. They call this plant *Chetùn*. **Nigella** <term>Nigella: Specifically *Nigella sativa*, or Black Cumin, prized in the Middle East for its medicinal seeds.</term> also grows there, appearing most beautiful when it is seen with its larger flowers and more numerous leaves; its seed is in very frequent use for children to kill worms, and they use the crushed seeds with vinegar in the manner of a plaster for many skin infections. Women who are not **purged** <note>Refers to the regulation of menstrual cycles, a common concern in Renaissance medicine.</note> are greatly helped if they are fumigated with it. They call this plant *suneg*.

**Capers** <term>Capers: The plant *Capparis spinosa*. While we eat the buds today, ancient doctors often used the bitter root bark.</term> grow in Alexandria larger than they are found elsewhere, which they also call *cappar*. From these, they very frequently use the bark of the roots to kill worms, to provoke the menses, and to heal any hard tumors—especially of the **spleen**. For this hardened organ, they administer a decoction by mouth for many days, while externally applying a plaster made from the powder of the bark and vinegar, to which some mix a little honey; they use this remedy for skin spots and infections by smearing the affected areas.

In Alexandria, the **First Nettle** <term>Nettle: The stinging nettle (*Urtica*), which was valued for its "heating" properties in traditional humor-based medicine.</term>, which they call *angiar*, grows most abundantly, and therefore its seeds also have many uses there, especially for **exciting lust** <note>original: *venerem excitandam*—Nettle seeds were a well-known historical aphrodisiac.</note>, particularly in those with "cold" constitutions, if they are drunk with sweet wine. Some compose an **electuary** <term>electuary: A medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup, designed to be dissolved slowly in the mouth.</term> for the same purpose from these seeds, the root of *buzeidam* <note>original: *buzeidam*—likely referring to *behen* or a type of orchid root used as a tonic.</note>, and honey, of which they take half an ounce daily. The seeds have no less use for ailments of the chest and lungs; for they very familiarly use its decoction prepared with honey for coughs, difficulty of breathing, **orthopnea** <term>orthopnea: A severe form of shortness of breath where a person can only breathe comfortably while sitting or standing up.</term>, and **pneumonia** <note>original: *peripneumonia*</note> stirred up by thick and cold humors. They also use the flour with honey in the manner of an **eclegma** <term>eclegma: A syrupy medicine, also called a linctus, licked from a spoon to soothe the throat and lungs.</term> for the same evils. They follow this with its use with vinegar as a plaster for all difficult skin pustules, and for all spots and infections of the skin, as well as for hard tumors, especially those of the **parotid glands** <note>Located near the ears; swelling here often indicated mumps or other infections.</note>. Women have nothing more powerful with which to warm the womb, cleanse it, and heal it from obstructions than the nettle; hence the use of a decoction of the seeds to move the menses and as a fumigant is very frequent among them, especially with **myrrh** added. They use the ash prepared from the seeds for all malignant and cancerous ulcers.

**Sleeping Nightshade** <term>Solanum somniferum: Likely *Withania somnifera* (Ashwagandha) or a related sedative species of the nightshade family.</term> grows most abundantly in the ruins of Alexandria and grows so much that it appears as a large shrub. The inhabitants use the bark of the roots, pulverized to the weight of a **drachma** <term>drachma: An ancient unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams.</term>, with lettuce water or nightshade water, taking it to induce sleep. The bark of this plant, no differently than **methel nut** <note>original: *nux methel*—the hallucinogenic *Datura metel*.</note> or **stramonium** <term>stramonium: Jimsonweed (*Datura stramonium*), a potent and dangerous sedative and deliriant.</term> seeds, is used by many wicked men, so as [to...]

<meta>catchword: ...to do something [aliquid]</meta>

<summary>This page describes the medicinal preparation and application of Nigella, Capers, Nettles, and Sleeping Nightshade in Egypt, emphasizing their use for respiratory issues, skin conditions, and reproductive health. The author notes both the therapeutic benefits and the potential for misuse of sedative plants like Nightshade and Stramonium.</summary>

This page transitions into a debate between the scholars Guilandius and Alpini regarding the safety and medicinal properties of coriander.</meta>

# BOOK ONE.
<meta>catchword: who</meta>

...lest they do some harm secretly while they sleep, they place the bark of this [nightshade] in feasts. They use the crushed leaves in the manner of a plaster for hot inflammations, and by smearing the juice on the forehead, temples, and other parts of the body where the pulses are felt—or by applying those same leaves crushed as a plaster—they use it most familiarly to induce sleep. It is used no less for burns. The **Metel Nut** <term>Metel Nut: *Datura metel*, a poisonous and hallucinogenic plant in the nightshade family, often called "Devil's Trumpet."</term> also grows there, though not as abundantly as the aforementioned nightshade. It is the true *stramonium* <note>original: *stramonium*</note> of Dioscorides. They say that its uses are clearly the same as those we mentioned for the sleep-inducing nightshade. There is also **White Henbane** <note>original: *hyoscyamus albus*</note>, which differs greatly from the common form; it grows in Cairo near the pyramids, with leaves much larger, wider, thicker, and whiter than the common variety. They use this in no way differently than *stramonium*.

Everywhere in the gardens, **Coriander** <term>Coriander: *Coriandrum sativum*. While Europeans often used only the seeds, Alpini notes the Egyptians used the fresh greens (cilantro).</term> grows most abundantly, which they all call *Cushbarà*. The use of the green herb in food is very common among all Egyptians; indeed, they prepare no dish without coriander leaves, even though it smells unpleasant.

**G V I L .** What is this I hear? Are they not greatly harmed by eating it, and do they not fall into a **frenzy** <term>Frenzy: *Phrenitis*, an ancient medical term for a delirium or mental disturbance thought to be caused by inflammation of the brain.</term> from its use? This is what Dioscorides, Avicenna, and many others have recorded.

**A L P I N .** Ridiculous. Who has ever seen anyone harmed by coriander (which is in the most common use among both the healthy and the sick), or seen their senses disturbed, unless they used it immoderately? Why do you wonder? It is as if you do not hold the same opinion as **Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamon (2nd century CE), whose medical theories dominated European and Islamic medicine for over a millennium.</note>, who established that pale coriander is **resolving** <term>Resolving: *Resolvens*, a medicinal action intended to disperse "bad humors" or reduce swellings.</term> and moderately astringent. Does not Dioscorides demonstrate that he acknowledges this power when he says that **scrofulous tumors** <term>Scrofulous tumors: *Chaeredas*, referring to swollen lymph nodes in the neck, often a symptom of tuberculosis.</term> are healed with coriander and honey? For "cold" tumors are never dispersed or cured by "cold" remedies, since the other medicines recorded by Dioscorides for healing hard tumors and scrofula were of a "hot" nature. Nor does **Avicenna** <note>The Latinized name for Ibn Sina (980–1037), the Persian "Prince of Physicians."</note> seem to have been silent on this, when he said that scrofula is healed by coriander, either from its specific property for healing that tumor, or from the thinness of its substance, by which it penetrates through the passages of the swollen part. But who is ignorant that nothing is resolved by what is "cold"—even if much penetration of the swollen part occurs—but rather that all resolving agents are "hot"?

**G V I L A N D .** You have rightly defended the true opinion. Who could now deny it, knowing from you that the Egyptians consume the green herb of coriander in food without any harm? Much less should the use of dried seeds thereafter be feared. From this, it will be clearly established that the **Arabs** <note>Refers to the Arabic medical commentators like Avicenna or Rhazes.</note> erred not a little...

<summary>This page describes Egyptian varieties of nightshade, henbane, and coriander. It features a debate where Alpini refutes the ancient claim that coriander causes madness, arguing instead for its safety and medicinal utility in treating tumors.</summary>
<keywords>Datura metel, Henbane, Coriander, Cairo, Egyptian medicine, Dioscorides, Galen, Avicenna, scrofula, humoral theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a dialogue regarding the medicinal properties and safety of common Egyptian plants, specifically addressing whether plants considered toxic by ancient authorities like Dioscorides are used safely in Egypt.</meta>

# BOOK ONE.
<meta>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on coriander.</meta>

...who attributed to coriander a cold narcotic power, inducing numbness, causing a disturbance of the senses, and even death. You have also discovered the same regarding **Psyllium** <term>Psyllium: Also known as Fleawort (*Plantago psyllium*), its seeds are high in fiber and contain a mucilage used to soothe the digestive tract.</term> among them. While psyllium was regarded as a poison by Dioscorides and others, it is frequently given without harm to those with fevers, often providing no small amount of seed-mucilage to the sick. They eat the herb boiled in water. But they affirm that the seeds are far more effective for strengthening the stomach; indeed, through constant use of the seeds, they greatly strengthen weak stomachs, cherish and increase its heat, and resolve and disperse flatulence. Consequently, they follow the use of the seeds in colicky pain and other conditions caused by flatulent spirits, using them either reduced to a powder or in a decoction prepared from them. By this, they also counter coldness and dampness of the uterus, especially for immoderate menstrual flow and to firm up gonorrhea <note>In early modern medicine, "gonorrhea" referred broadly to any excessive bodily discharge or "running of seed," not strictly the modern infection.</note>, because it also possesses an astringent quality. For this reason, I believe they also use the plant in the manner of a plaster for those with gout and other joint pains, because it strengthens those parts through astringency—preventing the parts from receiving humors—and it also resolves, disperses, or draws out what is contained within.

**Basil** <term>Basil: *Ocimum basilicum*. The variety described here is notably taller and more fragrant than European varieties.</term> grows most abundantly there in the gardens to a height of three cubits or more, with leaves longer and thinner than our local ones, suffused with a red color, and breathing out an extraordinary fragrance. For in the sweetness of its scent, it seems deservedly preferable to all our varieties, which is why they have called this plant *Rihau* <note>original: *Rihau*, likely a Latinization of the Arabic *Rayhan*, meaning "fragrant herb" or specifically Basil</note>, which is the same as if you said "fragrance." Thus, because of its exceptional pleasantness, this basil is called "Fragrance" par excellence by them. They use this plant for the sake of its scent, but it has few medicinal uses among them, having been instructed by many others.

There also grows spontaneously there a certain creeping plant, climbing higher and entwining itself around other trees, with hard, long leaves very close to those of **Ethiopian Seseli** <term>Ethiopian Seseli: A plant mentioned by ancient botanists, likely an aromatic member of the Umbelliferae (carrot) family.</term>, but larger. It bears white flowers similar to those of the **Styrax** tree, from which are produced pods, long and thick, such as are seen in the **Oleander** <note>original: *ulcododendro*, likely a corruption of *nerio-rhododendron* or similar, referring to silk-pods</note>, full of white down and small seeds. From this plant flows a yellow, burning juice which the Egyptians say, when dried, powerfully purges thin humors. They affirm this to be a species of **Scammony** <term>Scammony: *Convolvulus scammonia*, a plant known since antiquity for its potent purgative properties.</term>, which they call *sechamonè* <note>original: *sechamonè*, from the Arabic *saqmonia*</note>, though I know of no use for it in medicine among them.

**G V I L .** Perhaps we could affirm this plant to be the **Dogbane** <term>Dogbane: *Apocynum*, described by Dioscorides as a plant whose juice or leaves were used to kill wild animals, but also studied for its medicinal potential.</term> of Dioscorides. Since its characteristics are seen to agree with the marks handed down by Dioscorides, and perhaps it should be thought to be the one which someone said came from Syria...

<meta>catchword: plocam</meta>

# BOOK ONE.

...reported that it was called **plocam** <note>original: "plocam uocatam". This likely refers to a specific variety or local name for a plant mentioned by ancient authorities like Pliny.</note>. However, the true **Apocynum** <term>Apocynum: Commonly known as Dogbane, this genus of plants is famous for being toxic to dogs and for its milky, latex-like sap.</term> is now recognized in Italy.

# SECAMONE
<term>Secamone: A genus of climbing shrubs in the milkweed family. In the 16th century, botanists were keenly interested in identifying these plants due to their relationship to potent purgatives like Scammony.</term>

<meta>This page follows the discussion of Secamone and other Egyptian flora.
