# ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

The common people eat the sprouts of **fenugreek** <term>Fenugreek: *Trigonella foenum-graecum*, a plant used both as a herb and a spice. In this period, its sprouts were often consumed for health benefits.</term> as soon as they break forth from the seeds. Women in particular, desiring to become **plump** <note>In the 16th century, being "fleshy" or plump was considered a sign of beauty and high social status for women, and fenugreek was believed to aid in weight gain.</note>, consume them; they carry many lumps of earth full of seeds with recently produced sprouts through the city to be sold. Many other plants grow there which, because they are common to many other places, we shall not discuss further. But now we must conclude our discourse on the medicine used among those peoples and on the plants growing in those places, as nothing further remains to be said.

---

### *On plants observed during the journey.* Chapter XLIII.

## GUILANDINUS
<note>Melchior Guilandinus (c. 1520–1589) was a Prussian-born botanist and physician who served as the director of the botanical garden in Padua. He acts as the interlocutor in this dialogue with Alpinus.</note>

I give you many thanks for undertaking such a great labor for my sake until now. Nevertheless, lest we omit anything I judge useful to this history, I would wish—before we bring this discourse to an absolute end—that you would say something also about the plants you observed in various places while sailing on your journey to Egypt. 

## ALPINUS
<note>Prospero Alpini (1553–1617) was a Venetian physician and botanist who spent several years in Egypt. This text records his observations.</note>

I do not think you are unaware that, having heard of our journey, very little time was granted to me during that voyage to track down the plants of those places. However, I will not refuse even now to report those which I saw then, although I do not think them worthy enough for your most learned ears. I remember that I inspected a **rhubarb** <term>Rhubarb: At this time, true medicinal rhubarb was a highly prized and somewhat mysterious import from the East; Alpinus is trying to identify the specific species.</term> plant on the island of **Corfu** <note>original: "Cocyra", an old name for Corfu.</note>, in a certain garden of that ancient fortress. Someone had brought it there by ship from Syria, and he also affirmed that it had once been brought to Tripoli by a Persian man. That plant appeared clearly very close to the **dock** <term>Rumex: A genus of green leafy plants including sorrels.</term> in its leaves, which were nevertheless broader, thicker, and covered with a certain white fuzz. On that same island, I found very fragrant long roots of **Cyperus** <term>Cyperus: A large genus of sedges, some of which (like *Cyperus longus*) were prized for their aromatic roots used in perfumes and medicine.</term> and **Tree Germander** <note>original: "Chamedrim arboream".</note> living near **Butrint** <note>A site in modern-day Albania, across from Corfu.</note> at a height of nearly two cubits, which had leaves twice or thrice the size of the Italian varieties. I inspected the **wood sorrel** <note>original: "oxalidem".</note> of **Zakynthos** <note>An Ionian island.</note>, which the pharmacists of that island used for medicine; it was quite different from all our wood sorrels, as the herb itself is slender, with small, thick, oblong leaves rivalling the leaves of the **marigold** <note>original: "calthae".</note>, and it strikes the palate with a sour flavor no less than our own. There also grows the "second acacia of **Mattioli**" <note>Referring to Pietro Andrea Mattioli, the most famous 16th-century commentator on Dioscorides. Botanists often named plants based on his descriptions.</note>, which I saw in bloom...

<meta>The catchword "menfe" indicates the text continues on the next page with a mention of the month (mense) in which the plant was observed blooming.</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the description of Egyptian plants with a note on fenugreek and begins a new chapter where Alpinus describes botanical finds from his travels, including rhubarb in Corfu and wood sorrel in Zakynthos.</summary>

<keywords>fenugreek, weight gain, Prospero Alpini, Melchior Guilandinus, Corfu, rhubarb, Butrint, Tree Germander, Zakynthos, wood sorrel, Mattioli</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Prospero Alpini and Melchior Guilandino. Alpini is recounting his botanical observations made on various Mediterranean islands while traveling to Egypt. The previous page established that Guilandino requested Alpini to share these findings before concluding their discussion on Egyptian medicine.</meta>

In the month of February, I examined a plant with leaves and flowers very close to those of the smaller **anagyris** <term>Anagyris: likely *Anagyris foetida*, or stinking bean-trefoil, a Mediterranean shrub with a distinct, often unpleasant smell.</term>. When tasted, these parts strike the palate with an astringent flavor, and the plant is covered with a few soft thorns. I do not believe it to be the true **acacia** <term>Acacia: refers here to the medicinal extract or the plant *Acacia nilotica* described by ancient authorities.</term> which **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician and botanist whose *De Materia Medica* was the standard pharmacological text for 1,500 years.</note> reported as growing in **Cappadocia and Pontus** <note>Regions in modern-day Turkey.</note>, because it seems to bear very little resemblance to the primary acacia, or Egyptian thorn. I recognized another thing on that island: that **rough bindweed** <term>Rough bindweed: *Smilax aspera*, a climbing woody vine common in the Mediterranean.</term> is the true **sarsaparilla** <term>Sarsaparilla: A medicinal root used extensively in the 16th century to treat skin diseases and syphilis.</term> which physicians use to cure the **French disease** <note>The "French disease" (*lues gallica*) was the common name for syphilis in this period.</note>. 

**GUILANDINO.** I know that this plant also grows quite abundantly in many places in Italy; therefore, what you learned there is not new to me. 

**ALPINUS.** You speak the truth, for I had encountered and observed it elsewhere in many other places, but I was always hesitant and doubtful as to whether it was truly sarsaparilla. However, having seen it on this island, I recognized more clearly that it is indeed the true version. 

**GUILANDINO.** For what reason, when you saw that same plant in various parts of Italy, were you not of the same opinion—namely, that it was sarsaparilla? 

**ALPINUS.** Because all the rough bindweeds I had seen until then had very thin, short, and hair-like roots. But the ones I saw on the island of **Zakynthos** <note>Zakynthos (Zante) is an Ionian island in Greece where Alpini stayed during his voyage.</note> had long, thick roots, entirely similar to the roots of sarsaparilla imported from **Spain** <note>Sarsaparilla was frequently imported by the Spanish from the New World, and these "Spanish roots" were the medical standard.</note>. 

I will now explain to you how I recognized them. In order to escape the boredom of a long delay, I happened by chance to be scrambling up a hill on that island. In a certain valley, I cast my eyes upon a hedge woven with **mastic trees** <term>Mastic tree: *Pistacia lentiscus*, an evergreen shrub famous for its resin.</term> and many rough bindweeds. From all their roots, the earth had been so washed away by the force of a torrential flood that the roots of those plants weaving the hedge were seen naked—just like the veins, arteries, and nerves of a human body when separated from the flesh. The rough bindweeds, which truly imitated long serpents, were coiled around other roots and presented themselves to me with a marvelous length and thickness. I saw some of them that were two **cubits** <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger (about 18–20 inches).</note> or more in length, and of the same thickness as common sarsaparilla roots; from this, I immediately judged them to be the roots of sarsaparilla. 

While I was considering these things along with others who were there with me, a most opportune sign occurred. For I asked a passing Greek man, a resident and farmer of that place, what that plant was called there. He replied that it was named ***smilachia*** <note>original: "smilachiam", a Latinized form of the Greek name for the plant.</note> and that it was used among them for curing the French disease. From these words, and considering it more carefully, I saw its roots were very long and thick, with the same substance...

<summary>Alpini discusses his discovery on the island of Zakynthos that the common Mediterranean rough bindweed (Smilax aspera) is identical to the medicinal sarsaparilla used to treat syphilis. He describes a vivid scene where a flood exposed the plant's roots, allowing him to confirm their similarity to imported Spanish specimens.</summary>
<keywords>Prospero Alpini, Melchior Guilandino, sarsaparilla, Smilax aspera, rough bindweed, syphilis, Zakynthos, botanical identification, Dioscorides, mastic tree</keywords>

### ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

...possessing [certain qualities], and offering no flavor, much like the roots of **sarsaparilla** <term>Sarsaparilla: In the 16th century, the roots of *Smilax* species were highly valued as a "wonder drug" imported from the New World to treat syphilis.</term>. From this I immediately concluded that **rough bindweed** <term>Rough bindweed: *Smilax aspera*, a common prickly climbing vine in the Mediterranean.</term> is the true sarsaparilla. A pharmacist of that place affirmed to us that he had made a great deal of profit from these roots, because he had often gathered many bundles of them there and sent them to other locations, where he very frequently sold them as true sarsaparilla roots. He conducted this trade in secret, so that others would not recognize the plant. I should add that in **Cairo**, in bundles of sarsaparilla imported from Spain, I saw fragments of the knotty or jointed roots of rough bindweed from which the medicinal roots we use actually grow. These were found alongside the leaves of the same plant and its thorny branches; they were so obvious that no one who saw them could have denied that those bundles of sarsaparilla were selected from rough bindweed, or that this plant is the same as sarsaparilla.

**GUILANDINO.** It has pleased me greatly that you have correctly recognized the truth regarding rough bindweed. I want you to be persuaded that not only I, but many others, have shared this opinion—most notably **Gabriel Fallopius** <note>Gabriel Fallopius (1523–1562) was one of the most important anatomists and physicians of the 16th century, famous today for describing the Fallopian tubes.</note>, a man most learned in the matter of herbs. In his short book on the **French disease** <note>A common historical name for syphilis.</note>, in the chapter on sarsaparilla, he set forth these words:

> "Formerly I believed it was the root of the **dwarf elder** <term>Dwarf elder: *Sambucus ebulus*, a herbaceous species of elderberry.</term>, and I persisted in that opinion for many days, until a Spaniard brought a whole plant to the Duke of Florence; from this my error became clear, and I saw that it was the root of rough bindweed, of which **Dioscorides** <note>The ancient Greek author of the foundational pharmacological text *De Materia Medica*.</note> made mention in his fourth book, as did other ancient writers. This I have further confirmed through experience: for when I saw rough bindweed on Mount Saint Julian in **Pisa**, I arranged to have it dug up and have always used it in place of sarsaparilla. Throughout the two years I was there, I always freed many people from the French plague with happy success. This opinion is also confirmed by the physician **Amatus Lusitanus** <note>Amatus Lusitanus (1511–1568) was a famous Portuguese Jewish physician who traveled throughout Europe and wrote influential medical commentaries.</note>, who, though a Hebrew, is not to be despised, in the fifth book of his *Centuries*."

Therefore, you ought not to be carried away by your discovery, since many others knew of it before you. But how can we assert that the bindweed of that island demonstrates the true form of sarsaparilla, while the bindweed found in parts of Italy does not? I believe this happens due to the diversity of the soil in which the plant creeps. Indeed, in hard, dry, and rough soil, we will see that the rough bindweed we dig up has smaller and slenderer roots than sarsaparilla; but in the softest soil, where they can easily spread, the roots are longer, thicker, and clearly very close to sarsaparilla. Indeed, that bindweed plant which I inspected on that island when I was staying in those parts grew in the softest, richest...

<sig>ac</sig>

<summary>Alpini and Guilandino discuss the botanical identity of sarsaparilla, concluding it is the same as the common Mediterranean rough bindweed (Smilax aspera). They cite the authority of Gabriel Fallopius and Amatus Lusitanus, noting that soil quality accounts for the difference in root size between local and imported specimens.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Prospero Alpini’s argument regarding the botanical identity of sarsaparilla. He is explaining how environmental factors like soil density change the appearance of roots, leading other physicians to wrongly believe that the Mediterranean rough bindweed and the imported sarsaparilla are different species.</meta>

# BOOK.

...and was nourished by moisture, from which it is no wonder if it was found with long and thick roots. But in dry and hard soil, with the hardness of the earth resisting, the roots are unable to creep and pass freely; thus **rough bindweed** <term>Rough bindweed: *Smilax aspera*, a prickly climbing vine common in the Mediterranean, which Alpini argues is the same as the medicinal sarsaparilla.</term> will appear with shorter and thinner roots. Such roots were observed by me in the rocky and harder places of the islands of **Zakynthos**, **Corfu**, and **Crete**. I add that many are deceived in recognizing **sarsaparilla** <note>original: *salsa parilla*</note> from rough bindweed, because they do not notice or observe all the roots of both the bindweed and the sarsaparilla.

For indeed, in rough bindweed, two distinct types of roots are seen. Specifically, it first has roots proceeding from the stem that are white, round, and knotty; they are the thickness of a finger and very similar to small reeds <note>original: *cannulis*; Alpini is describing the hollow-looking but solid structural part of the root system.</note>. These roots certainly do not grow very long. From their joints or nodes, other roots set out and spring forth that are plainly different from the first. In hard soil, these are seen as small, short, and thin, no different than certain hair-like fibers. But in soft soil, they are found long and thick, just as they are in sarsaparilla. 

From this, the difference in the roots of bindweed becomes apparent, which is likewise easily observed and found in the sarsaparilla plant imported from **Spain**. So much so that each of these plants has primary roots proceeding immediately from the stem that are white, marked by nodes at equal intervals, and very close to reeds—though not hollow inside, but full and packed, about the thickness of a pinky finger—and secondary roots springing and exiting from these nodes like filaments, which are long, more slender than the primary ones, uniform, and lacking nodes or joints. 

Those who therefore deny that rough bindweed is the true sarsaparilla say that the roots of both plants are different from each other in form and also in size. In form indeed, because they only look at the primary knotty, white, short, thick roots of bindweed and compare them to the long, slender, non-knotty, uniform secondary roots. Thus they create a most obvious distinction between them. They also confirm this by the fact that the first are thick and short, while these others are slender and long. 

These people are indeed mistaken, as they do not compare the roots with one another as would be proper. For if they had compared the slender, long roots of sarsaparilla—which are the secondary roots—not with the thick, knotty, white, short primary roots of rough bindweed, but with the secondary ones springing from the nodes of the primary ones, they would without doubt have found no difference, except this: that the secondary roots of bindweed are observed to be shorter and more slender than the roots of sarsaparilla due to the location. This difference is purely by reason of the softness or hardness of the soil, or the diversity of the same, and perhaps also

even <note>The catchword "etiam" (even/also) at the bottom of the page indicates the first word of the next page.</note>

<summary>Prospero Alpini explains that rough bindweed and sarsaparilla are the same plant, arguing that differences in their root size and shape are caused by soil conditions rather than species variation. He details the two-part root system of Smilax aspera to show how previous botanists were misled by improper comparisons.</summary>

...we are persuaded that this also occurs in a warmer climate. For in hard soil and in a colder climate, the roots do not grow and thicken in the same way. 

**ALPINI.** You have touched upon the truth, and all those matters you brought forward regarding the roots of the bindweed <term>bindweed: *Smilax aspera*, a climbing woody vine. Alpini is arguing that various medicinal roots are actually just different environmental expressions of this one plant.</term>, I have diligently observed and recognized with my own eyes on the island of Zakynthos <note>Zakynthos (Zante) is a Greek island in the Ionian Sea where Alpini studied the local flora.</note>. This truth will shine forth quite clearly from the illustration <term>ichon: from the Greek *eikon*, meaning a botanical illustration or image drawn from life.</term> of the plant drawn from life. In it, both the first roots—knotty, thick, and short—and the second ones—born from the nodes of the first, long, slender, and uniform without joints—can be most clearly seen. 

**GUILANDINO.** From seeing this illustration of the bindweed, and from what you have said about it so far, it is easily recognized that the bindweed which you reported growing on the island of Zakynthos is much

# ON THE PLANTS OF EGYPT

<meta>catchword: lon-</meta>

Continue now to finish the narration and history of the remaining plants seen by you, since no doubt is left to us regarding this plant <note>referring to the *Smilax aspera* discussed previously</note>, so that we may believe it to be the true sarsaparilla <note>original: *falsamperillam*, a variant name for sarsaparilla or a related Mediterranean substitute.</note>.

**ALPINI.** On the island of Crete, among other plants there, I came across Thyme, Savory, Dodder-of-thyme, Marum, Ladanum, Aspalathus, and Storax. I saw both kinds of Thyme, namely the large and the small. The large is a plant with long, flexible branches with leaves very close to Savory, but somewhat rougher, broader, and greener, with a very sharp and fragrant taste: many falsely state this plant to be the *tragoriganum* <term>tragoriganum: "Goat-oregano," a specific aromatic herb described by the ancient physician Dioscorides.</term> of Dioscorides. The second Thyme, with smaller, thinner, and whiter leaves, is very well known throughout all Italy.

*Thymbra* <term>Thymbra: Often identified as Satureja thymbra, or Pink Savory.</term> is a plant growing very abundantly on the island of Crete, similar to the larger Thyme, with hard woody branches and leaves broader and softer than Thyme, of a grassy color, not growing in an orderly fashion as some have reported; nor do other small leaves grow between these. From this, it is conjectured that the plant commonly sketched as the "second Savory" is not the true one, which I saw there growing with Dodder-of-thyme, being very different.

Indeed, in a place on that island called Fraschia <note>A coastal area near Heraklion, Crete.</note>, I found Dodder-of-thyme <term>Dodder-of-thyme: *Epithymum*, a parasitic, leafless vine that wraps around host plants.</term> entwined around Thyme, Savory, and other plants, in the manner of *cuscuta* <term>cuscuta: The genus name for dodder; Alpini is distinguishing between the parasite and the host.</term>. From this, I recognized it to be false that there are two species of Dodder-of-thyme—namely, one being the flower of thyme, and the second being this hair-like herb emulating *cuscuta*. For everyone affirms that Dodder-of-thyme purges black bile <note>In the medical theory of the time, "black bile" was one of the four humors; its excess was thought to cause melancholy.</note>, but no one would say that the flowers of thyme perform this.

Some of the Cretans say that Dodder-of-thyme and Dodder-of-savory purge blood burned by heat <note>Referring to "adust" or scorched humors, a condition thought to cause inflammation and fever.</note>, and many different diseases are cured by the use of that hair-like herb found upon various plants. Indeed, they affirm that when it is collected entwined with Oregano, it is conducive to purging all diseases which Oregano itself is known to help when used alone. And thus, when found and gathered from other plants, it has the power of purging the humors from which those diseases arise that these plants are accustomed to help and assist by their medicinal faculty.

I saw Marum among some Cretans, clearly similar to our Marjoram <term>Marjoram: *Amaracus* in Latin; Alpini is comparing the Cretan *Marum* (likely *Teucrium marum* or Herb-Mastich) to common sweet marjoram.</term>. Although it consists of more slender, smaller, and rougher leaves than Marjoram, it is much more fragrant and superior to our Marjoram; I judge this plant should be called "wild marjoram" and not Marum.

I also observed the plant from which the Cretans collect Ladanum <term>Ladanum: A sticky resin, also called labdanum, used in perfumes and medicine.</term> to be clearly different from that which almost everyone has reported until now. Indeed, this is very similar to the female Cistus <term>Cistus: The Rockrose genus. Ladanum is typically harvested from *Cistus ladanifer* or *Cistus creticus*.</term>, though with the leaves of the Cistus...

<summary>Prospero Alpini describes his botanical observations on the island of Crete, identifying various species of Thyme, Savory, and Marum. He specifically clarifies the nature of Epithymum (dodder) as a parasitic herb rather than a flower of the host plant, noting its local medicinal use in purging humors.</summary>

<keywords>Crete, Thyme, Savory (Thymbra), Dodder-of-thyme (Epithymum), Marum, Ladanum, Cistus, Dioscorides, medicinal herbs, black bile, botanical classification</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical dialogue between the physician Prospero Alpini and the scholar Melchior Guilandinus. On the previous page, Alpini was describing the varieties of Thyme and Savory found on the island of Crete before moving into the description of the rockrose family.</meta>

### BOOK.

...provided with longer leaves, and very close to Sage. It bears flowers entirely similar to wild roses, which are seen to be suffused with a rosy color, indeed like common roses. This plant seems to differ from the female rockrose <note>original: *cysto fœmina*</note> only in the size of the flowers. Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist known for his commentaries on Dioscorides.</note> was deceived by Dioscorides saying that ladanum <note>A sticky resin obtained from rockrose shrubs, used in perfumes and medicine.</note> was a type of rockrose filled with longer and blacker leaves; which features are indeed all observed in the rockrose from which the Cretans collect ladanum. Since this plant is entirely similar to the female rockrose, it is seen with the same leaves and flowers, even if they are larger.

As for Aspalathus <term>Aspalathus: A thorny Mediterranean shrub, often identified as Calicotome villosa or a related species, valued for its fragrant flowers.</term>, as has also been said elsewhere, I often inspected it growing and flowering in great abundance on a certain hill near the port of Fraschia <note>A coastal location in Crete.</note>. The extraordinary sweetness of the scent brought to me from the flowers of this plant was the reason I recognized it. This plant is a shrub with dense branches, horrific with hard white thorns, with the bark of the trunk being white, rising high to a height of three cubits <note>A cubit is roughly 18 inches, so about 4.5 feet.</note> and more. It has many small, saffron-colored flowers similar to broom <note>original: *spartij*</note>, but highly fragrant. At that time, I observed the roots of this shrub to be hard, long, and thick, with a yellow color and a highly fragrant bark. My pharmacist gathered many of them, and by my order brought them to Cairo for medical use.

I also inspected several storax <note>The *Styrax officinalis* tree, which produces a fragrant resin.</note> trees there, growing near the stream of that place, many of which I brought with me into Egypt. Because of the climate in that location, they did not survive. But why should I tell you more about the plants of that island, which are better known to you than to me? For what part of Greece is there now which you have not frequently traveled through in your desire for plants? Let these things we have said about the plants of Egypt suffice. Now that these matters are finished, we must move on to others, and perhaps to the study of certain ambiguous "simples" <term>simples: *medicamentis simplicibus*, or "simple medicines," which are medicinal herbs or substances used on their own rather than in a complex mixture.</term> which I have often desired to identify. For a long time, I have greatly desired that an absolute discussion be held among us concerning all the simple medicines used by the ancient physicians, which until now are held as doubtful among most of our doctors.

**GUILANDINUS.** I see now that the history of the medicine currently used by the Egyptians, as well as those plants growing in those places, has reached its end. Regarding this matter, and for that labor undertaken for my sake, I shall owe you very much as long as I live. Most especially now, thanks must be given to you by me, and held perpetually. Indeed, the discussion proposed by you regarding the nature of simple medicines—about which our doctors have some doubt—truly seems most worthy to be weighed by us. However, this must be done for the present...

<sig>Q</sig>

...I do not think so: for with the sun now descending toward the Antipodes <note>original: *Antipodes*. In this context, it refers to the opposite side of the earth, signifying that evening is approaching.</note>, little of this day remains for us. For this reason, this conversation must be postponed to another time more suitable for us. In the meantime, we shall relax a little from our labor, granting some rest and leisure to our spirits. As soon as a time seems opportune to you for that discussion, you shall return to me, so that we may compose something worthy concerning this matter. **ALPINUS.** I shall do so; but before night closes in, it is more advisable that I return home. Farewell, and hold me in your affection, as you are accustomed to do. **GUILANDINUS.** <note>Melchior Guilandinus (c. 1520–1589) was a Prussian-born physician and botanist who served as the director of the botanical garden in Padua.</note>

# AT VENICE, 1592.

-> Published by Francesco de' Franceschi of Siena. <note>Francesco de' Franceschi (active 1558–1599) was a prominent Venetian printer known for high-quality academic and scientific works.</note> <-

<summary>This is the title page for Prospero Alpini's 1592 treatise on the balsam tree, a plant highly prized in ancient medicine for its aromatic resin. The work is dedicated to the administrators of the University of Padua and aims to provide definitive botanical identifications that had eluded previous generations of doctors.</summary>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>continuity: This page follows the title page of Prospero Alpini’s treatise on balsam. It begins the formal dedication to the high-ranking Venetian officials who oversaw the University of Padua, where Alpini conducted his research. MOST illustrious Senators, we read that the balsam plant was of such great renown among both ancient and more recent physicians that its oil, or juice (which the Greeks call <term>opobalsam</term> <note>From the Greek *opos* (sap) and *balsamon*. It refers to the pure resin exuded from the balsam tree.</note>), was regarded and celebrated throughout the whole world as a kind of divine aid. Even common people are accustomed to calling anything remarkably beneficial for health a "balsam." And indeed, it has been recorded that nothing more excellent or worthy can be found in the entire forest <note>The "forest of simple medicines" is a metaphorical term for the vast array of natural, uncompounded medicinal ingredients—known as "simples"—available to physicians.</note> of simple medicines; and by Hercules, not without reason. <meta>continuity: This page continues the dedicatory epistle from Prospero Alpini to the Venetian Senators. The final word of the previous page was "common," which in the original Latin was "re-," completing the word "rebus" (matters/things) on this page.</meta>

*...among those versed in the art of healing, who is unaware of its power to heal ulcers and wounds? How much it resists lethal poisons, the stings of scorpions, and the bites of vipers? To whom is the nobility of this juice not of miraculous efficacy, hidden in its ability to ward off the plague and pestilential fevers? Since it is established by the testimony of many that the bodies of kings were once preserved from decay by it. Have not putrid fevers often been taken away by this protection? What? Has not experience taught that the human body is aided by balsam from the skin all the way to the internal organs? Women make their faces most beautiful by anointing themselves with it, and drive away wrinkles that have appeared on the skin. And from this, it is common knowledge that youth is preserved for a long time. And to sum up the matter briefly and touch upon it: it greatly aids the head, stomach, liver, spleen, mesentery, <note>The mesentery is the fold of membrane that attaches the intestines to the abdominal wall.</note> lungs, kidneys, bladder, womb, spine, nerves, muscles, and all the joints. Indeed, it wipes away the dimness that has appeared in the eyes and restores impaired or lost vision; it heals aching ears and the ringing within them. Furthermore, it is wonderfully beneficial for those with convulsions, epilepsy, vertigo, paralysis, <note>original: "resolutis," referring to those with "loosened" or palsied limbs.</note> and tremors; it also assists with difficulty breathing, coughs, orthopnea, <note>A condition where one can only breathe comfortably when standing or sitting upright.</note> coldness of the lungs, discharges, and pains. It greatly warms, increases, and strengthens the heat of the stomach, and is therefore an immediate aid for its indigestions and for gas stirred up by diminished heat. It removes obstructions of the mesenteric veins from the liver and spleen, and cures the royal disease. <note>original: "regium morbum." A historical term for jaundice, so named because it was supposedly cured by the touch of a king, or perhaps because of the "golden" color of the skin.</note> It provokes urine that is often blocked, and powerfully breaks stones, wiping them from the kidneys and expelling them through the urine. It regards the womb more than other parts of the body, and most excellently assists all the evils arising there from coldness; it moves the menses, and brings out the birth and the afterbirth; <note>original: "secundas," referring to the placenta.</note> it safely cures suffocations <note>referring to "suffocation of the womb," a historical diagnosis for various physical and emotional symptoms in women.</note> and gas. And what is no less desired and sought after by many, it makes a woman fertile who has become sterile for those same reasons. And since such extraordinary gifts of balsam exist, recorded long ago by the most famous ancient physicians, it is no wonder that...*

matters, that this tree was dear to the Roman Emperors, the Vespasians <note>Referring to the Emperors Vespasian and his son Titus, who famously brought balsam plants to Rome after the Jewish War</note>, and that on its account they once fought against the Jews. It is clearly evident to everyone that the juice, fruit, and wood of this tree have had the greatest use in medicine. For it completes the composition of many antidotes, especially **Theriac** <note>A famous ancient "universal" antidote and panacea composed of dozens of ingredients</note> and **Mithridatium** <note>An ancient complex antidote named after King Mithridates VI of Pontus</note>, which are now prepared by our physicians without it, not without disgrace to the medical art.

Since they are ignorant that these things can sometimes be found here, they most boldly deny that the true juice, fruit, and wood of this tree, which are often offered to them, are authentic. Therefore, I cannot help but deeply lament, along with the physicians of our time, the present calamity concerning balsam; for even though the juice, fruit, and wood of this plant are now truly imported from Egypt and Syria into many parts of Italy, our physicians nevertheless refuse to recognize them. Nay, most of them even affirm that in this day and age the true balsam plant is found nowhere in the world, and that its juice, fruit, and twigs—which the Greeks call <term>opobalsamum</term> <note>the sap or juice</note>, <term>carpobalsamum</term> <note>the fruit or seed</note>, and <term>xylobalsamum</term> <note>the wood or twigs</note>—are by no means imported to us in their true form.

And some of these men assert that it is simply impossible for enough true *opobalsamum* to be found—of which no small quantity is often circulated in these parts—given that Egypt and Syria are now, by universal consensus, devoid of balsam plants. They argue that even in ancient centuries, when all of Egypt was filled with these same plants, only a very modest amount of that liquid could be obtained. Others, knowing that it is brought here from **Arabia Felix** <note>Latin: "Happy Arabia"; the fertile southern region of the Arabian Peninsula, primarily modern Yemen</note>, deny that it is genuine *opobalsamum*, asserting that only Egypt and Syria once possessed it, and that botanical writers have never recorded its discovery elsewhere. 

Persuaded by these reasons, they are convinced that genuine *opobalsamum* is not to be found in these times. Thus, if it is sometimes offered to them by people coming from Egypt or Syria, they mock them, deny it is authentic, and reject it; and they refuse to use it in any way when preparing Theriac, Mithridatium, and other most noble compounds. In my opinion, I judge this conduct of theirs to be most wretched, and not without great disgrace to our profession; and since to us legit-

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the authenticity of Balsam imported from the Levant. The word "mum" at the start completes "opobalsamum" from the previous page.</meta>

[opobalsam]um, the juice of the tree which was called balsam by the ancient herbalists <note>The author refers to the "priscis herbarijs," the botanists of antiquity such as Dioscorides and Galen</note>. Since I know for certain that the wood and the fruit are imported from many places, I could not help but wonder greatly at the ignorance and extreme error of those [physicians], and to refute them in this error with the arguments I have brought forward. This is especially true since I have learned that all the Egyptian, Arabian, and Syrian physicians, by the unanimous consensus of everyone and without any contradiction or hesitation, recommend and seize upon the same juice, the same fruits, and the same wood for compounding **Theriac** <note>A complex ancient remedy used as an antidote for poisons and a general cure-all</note>, **Mithridatium** <note>A famous semi-mythical remedy named after King Mithridates VI of Pontus, intended to make the user immune to all poisons</note>, and other noble antidotes. These physicians affirm that there has never been any other <term>opobalsamum</term> <note>original: "opobalsamum"; the resin or sap of the balsam tree</note>, nor other fruits or wood accepted for medical use and common among them. 

Will anyone truly contend that our Italians should be preferred to the Egyptians, Syrians, and Arabs in the knowledge, use, and experience of these same medicines? Our countrymen have neither seen the place where the shrub grew, nor have they seen it living anywhere else. Or should we rather believe those who lived for many years in the same region as that plant, saw it very often, and diligently observed it? For they were the first to call that plant *Balessam* <note>A name derived from the Arabic "balasan"</note>, a name which all others—both Greeks and Latins—have used most willingly. 

Since, therefore, those people are rightly preferred to the Italians and all other nations in their knowledge of this plant and its juice, fruit, and twigs; and since these are believed by them to be the true materials and are used as legitimate and authentic for compounding all medicines; and since they affirm that the juice brought here from Egypt or Syria and sometimes offered to us is true <term>opobalsamum</term>: why should we refuse to use them or to admit them into medical practice, rather than substituting something else in their place when compounding medicines? For this reason, many should not wonder if the **Theriac** and **Mithridatium** of our time no longer retain that ancient excellence and nobility, nor if those extraordinary powers celebrated by the elder Andromachus <note>Andromachus the Elder was the court physician to the Emperor Nero; he is credited with refining the formula for Theriac by adding vipers' flesh</note> are no longer preserved.

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of Egyptian balsam. The previous page discussed how Eastern physicians unanimously use the genuine resin, fruit, and wood of the balsam tree in their most potent antidotes, whereas European physicians are often misled by ignorance or false substitutes.</meta>

->Trusting in this remedy, Caesar, you shall wander through the countryside,<-
->though you may advance upon Libyan soil.<-

For who among the physicians of this age would trust that those bitten by vipers, Egyptian asps, and scorpions, or by other such serpents, could be cured by **Theriac** or **Mithridatium** <note>original: "Theriaca, vel Mithridatio"; Theriac and Mithridatium were complex, prestigious ancient multi-ingredient remedies used as universal antidotes</note> alone? Or who, relying on the hope of these remedies, would dare to harmlessly ingest monkshood <note>original: "aconitum"</note>, wolfsbane <note>original: "napellum"</note>, or poison <note>original: "toxicum"</note>? Why is it that the **Theriac** prepared even by our own people with every care and diligence is entirely lacking in those great virtues? It is because this antidote requires many ingredients that were used in its preparation in those ancient times. Among these, the foremost place is surely held by <term>opobalsamum</term> <note>the sap or resin of the balsam tree</note>, <term>carpobalsamum</term> <note>the fruit or berry of the balsam tree</note>, and <term>xylobalsamum</term> <note>the wood or twigs of the balsam tree</note>, in place of which our pharmacists substitute other medicines. 

For this reason, many proclaim Egyptian **Theriac** to be far superior, because the true and fresh medicines mentioned above are included in its composition. Therefore, having stayed in Egypt for several years for the sake of medical study, I noticed and truly learned that those genuine medicines—which are rejected from medical use by our people (not without great loss to the compounding of remedies) and are said to be false or adulterated—are not infrequently exported to us from those places. 

Out of a zeal for discovering the truth, I have decided to dedicate this book—written some time ago in Egypt, and which contains the knowledge of all these things—to you, most Illustrious and Wise men, for your reading and inspection. This work covers the balsam plant, its juice, fruits, and branches, as passed down by the writings of ancient doctors but now almost obsolete among our own. Through this, once the truth regarding balsam has been seen and understood, may you endeavor so that these excellent medicines, through the work and authority of you all, may be recognized by all lovers of medicine and restored to their former medical use.

In the year of human salvation, 
1592.

He dedicates his Egyptian-authored treatise to the learned authorities to restore these "obsolete" but vital components to medical practice.</summary>

# PROSPER ALPINUS
## TO THE READER.

<image-desc>A small decorative woodcut ornament featuring a central human face mask flanked by symmetrical leafy scrolls or wings.</image-desc>

<image-desc>Large ornamental woodcut initial 'M' depicting a king or noble figure in a robe, seated on a throne and holding a scepter.</image-desc>*MANY years ago, while staying in Egypt for the purpose of medical study, I wrote several books both on the medicine commonly used by that nation and on its plants. I especially composed a dialogue on balsam, through which the truth about the tree, the sap, the fruit, and the twigs—hitherto obscure to most physicians and pharmacists—might be recognized and shine forth clearly. I was indeed planning to publish all those books together. But at the urging of certain friends, who thought I would not be publishing the edition of the other books quite so soon, I changed my mind. This was especially due to the earnest persuasion of some very learned pharmacists who, being now ready to prepare **Theriac** and **Mithridatium** <note>These were prestigious, multi-ingredient "universal antidotes" in the ancient and Renaissance world</note>, long for the truth and history of genuine <term>opobalsamum</term> <note>the resin or sap of the balsam tree</note>, <term>carpobalsamum</term> <note>the fruit or berry of the balsam tree</note>, and <term>xylobalsamum</term> <note>the wood or twigs of the balsam tree</note> to be recognized by the most Excellent College of Physicians of this famous city <note>original: "inclytae vrbis"; referring to Venice, where the quality of such medicines was strictly regulated</note>. They desire this so that they might freely use these ingredients for preparing the aforementioned medicines and others. Not without reason, they doubt that **Theriac** and **Mithridatium**—if these ingredients are removed—fall short significantly of their ancient excellence. Lest I should seem unwilling to comply with their desire, I have not hesitated to release this little book first. Believe this, dear reader, to be the true reason for this speedy publication. In the meantime, you may expect the other books called "On the Medicine of the Egyptians" to come to light shortly (provided I understand that this little book of mine has pleased students of medicine in some way). And you shall receive this discussion on balsam to be read with the same spirit with which I pursued it: solely out of a zeal for recognizing the truth and a desire to be of service. Farewell.*

<vocab>Prosper Alpinus, Egypt, medicine, balsam, Theriac, Mithridatium, opobalsamum, carpobalsamum, xylobalsamum, pharmacists</vocab>

# PROSPER
# ALPINUS'S
## DIALOGUE ON BALSAM.

<image-desc>A small decorative woodcut vignette of a winged cherub's head.</image-desc>

### *THAT WE MUST SPEAK CONCERNING THE BALSAM PLANT, ITS SAP, FRUITS, AND TWIGS. Chapter 1.*

-> ABDELLA, an Egyptian Physician; ABDACHIM, a Jew; and ALPINUS, an Italian. <-

<image-desc>A large historiated woodcut initial 'Q' containing a figure standing in a landscape with architectural structures in the distance.</image-desc>

WHO is this man I see moving through this garden of **Al-Matariyyah** <note>original: "el Mattharia"; a famous site near Cairo which held the last remaining grove of balsam trees in the world during this period</note>? It is certainly Abdachim the Physician, a man endowed with most honorable character (even though he is a Jew), and very familiar to me. I shall therefore go to meet him and greet him. Greetings, Abdachim! How early you have entered this garden; what business brings you here so early in the morning?

ABDACHIM. I am very glad you have come here now, as I have long desired to see you and talk with you. May this day be a happy one for you. I came here to refresh myself a little with the beauty of this garden, the very sweet scent of the **cassia** <note>an aromatic plant often used in perfumes and medicines</note> flowers, and the cool morning air.

ABDELLA. But why are you observing the plants of this garden on every side? Why do I not see you stretched out on the ground beneath the broad branches of this **Egyptian fig tree** <note>likely the Sycamore fig (*Ficus sycomorus*), famous in Egypt for its massive, shade-giving canopy</note>, so that you might refresh both your mind and body in greater peace?

ABDACHIM.

The characters begin a casual conversation about the garden's beauty and botanical variety before delving into the technical study of balsam.</summary>

# ON BALSAM

I was moving my eyes here and there to see if I could spot any living balsam plant, whose exceptional scent I might have used to delight my **animal spirits** <note>In early modern medicine, 'animal spirits' were believed to be subtle vapors that traveled through the nerves to facilitate sensation and movement.</note>. However, I discovered they have withered away due to the negligence of the guards. This affects me quite painfully; it vexes me greatly that this garden now lacks those plants, and for this reason, it seems much of its ornament is missing. 

ABDELLA. Do not torture yourself over this, since many times plants brought here from elsewhere and grafted have perished; perhaps they will soon be renewed, with others being brought by the order of the **Governor** <note>original: "præfecti"; referring to the Ottoman governor of Egypt</note>, as has often been done before. But who is this I see coming toward us? Is it Alpinus, the physician to the Venetian Consul? 

ABDACHIM. It is he, and it is only right that we go to meet him, for he is a truly kind and affable man. 

ABDELLA. What is this, Alpinus? That we find you here now? Your arrival is most joyful to us for many reasons. 

ALPINUS. I have come here for the sake of seeing this garden, and I am greatly indebted to you for your kindness toward me. I am no less glad to have found you both here, for I have long desired to see you—and not without reason, since I hold you in such high regard for many reasons. Nor is there any need for me now to extol your learning in the medical art to your faces, nor how much I have profited or learned from you in it. 

ABDELLA. You are kind to think so; however things may stand, we most desire to be loved by you. 

ALPINUS. I thank you, but I fear lest by my untimely arrival I might interrupt the conversation you have already begun. Yet, if I might be permitted to share in it and in your most pleasant company, my arrival here would be a source of wonderful pleasure to me. 

ABDELLA. It will be most welcome to speak with you now, most learned sir; from your learning, we hope our discussion will receive the greatest clarity. 

ALPINUS. I thank you. But what was the subject you were discussing? 

ABDACHIM. We were conversing among ourselves about the balsam plant, which I found had withered in this garden. While I was taking its loss poorly, our friend Abdella, who is most experienced in the matter of plants, restored my spirits by telling me that many were once brought here from **Mecca** <note>original: "Mecha"</note> several times and grafted, and they fully revived. In this way, the balsam plants have often been renewed, so it is to be hoped that now, by order of the Governor of Egypt, once the news of this loss eventually reaches his ears, they can be restored once more. 

ALPINUS. What do I hear? That those plants were brought here from elsewhere...

# DIALOGUE

...and in what way they are constantly renewed? As if Egypt were not their own native soil. I hope I have not now fallen into an idle conversation, having begun about **balsam** <note>Also known as the Balm of Gilead, this resin was one of the most precious substances in the ancient and Renaissance world, used for medicine, perfume, and holy anointing oil.</note>: concerning which I have long desired to speak many things with you. If I thought these things would be pleasing to you now, I would gladly propose them. ABDEL. You will do us a favor if you move forward with some discussion on this matter; we hope to gain much benefit from it, given your learning and kindness. Accordingly, whatever it is that you wish to know from us, bring it forth now. ALPIN. I owe you much for this, and I recognize the wisdom and kindness of the ancient Egyptians. But so as not to draw the matter out with words, I desire now to discuss with you the balsam plant, its juice, fruits, and wood, since I have hitherto been uncertain and doubtful due to the **variety of writers** <note>Alpinus refers to the conflicting descriptions of balsam found in classical texts by Pliny, Dioscorides, and Theophrastus.</note>. ABDACH. I believe there is no one among the Egyptians or Arabs more skilled in **botany** <term>re plantaria: the study or science of plants</term> than our Abdella; he has inspected many of these same plants both here and elsewhere, observing them with all diligence. But so that we may discuss this matter more clearly, you ought to organize it into chapters, which should be discussed one by one. Therefore, bring all these things forth now. ALPIN. I think you should first say whether the balsam plant is living anywhere at this time, since in this **garden** <note>The Matariyya garden near Cairo, the only place where balsam was famously cultivated in Egypt for centuries.</note>—where it has lived from ancient times until now—it has completely withered away; and likewise, whether the juice, fruit, and wood collected from it are still brought to us, or if they have all perished along with the tree. These are the things I primarily wish you to explain to me; once these are known, we will deal with each one individually.

## *Whether the balsam plant, and its juice, fruit, and twigs are found now, and in which places they grow.*

### Ch. II.

->ABDELLA.<-

<image-desc>Decorative ornamental drop cap 'Q' featuring a seated figure, possibly a scholar or saint, within a square frame with floral and leaf patterns.</image-desc>

It is most certain that very many balsam trees are living now in many places in Arabia, whose juice, fruit, and twigs are carried here to us by many. Nor is this hidden from the Egyptians, Syrians, Turks, and many people of other nations traveling through the regions of Arabia; on the contrary, every—

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Prospero Alpini and his guides, Abdella and Abdachim, regarding the geographic origin and survival of the balsam plant. The text begins mid-sentence, completing a thought about those who travel to the holy cities of Arabia.</meta>

# ON BALSAM

...people who every year visit Mecca and Medina, the primary cities of **Arabia Felix** <note>Latin: *Arabia foelix*. A classical term meaning "Happy" or "Fertile Arabia," referring to the southern part of the Arabian Peninsula which was much more lush than the desert interior.</note>, for the sake of pilgrimage, can clearly testify to how great the harvest of balsam from the tree is in those places. For those going from here to Mecca, and approaching a certain village called **Bedrunia** <note>Likely the town of Badr, famous as a stop on the pilgrimage route between Egypt and Mecca.</note> by them, which is a journey of fifteen days from the city of Cairo, a very large, sandy mountain situated near that village is immediately seen, filled with innumerable small balsam trees. Many of our people have been persuaded that these trees sprang up there by a miracle of **Muhammad**; but however they may have been born there, it is enough for us that everyone who travels through those places every year has found that countless trees live there. You may hear this from very many inhabitants of this city who once, and even in the year just passed, traveled through those places, and you will know it to be true. 

I could also persuade you of this truth by many reasons, but if you were to smell the earth of this garden—which was once filled with many balsam plants—you would perceive that even now it gives off the sweetest scent of balsam, and only two or three times has the **Sun completed its orbit** <note>A poetic way of saying two or three years have passed.</note> since these plants perished through the negligence of their guardians. But why are you silent, **Abdachim**, whom I know for certain has inspected them here a thousand times? Or why do you not help me, the Italian physician, as I attempt to persuade the incredulous of this truth? 

ABDACH. What? Do you doubt that our fellow physician here, who has stayed many years and is a student of all plants, was ignorant of this truth? I certainly know for a fact that countless balsam plants, brought from Arabia at different times and planted here, have lived, which many other inhabitants of Cairo have also inspected. 

ALPINUS. I would think it a **wickedness** <note>Latin: *nephas*. Something contrary to divine law or deeply improper.</note> and a sign of rudeness not to be moved by the testimony of such great physicians, who, besides their skill in plants—for which they are considered illustrious among most Egyptians—have lived in the same land where the balsam was born. But, to speak frankly, although I feel exactly as these most distinguished men of medicine do, and I recognize the truth of this matter entirely, nevertheless this truth does not satisfy me so completely that I am able to persuade very many other skeptical physicians and pharmacists in Italy and other places in Europe as I would wish. They absolutely assert that the balsam plant is found nowhere at this time and that it has clearly perished; because of this, they also determine that the juice, fruit, and twigs brought from here to Italy are false. Furthermore, in ancient times, **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and botanist (c. 40–90 AD) whose work *De Materia Medica* was the primary authority on herbal medicine for 1,500 years.</note> and others recorded that balsam lived only in Egypt and Judea; in which places...

<summary>Alpinus and Abdachim discuss the current existence of balsam in Arabia and Cairo, noting that while locals confirm its abundance, European scholars remain skeptical and believe the plant is extinct.</summary>

# DIALOGUE
### Page 64

...now, since no one reports that this plant lives any longer, they conclude that it has plainly perished in all places. This opinion they defend most stubbornly, and they do not seem to lack arguments by which they might protect their own error. 

**ABDEL.** How, I ask you, can that be? Are such great physicians and most learned professors of herbs so blinded that they have not seen, nor recognized, that the balsam plants have by no means perished in their native soil? You will learn from me in a little while how **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and botanist (c. 40–90 AD) whose work *De Materia Medica* was the primary authority on herbal medicine for 1,500 years.</note> and others falsely believed it to be Egyptian and Judaic. Nor are those men the best philosophers who think that the species of vegetables—which, according to the teachings of the philosophers, exist perpetually—can be entirely removed from existence. For since the Heavens are not now deprived of any of the causes that lead to generation, and the Earth and the other elements remain in the same state now as they once did, why shall we say that the Earth cannot bear the same plants now as it used to do? Please, let it not be troublesome for you to tell me of those who affirm that balsam has perished, and to explain by what reasons they have come to such a false opinion. 

**ALPIN.** I think it matters little for you to know these men by name; it is better to hear their reasons for denying that balsam trees, and their juice, fruit, and twigs, are found among us at this time. That will be enough for the investigation we have begun. For there are not a few who not only deny that the true balsam mentioned before is brought to us in Italy, but even preach that the trees from which those things are collected have utterly perished. 

This opinion was greatly increased by **Amatus Lusitanus** <note>João Rodriguez de Castelo Branco (1511–1568), a famous Portuguese Jewish physician.</note>, a physician not to be despised in other matters, and **Nicolaus Monardus** <note>Nicolás Monardes (1493–1588), a Spanish physician famous for his descriptions of plants from the New World.</note> the Spaniard. They affirm that Syrian and Egyptian balsam has perished, and have declared that another kind of balsam is born in the **West Indies** <note>Latin: *Occidentalibus Indijs*. Monardes was describing "Balsam of Peru" or "Tolu," which Europeans initially hoped was the same as the lost biblical balsam.</note>. They affirm that the balsam celebrated and described by **Dioscorides**, **Theophrastus**, **Pliny**, and many other ancient writers on herbs, has perished because it is established by the testimony of those same ancient writers that it was once granted only to two gardens in **Judea**, as Pliny and Theophrastus, among others, testify. 

They establish that after Judea was devastated by **Ptolemy**, the first king of Egypt, and later by **Vespasian** <note>Roman Emperor (r. 69–79 AD) who led the initial Roman response to the Jewish Revolt; the balsam groves of Jericho were a major prize of this war.</note>, the balsam was transplanted into the valley called **Jericho** <note>Latin: *Hiericum*.</note> and lived there, as **Justin**, **Strabo**, and **Solinus Polyhistor** affirm. But they claim that after the war led by **Artaxerxes**, the first king of the Persians, and then again when that whole region was overturned in war by the Romans under **Hadrian Caesar**, the balsam plant utterly perished. And finally—

...that a few survived in Egypt, preserved here in this pleasure garden. In this place, balsam lived not only in ancient centuries, but much later as well; indeed, they assert that this plant was preserved here almost until our own times. But now, it is confirmed by many who have traveled through Egypt recently that here, too, it has finally withered away. Since it is extinct here—and since **Dioscorides** <note>A famous Greek physician and botanist (c. 40–90 AD) whose work was the standard authority for centuries.</note> and others recorded that balsam lived only in Judea and Egypt—who can doubt that it can no longer be found anywhere in the world? Based on this, how shall we affirm that the juice, fruit, and twigs brought from Egypt or Syria into Italy as true **opobalsamum** <note>The precious resin or sap of the balsam tree.</note>, **carpobalsamum** <note>The fruit or seeds of the balsam tree.</note>, and **xylobalsamum** <note>The wood or twigs of the balsam tree.</note> are genuine? It seems, therefore, that we have rightly claimed all these things to be false. From this, such great disbelief and stubbornness has invaded their minds that they would rather persevere in their errors and reject the truths offered to them than change their opinion.

**ABDEL.** I could scarcely contain myself from interrupting the speech you began, so much have you stirred my spirit against those men. They assert with no reasons, but mere trifles, that all the balsam plants have perished, and that the opobalsamum, carpobalsamum, and xylobalsamum found among us are false. I am so provoked by their impudence that it is as if I do not know what I am saying. So that I do not delay in answering them, I tell you, and I declare to you, that it cannot be said that the balsam shrubs have completely perished even in Egypt. This is because often, by order of the governor of Cairo—whom they call the **Basha** <note>Original: *Beſsà*. This refers to the Pasha, the high-ranking Ottoman official governing the region.</note>—many plants brought from **Arabia Felix** <note>Latin for "Fertile Arabia," the southern part of the Arabian Peninsula known for its lush vegetation compared to the desert.</note> are planted in that garden. And now, barely three years have passed since forty plants were brought from **Mecca** and planted here by order of Meſſinora, the governor of Egypt at that time. Although they lived for a year, they perished again through the negligence of the keepers; yet these can easily be renewed again.

Indeed, in many places in Arabia, almost countless numbers of these plants are cultivated, bringing great profit to those people. Nor is it only recently that this utility and profit has been recognized among the Arabs; for a great quantity of opobalsamum is carried to various places in the East, where that juice is held in the highest price, as many of the Arabs now living in Cairo relate. Having recognized the profit to be made from this juice, everyone eagerly tries to transplant many balsam shrubs from the sandy and mountainous places into well-tended gardens. For this reason, countless beautiful balsam groves can be seen there. Nevertheless, it is forbidden by law to those peoples for anyone to plant that species except for the **treasury** <note>Original: *fiscum*. This indicates a state or royal monopoly on the production of balsam.</note>; nor may they sell the juice, or flowers, or twigs, or fruit without public...

-># DIALOGUE<-

...by that mandate, who may take it. The same situation occurs there with the balsam trees as happens here with **cassia** <note>A spice and medicinal bark similar to cinnamon.</note>. For it is customary here for the business of cassia to be leased out to someone, and therefore no one is permitted to take cassia from the tree, sell it, buy it, or carry it away once bought. That the same is observed concerning balsam in parts of Arabia is clearly established from the reports of the Arabs. 

But what will those herbalists say—ignorant as they are of Egyptian and Arabian plants—who claim that balsam was formerly granted only to two places, namely Egypt and Syria (as **Dioscorides**, **Theophrastus**, **Pliny**, and **Justin** <note>These were the primary authorities on natural history in the ancient world.</note> teach)? As if it were not established by the testimony of other ancient writers that this kind of plant lived in many other places in the past! Did not **Diodorus Siculus** <note>A Greek historian of the 1st century BC.</note> in the second book of his *Histories* record that balsam grows in a certain valley of Arabia and nowhere else? **Constantine** <note>Likely Constantine the African, a 11th-century physician and translator.</note>, in his book *On the Degrees*, says it grows in India; and **Josephus** <note>A famous 1st-century Romano-Jewish historian.</note> also, in the eighth book of his *Jewish Antiquities*, writes that the **Queen of Sheba** <note>original: "Sabam Reginam"</note> brought the balsam plant from Arabia into Judea and gave it as a gift to **Solomon**, from which other plants were later propagated there. And **Strabo** <note>A Greek geographer and historian (c. 64 BC–24 AD).</note> wrote these things in the 16th book of his *Geography*:

<margin>»</margin>
> Closest to these is the most fertile land of the **Sabaeans** <note>An ancient people of South Arabia, in modern-day Yemen.</note>, which is a very great nation. Among them grow frankincense, myrrh, and cinnamon; and on the coast, also **balsam** and a certain other very fragrant herb.

<margin>»</margin>
And **Pausanias** <note>A Greek traveler and geographer of the 2nd century AD.</note> also confirmed the same in his ninth book, saying in these words:
<margin>»</margin>
> Regarding those vipers that live among the balsam trees in Arabia, I have received a different account. It is of this sort: The balsam trees are about the size of a **myrtle** <note>original: "mirthi"</note> shrub. Their leaves are like those of the **marjoram** herb <note>original: "amaraco"</note>.

**Theophrastus** <note>The "Father of Botany" and successor to Aristotle.</note> indeed recorded that it also grows in Asia. The inhabitants of the "Basilian" part of **Mount Lebanon** also relate that long ago, during the reign of the Greek Emperor **Alexius**, many balsam plants lived in a certain spot on that mountain exposed to the sunrise; from these, as much **opobalsamum** <note>The precious sap of the tree.</note> was obtained as was customarily collected here in Egypt. From these accounts, it can certainly be known that in the past, not only Egypt and Judea, but also **Arabia Felix** <note>"Fertile Arabia," the southern part of the Arabian Peninsula.</note> and other places nourished and possessed balsam. 

This truth even seems to be confirmed by Dioscorides himself, who writes that **carpobalsamum** (that is, the fruit of the balsam) was in his time adulterated with seeds similar to **St. John's Wort** <note>original: "hyperici"</note> brought from the town of **Petra** <note>The famous rock-cut city in modern-day Jordan.</note>. From this, it is certainly gathered that the balsam plant lived elsewhere than in Egypt and produced seeds. Furthermore, you shall hear from me that this is also true: balsam did not come into Egypt from Judea, as some have dreamed, but was always brought from Arabia Felix. The Egyptian soil indeed...

<meta>This page continues the argument that the balsam tree is not native to Judea or Egypt but is an exotic transplant from Arabia. It follows a discussion on the Queen of Sheba introducing the plant to King Solomon.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>
# ON BALSAM

It is no small indication that balsam was never truly local, nor native to Judea, but has always existed in those places as a foreign plant, for it was only ever cultivated in gardens and survived only within them, as **Theophrastus**, **Pliny**, **Strabo**, and **Solinus** <note>These are major Greek and Roman authorities on natural history and geography.</note> have recorded. Pliny, for instance, indicated this in these very words, saying:
> “But balsam is preferred above all other scents; it was once granted to the single land of Judea, and then only in two gardens, both of which belonged to the king—one no larger than twenty **iugera** <note>A Roman unit of land area, roughly equivalent to five-eighths of an acre.</note>, the other even smaller.” 

Theophrastus indeed said this: “Balsam occurs in the Valley of Syria. They claim there are only two groves of it, one of twenty *iugera*, the other much smaller.” And Strabo, writing of the balsam of the valley of **Jericho** <note>original: "Hierici"</note>, also handed down this memory: “Where there is both a palace and a balsam garden; the tree is aromatic and shrub-like, very similar to the **cytisus** <note>Likely a reference to the tree-medick or a similar flowering shrub.</note> and the **terebinth** <note>The turpentine tree, known for its resin.</note>.”

What of this? Does not everyone observe that foreign plants are only nurtured and guarded in gardens, while native plants—even if they are sometimes kept in gardens—nonetheless do not lack many other uncultivated and wild places where they grow of their own accord? But foreign plants are nurtured and preserved in gardens only with great zeal and diligence; they are never seen to grow spontaneously in uncultivated or wild places. 

That the balsam plant was always an immigrant or a foreigner here, and was perpetually brought in from parts of **Arabia Felix** <note>original: "Foelicis Arabiae"; meaning "Happy" or "Fertile" Arabia, a term used for the southern Arabian Peninsula, primarily modern Yemen.</note>, many currently living with us in **Cairo** <note>original: "Cayrum"</note> can confirm with certain faith. All of them have observed that the balsam plants which this garden has held up to now were brought from there at various times. They survived only with much labor and the diligence of guardians, and they withered easily from even the slightest cause; when they dried up, others were again extracted from those places, grafted and renewed here. In this way, by alternating turns, the balsam has been preserved in this garden until now. No one would call plants native that are kept in gardens with such labor and diligence, since native plants everywhere are born and grow spontaneously, and live in their proper places with no labor at all. 

From these facts, we ought to be plainly certain that balsam was never native to the Egyptians or the Syrians, since it was always guarded there in gardens with much diligence and care. However, the same cannot be said of **Arabia Felix**, since balsam lives there perpetually. For nowhere else now, so far as I know, is such a plant found, unless granted to Arabia Felix alone—which region, by the testimony of **Diodorus Siculus**, **Strabo**, and **Pausanias**...

<meta>catchword: as</meta>

He provides contemporary evidence from Cairo that the trees are difficult to maintain and must be constantly imported from Arabia Felix.</summary>

<page-num>66</page-num>

### DIALOGUE

As was recently mentioned, it is established that this region was once fertile with balsam, which even now supports countless plants of the same kind growing spontaneously. This fertility of balsam is confirmed by all Egyptians and people of other nations who visit Mecca in **Arabia Felix** <note>literally "Happy Arabia," the ancient name for the fertile southern part of the Arabian Peninsula, contrasting with the desert regions</note> every year, asserting it to be plainly true. They confirm that a remarkable quantity of **opobalsamum** <term>the resin or sap extracted from the balsam tree, highly prized in medicine and perfumery</term> is collected there annually, along with a great many fruits and branches (since they prune the balsam shrubs every year) which are sent by them to various parts of the world to be sold.

**ABDACH.** But even granting those things are true, some will say that the plant you and our people claim Arabia Felix now supports is not the true balsam, but another plant entirely.

**ALPIN.** The characteristics of this plant, described and handed down to memory by ancient writers, will be able to teach us the truth of this. For all seem to have agreed (as I hope to learn more distinctly from you) that the balsam plant is a shrub, not very large, with leaves very close to **rue** <note>a bitter herb known for its distinct, lobed, bluish-green leaves</note>, which are evergreen. Furthermore, from the incised bark of the trunk, a liquid flows that is primarily white and similar to oil, endowed with an extraordinary and sharp scent.

**ABDACH.** I know that I, together with Abdella, have inspected this shrub described by Alpinus a thousand times in this garden. But for one who seeks the characteristics of this plant, which people should he believe more? Italians, or us Egyptians, who have lived with the balsam in this city until now and have very often inspected that tree in this garden? Surely no one will be so foolish as to prefer to trust and rely on you Italians rather than us in this matter. Our Arabs have held this plant to be the true tree—the one called balsam by the ancients and recorded for posterity—with the utmost constancy until now. None of our people has ever come across or seen, or even heard of anyone else seeing, any other plant shown to be the balsam.

To this I add the name of this plant, which is **Balessan**, a name given to it by our people. From this, the Greeks—lacking the letter "b" <note>This likely refers to the phonetic shift where the Greek 'beta' (β) began to be pronounced like a 'v', or the specific way the Semitic 'b' was adapted into Greek</note> and changing it to "v"—called it *balsamon* <note>original: "βάλσαμον"</note>, and from them the Latins called it **Balsamum**. Why, then, should Italians be believed more than our locals regarding this plant, when both Italians and Greeks learned the very name of the plant from our ancestors? Therefore, I judge that knowledge of the Balsam should by no means be sought from **Dioscorides**, **Theophrastus** <note>Ancient Greek authorities on botany and medicine</note>, or other ancient writers, but from the Egyptians and Arabs who inhabited the places where the balsam plants, by everyone's consent, once lived. If the Egyptians, Arabs, Syrians, and all others...

# ON BALSAM

All physicians using the Arabic language accept such a shrub—which grows now in the cultivated and uncultivated, domestic and wild places of **Arabia Felix** <note>literally "Happy Arabia," the ancient name for the fertile southern parts of the Arabian Peninsula</note>—as the true balsam without any difficulty. They use its juice, its fruits, and its twigs as true **opobalsamum** <term>the resin or sap extracted from the balsam tree</term>, **carpobalsamum** <term>the fruit of the balsam</term>, and **xylobalsamum** <term>the medicinal wood of the balsam</term>, with none of them hesitating to use these to compose their medicines. Why do the Italians and physicians of other nations—who are less practiced and learned in this knowledge and its use than they are—refuse to do the same? Surely it is a shameful injustice that you do not acknowledge this truth, but rather take pains so that it remains unrecognized by many.

**ALPINUS.** I believe these things have been most learnedly presented by you; however, since this dispute is being conducted with skeptical physicians and pharmacists, I ask that if you have anything else that could further illuminate this truth, you do not begrudge teaching it to me. In this way, after I have returned to my homeland, God willing, I may more easily persuade those skeptical men.

**ABDELLA.** In the year 1575 from the birth of the Blessed Virgin <note>the year 1575 AD</note>, while the most distinguished Pietro Michele was acting as Consul here for the Most Serene Republic of Venice, the Prefect of Egypt, a eunuch named Mesir, visited certain places in this city of **Cairo** <note>original: "Cayri"</note>. He especially visited this place called **Matariyah** <note>original: "el Mattharia," a site near Cairo famous for its ancient balsam grove and a spring where the Holy Family reportedly rested</note> out of devotion. (For all Egyptians believe that the Blessed Virgin, fleeing the wrath of Herod with her young son, once took refuge in a nearby cottage and remained in it for many years; for this reason, they hold this place in the highest veneration.) Visiting this sacred and venerable place every week on Friday, he occasionally entered this nearby garden for relaxation, and he saw that all the balsam plants had withered due to the negligence of the guards. Moved by this, he arranged for forty plants to be brought from **Mecca** <note>original: "Mecha"</note>, and he assigned this task to a certain leader of soldiers and pilgrims who travel to Mecca every year for devotion, named Haly Bei. He was very much a domestic and familiar friend to me, and I had often visited and treated him when he was gravely ill. He brought those forty plants with their branches pruned, which were planted in the accustomed place of this garden. While they were being planted, I was present along with Paul Marianus—at that time the Consul for the King of the French, and a close friend of mine for the sake of medicine—and also with many others...
