-> # DIALOGUE <-

I was present with Cairene physicians who were highly practiced in the study of plants. From **Ali Bey** <note>original: "Haly Bei," likely a high-ranking Ottoman official in Egypt</note> himself, upon his return to **Cairo**, I received two ounces of true **opobalsamum** <term>the resinous sap of the balsam tree</term> as a gift, and from another who had completed that journey after him, I received three ounces. **Horrem Bey**, another leader of the pilgrim caravan and a very dear friend of mine—in whose house I often practiced medicine for both him and others—set out for **Mecca** in the year 1580. At my request, he brought back many seeds and small branches breathing an exquisite scent, along with a great deal of opobalsamum. Part of this was received as a gift from the **Sharif of Mecca** <note>the traditional ruler and steward of the holy cities of Islam</note> for **Francesco Priolo**, who was at that time the most distinguished Consul for the **Venetian Nation** <note>the community of Venetian merchants and citizens</note> in Cairo, and part was purchased from those to whom the balsam business is leased. 

Later, a certain **Skander**, also a military leader, who was sent by the **Pasha** <note>the Ottoman governor of Egypt</note> as a governor to **Medina** in **Arabia Felix** <note>literally "Happy Arabia," the fertile southern and western parts of the Arabian Peninsula</note>, and who was a close friend to me (partly for medical reasons and partly because he traced his lineage from the **Seguro** family of the island of **Zakynthos** <note>a Greek island then under the rule of the Republic of Venice</note>), sent me fresh branches, fruits, and juice of the balsam. He indicated in letters written in his own hand that he had collected these there with his own hands; all of these items are still preserved by me. All the aforementioned Turkish nobles affirm with one voice that near Mecca and Medina, in the mountains, plains, and both cultivated and uncultivated places, countless balsam plants are seen growing spontaneously. Many also grow in sandy and sterile places, which nevertheless produced either nothing or a very small amount of juice. 

However, they bear many seeds, a great abundance of which is sent to us for sale. Such are those found on a sandy mountain located near the village of **Bedrunia**. These plants, and others living in sandy and sterile places, yield no opobalsamum; therefore, the inhabitants uproot them and plant them in rich soil, striving to make them most fruitful. They add that it is well-known in the collective memory of all Arabs that countless balsam plants have lived and grown spontaneously in many places of Arabia Felix, and that those regions have never been without them. However, it has not been long since many people began to cultivate them for the sake of profit—specifically since the power and medicinal virtues of opobalsamum became known in so many regions of the East. 

All these same Arabs further recount that they use the balsam juice, fruits, and twigs to prepare all their medicines and for other medical purposes. These are clearly the same substances that the **Egyptians** and **Syrians** now accept for use, and which are neglected and rejected by you when brought from here to various places in Europe—and their false... <note>The text likely continues to describe the "false opinions" of European doctors who doubt the authenticity of these Arabian specimens.</note>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Italian botanist Prospero Alpini and his local informants in Egypt, Abdach and Abdella. In the preceding text, Abdella had been recounting his firsthand experiences and the accounts of travelers to show that the balsam plant is not a lost legend but a living reality in the Arabian Peninsula. This page shifts from the history of its acquisition to a detailed botanical description of the plant itself.</meta>

Many, according to you, argue stubbornly and uselessly. **ALPINUS:** Regarding those things which I believe you have spoken truly, I am not a little ashamed that I remained in such great error along with them for so many years—an error which I confess I have now properly recognized through you. I desire, therefore, that this plant also be described by you according to its physical characteristics: to what height it grows, and what sort of flowers and fruits you have observed on it; and also in what manner **opobalsamum** <term>the precious liquid resin or sap of the balsam tree</term> drips from that plant—whether it flows spontaneously or, as many have said, it is rather drawn out by art <note>meaning human intervention or skill, such as making intentional cuts in the bark</note>. **ABDACH:** I have never been able to observe any plants growing here that bore flowers or seeds, even if, in the meantime, a small amount of juice occasionally distilled from the scarred bark of the trunk. But let us hear from Abdella on this matter, who will teach these things more thoroughly, along with the rest. Meanwhile, as we speak, I would like you to command your servant to bring us the illustration <note>original: "ichonem," from the Greek *eikon*</note> of this plant from the house, the one which is in your possession, so that our Alpinus may see a true image of the Balsam. **ABDELLA:** You advise me well, and I will do so gladly. **ALPINUS:** Indeed, I return many thanks to both of you for this reason. That will always be most welcome to me.

->*A description of the fruits and juice of the plant which was called **Baleſſan** <note>the Arabic name</note> by the ancient physicians of Arabia, **Balsamon** <note>original: "βάλσαμον"</note> by the Greeks, and **Balsamum** by the Latins. Chapter III.*<-

## ABDELLA.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'B' with scrolling floral motifs and a cross-hatched background.</image-desc>
**BALSAM** is a small tree which grows to the height of the henna <note>original: "hannæ"</note>, or privet, or laburnum <note>original: "cytisi," a genus of flowering shrubs</note>, bearing very few leaves that are most similar to rue, though they are not whiter than those of rue, as **Dioscorides** <note>a famous 1st-century Greek physician and the author of a massive herbal encyclopedia</note> had taught; rather, they are of a whitish-green color and are evergreen. Its wood appears gummy and smooth, with a reddish color on the outside, and its twigs are seen to be of the same color—long, straight, and slender, sparsely covered with leaves in no particular order. These leaves are attached to the stems in groups of three, five, or seven at a time, in some way rivaling the leaves of the mastic tree <note>original: "lenthiſci"</note>. The twigs are fragrant and gummy, and when handled with the fingers, they stick to them tenaciously. It bears small, white flowers, very close to those of the acacia, hanging in threes from each leaf joint in the form of an **umbel** <term>a flower cluster where the stalks spread from a common center like the ribs of an umbrella</term>; these are extremely fragrant. From these flowers the seeds emerge; they are yellow, contained in blackish-red pods, and are very fragrant; and inside...

and containing within a yellow moisture very similar to honey, with a somewhat bitter taste and striking the tongue with a slight sharpness, smelling of **opobalsamum** <term>the precious liquid resin or sap of the balsam tree</term>, rivaling the shape and size of terebinth <note>the fruit of *Pistacia terebinthus*, common in the Mediterranean</note> fruits, having pointed ends and a thick middle. To this description, that which was handed down by **Constantine the African** <note>an 11th-century physician whose translations of Arabic medicine were foundational to European science</note> seems to fit best, which is read in his own words in his book *On Degrees*:

> Balsam is a tree growing in India, rising above the ground to the measure of one cubit <note>Latin: *brachij*, roughly 18 inches</note>, or a little more. Its branches, suffused with red and green colors, are thin like the branches of the spurge <note>original: *tithymali*, plants in the *Euphorbia* genus known for their milky sap</note>, and having a green color. And they form at the top something like buds, producing small grains in the manner of pepper. From the cracks in the branches of this tree, a certain liquid comes out during the dog days <note>the hottest period of summer, usually July and August</note>, like the milk from the spurge.

**Opobalsamum** drips in the months of June, July, and August, partly spontaneously and partly from the trunk being scarred <note>intentionally cut or incised</note> with iron; it is received into small glass vessels. This, as soon as it is drawn out, so I hear, is white in color, which a little later changes to green, thirdly to gold, and finally to a honey-like hue. The substance of this juice is initially seen as cloudy and thick, like oil immediately pressed from olives; later it becomes very thin and clear, and finally thickens in the manner of terebinth resin. It is observed to have an extraordinary and very sharp scent at first, smelling of terebinth but with a sweeter fragrance, and a bitter, sharp, and astringent taste. It is very light in the first days, and of such a strong and sharp scent that, while some were smelling it, blood has burst from their nostrils. Furthermore, because of its great lightness, when dripped into water it does not sink; but if dropped into it from a height, it sinks for a moment, and almost immediately rising back up, it spreads entirely across the whole surface of the water and mixes with it. It is so quickly dissolved in the water that it is separated from it only with the greatest difficulty. In the water, a little later, it thickens and coagulates; once coagulated, it is all removed from the water with a stylus <note>a small pointed tool</note>, at which time it turns milky, or appears white like milk. The older this juice is, the less fragrant it becomes, and the slower it is to move. Thus, at first it is seen with a white color, which color changes to green in very few days, as I also just said, and it is seen with the thickness of oil. Later, after a long interval of time, it is purified, and over several years it becomes very thin and clear, passing from a green color to a most brilliant gold. But when worn out by old age, it becomes thick like terebinth resin; if it is then dripped into water or milk, it sinks immediately, and with much difficulty, and not except after a long time

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the maturation and aging process of balsam resin (opobalsamum). It explores the biological metaphor of the "four ages" of the substance and transitions into a dialogue regarding the inconsistencies found in ancient botanical authorities like Dioscorides and Theophrastus.</meta>

# ON BALSAM

Over time, it is carried again above the water and is dissolved in it very slowly. Therefore, just as in all living creatures four ages are established—which create the greatest differences within them—so undoubtedly four stages are assigned by our people to the juice of the balsam, which is called **opobalsamum** <term>the most precious liquid resin or "juice" of the balsam tree, as opposed to the fruit or wood</term>.

Since that juice, when continuously drawn from the tree trunk up to about the fifth year, is perceived to have an extremely sharp and powerful scent, it is observed to be so light that when dripped into water, little or none of it settles, and it is most easily diluted. This is because its "heat" <note>in the Galenic medical tradition, "heat" was a vital quality that determined the potency and thinness of substances</note>, being most abundant in its years of infancy, provides it with sharpness and intensity. It is also seen with a thicker substance at that time, and turbid; because when much raw moisture overflows within it, and is greatly agitated by the heat acting upon it, it appears like turbid urine, which is usually produced during pestilential fevers. And just as the bodies of children, endowed with much humidity and much heat, seem thick and almost swollen, so **opobalsamum** in its childhood years appears thick and turbid due to the presence of much waste moisture, with a very strong heat acting within it.

In its more advanced age, it is purged of this moisture, becomes very thin, and is seen with a very clear golden color. It is preserved in this state while its age maintains its consistency. Later, after its native heat has been somewhat dissolved and diminished, as it grows old it becomes thick like turpentine, and fails in its usual excellent scent and lightness. Consequently, it neither floats upon the water nor smells much or sweetly. 

Let these things said to you about the balsam plant, its fruits, wood, and juice be sufficient.

**ALPINUS:** I ask that you not be reluctant to respond to and satisfy some points still brought forward by our people which seem to contradict the description of balsam; for these points seem to obscure the known truth of this plant and leave us in doubt. For all the ancient writers on balsam seem to disagree among themselves in their descriptions of that plant, its juice, fruits, and branches. Because of this, it does not seem safe enough to seek out a definitive knowledge from them. This has been the reason why our people, imbued with these ancient traditions, have not believed that the true **opobalsamum**, fruits, and branches offered to them were genuine.

**ABDELLA:** I have always thought it a supreme error to want to demand knowledge of this plant, its juice, fruits, and branches from **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician whose *De Materia Medica* was the primary botanical text for 1,500 years</note>, **Theophrastus** <note>the "Father of Botany," a student of Aristotle</note>, or other ancient writers, when that diversity among them in writing about the shrub, juice, fruits, and branches is a most certain sign of igno—

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the inaccuracies found in ancient botanical authorities. It transitions from discussing the chemical properties of balsam resin to a critique of classical authors like Dioscorides and Theophrastus, who the speaker argues never actually saw the plant in person.</meta>

-># DIALOGUE<-

...evidence of their ignorance. I am of the opinion that perhaps none of them had accurately seen this tree, and that everything handed down by them in literature regarding it was based on hearsay. For in the past, it was not so easy for men to reach the places where balsam grew, due to the longer and more difficult voyages used by the ancients at that time. Therefore, Dioscorides, Theophrastus, and many others wrote about balsam from reports of others rather than from personal inspection. 

Thus it is no wonder if many of our own contemporaries, clinging to the opinions of these authors, were deceived, since we must think that the ancients themselves were deceived by the false reports of others. Have we not already demonstrated above that Dioscorides, Theophrastus, and others were greatly mistaken in claiming that Egypt or Judea was the homeland of the balsam plant? They claimed it lived only in Egypt and the Judean valley, when it is most certainly established from the reports of the Egyptians that this plant has consistently come into Egypt from *Arabia Felix* <note>original: "Arabia fœlici"; this refers to "Happy Arabia," the fertile southern part of the Arabian Peninsula, primarily modern-day Yemen.</note>. 

Because of this, it would not be surprising if they were deceived in other matters as well, especially regarding their knowledge of the juice and the fruits. Hence, we should not wonder that all physicians and pharmacists who placed their full trust in these botanical writers were mistaken; for their knowledge should have been sought not from those authors, as I have said, but from the Egyptians and Arabs. These peoples were the first to know and observe the balsam, they were born in the same soil as this plant, and they inhabited the very places where the balsam lives and has lived. But let us now hear from you what those points are that contradict our findings.

**ALPINI:** I will now bring forward what has been handed down by many in literature concerning the balsam tree; the rest concerning the medicinal use of its juice, fruits, and branches, I think will be reviewed by me later. Many and varied things have been reported by many writers regarding the size, shape, location, and leaves of this plant, which obscure the truth and make people waver, rendering this whole matter uncertain. 

Some, for instance, compared the size of this plant to the boxthorn <term>original: *lycium*, a thorny medicinal shrub</term>, the firethorn <term>original: *pyracantha*</term>, tree medick <term>original: *cytisus*</term>, or the terebinth tree <term>original: *therebinthus*, the source of turpentine</term>, as Dioscorides and Strabo stated. Theophrastus, however, said that balsam is seen at the size of a large pomegranate tree. Justinus claimed it was similar to pitch-producing trees <note>specifically coniferous trees like pines or firs</note>, and Pausanias compared it to myrtle. Pliny, Solinus, and the author of the *Description of Africa* <note>Likely referring to Leo Africanus, a famous 16th-century traveler</note> compared this plant to a grapevine. Regarding its leaves and the overall shape of the tree, there is no less diversity of opinion among them. When Dios...

-># ON BALSAM<-
<meta>catchword: pitch-trees</meta>

...[Dio]scorides, Theophrastus, Pliny, Avicenna, and Simeon Seth <note>Simeon Seth was an 11th-century Jewish-Byzantine physician who wrote on the properties of foods and medicines.</note> determined that the leaves of the balsam are very close to those of rue; Pausanias compared them to marjoram, Justin to pitch-trees, and Solinus and the author on Africa to the leaves of the vine. Regarding the form or shape of the tree, some make it a shrub, others a bush. For Pliny said of it: "Its nature is entirely different from what our own and foreign writers have reported; indeed, it is more like a vine than a myrtle." It is clear from Solinus's own words that he said the same, which appear as follows: "They have stems similar to vines." And Justin says: "Indeed, it is distinguished by palm groves and opobalsam; the opobalsam <term>opobalsamum<note>The high-quality resinous juice or oil extracted from the balsam tree.</note></term> trees have a form similar to pitch-trees." And Strabo says: "It is a shrubby tree very similar to *Cytisus* <note>original: "Cytiso"; likely refers to a type of woody broom plant.</note> and Terebinth."

Some even assert that this plant is nourished by water, and only that water which is seen to gush forth in the nearby spring of that little house right next to it. They say this happens by a miracle, since the water itself is endowed with many virtues because the Blessed Virgin once stayed there for many years and used that water for drinking, for food, and for washing the swaddling clothes <term>panniculos<note>original: "panniculos"; literally "little rags," referring to the infant clothes of Jesus.</note></term> of the infant Jesus Christ. For this reason, all Egyptian men and women, and even the Arabs devoted to Muhammad, have held it in such great veneration <note>This refers to the "Matariya" spring near Cairo, a site of Christian pilgrimage that was also respected by Muslim rulers.</note> that they confidently use it for the cure of many diseases. Many come here from long distances and the most remote places to receive this kind of water; therefore, some say the balsam has survived here until now by the benefit of this water. These are the things they say about that tree.

**ABDEL.** Regarding the height, shape, and leaves of the balsam shrub, those things you have just put forward can in no way obscure the truth. For Dioscorides and others, who asserted that this plant was similar in size to *Lycium* <note>Box-thorn</note>, *Cytisus*, Terebinth, and *Pyracantha* <note>Firethorn</note>, were not at all in error, since all these shrubs do not differ much in size among themselves, and the balsam plant is no larger than them. But here no balsam trees are seen that are observed to be so large; indeed, the larger ones I have seen elsewhere here do not rise to a height greater than three cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches, meaning the plants were about 4.5 feet tall.</note>. However, I hear that in the soil of Arabia these plants grow to the height of the aforementioned shrubs, and even larger. In Egypt and Arabia, pomegranate trees are small, so that they are numbered among the shrubs and bushes and do not rise to such a height as is observed in parts of Italy. Therefore, Theophrastus should not be blamed for writing that balsam is similar to a large pomegranate tree, since our pomegranates are much lower than the Italian ones. But as for Justin...

<meta>This page continues the critique of ancient authors' descriptions of the balsam plant. The previous page listed various contradictory comparisons (to rue, marjoram, pitch-trees, and vines) and introduced the legend of the Virgin Mary at a spring in Egypt. This page specifically refutes the comparison to pitch-trees and provides a detailed botanical observation of the plant's growth stages.</meta>

-># DIALOGUE<-

...one who claims that the balsam plant is similar to a pitch-tree <note>original: "picei arbori"; referring to Justin's description mentioned on the previous page</note> must be believed to have been entirely deceived. The error appears conspicuous from his very own words; for while saying that balsam is similar to pitch-trees, he adds that they are cultivated in the manner of vines. Yet who has ever seen a pitch-tree that resembles a vine and is cultivated in the manner of a vineyard? There is no need to demonstrate now how great a difference exists between them. However, there is certainly no doubt that the balsam plant, with its shrubby and twiggy branches, is similar to a vine, nor is there any doubt that it is pruned every year just like vines are, because it is extremely shrubby and full of shoots. 

Yet, as far as the leaves are concerned, the plant is not similar to a vine, since it is filled with leaves that are somewhat close to those of rue <note>original: "rutæ"; Rue is a hardy, bitter herb with bluish-green leaves</note>, especially the last three leaflets seen on each wing of the balsam spray; these are very similar to those three leaflets positioned at the end of the wing of a rue spray, though the color is different. Nor is it absurd that Pausanias said balsam has leaves rivaling marjoram <note>original: "amaraci"; marjoram is an aromatic herb in the mint family</note>, because I suspect he inspected a balsam plant that was not fully grown, but only recently born from its seeds. Such young plants have leaves entirely similar to those of marjoram in size and shape, except for their thickness and color, in which these leaves differ from each other—for in marjoram they are thinner and whiter. 

Balsam that grows from seeds first has two leaves, very much like a sprouting vine when it is two-leafed. Those produced after these—which are three, four, or five in number—are closest to the leaves of marjoram. However, those that grow third appear as three leaflets each, and are seen to be closest to rue. I repeat: the first leaves to sprout are in pairs; those born second come forth very different from these, and are arranged irregularly on the spray. Those produced third are seen hanging as three leaflets on any given wing, which are closest to rue—I mean those three leaflets visible at the end of a rue spray. Fourthly, those that grow on the balsam spray are five in number, and later seven. 

These are the leaves of the balsam, seen very frequently on the balsam shrubs in this garden <note>original: "viridario"; likely the famous Balsam Garden at Matariyah, Egypt</note>, though described differently by ancient writers. Because of these observations and others, we rightly suspect that Dioscorides and many other ancient writers never actually inspected a living balsam plant. It is certain that this tree lived in this garden because it was irrigated by water from this nearby spring, in which all our people maintain that the Most Blessed Virgin once washed the swaddling clothes <term>panniculos<note>original: "panniculos"; literally "small cloths" or "rags," referring to the infant clothes of Jesus during the Flight into Egypt.</note></term> of our Savior as a boy. Nevertheless, many are found in sandy and desolate places which, however, remain sterile in regards to producing <term>opobalsamum<note>The precious resin or "juice of balsam" prized as a medicine and perfume.</note></term>. And so, these things in passing concerning the balsam...

### Chapter 4: From where the knowledge of true opobalsam is derived, and which of the ancient authors truly recorded this juice in their writings.

->ABDELLA<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial Q depicting a figure in a garden or landscape surrounded by foliage.</image-desc>
WHAT will you say, Alpinus, about the balsam plant? Has it not been properly described by us, as the truth required? And do the things I have said about its leaves, flowers, fruits, and twigs seem true to you? ALPINUS: Indeed, you have followed everything most correctly and truthfully. However, some things still remain to be said regarding the juice, the fruits, and the twigs, since the ancient writers who wrote about balsam seem to have reached almost no agreement among themselves in their descriptions of these parts. Their disagreement—as was also recently mentioned regarding the tree itself—has caused many to deny that the items shown to us as opobalsam, carpobalsam <note>original: "carpobalsamo"; the fruit of the balsam tree</note>, and xylobalsam <note>original: "xilobalsamo"; the wood or twigs of the balsam tree</note> are genuine. For this reason, they believe that no pure and legitimate opobalsam is imported to us. I desire for us to have a discussion about this first, and then about the others. Regarding opobalsam, then, many are of the opinion that the pure substance is not brought to us—and not without reason, since the opobalsam described by you and inspected by us many times seems quite different from that which all the ancient writers on herbal matters have recorded. For first of all, many of them produced evidence that it is white;
> indeed, Strabo used to say: by cutting its bark, they receive a
> juice in vessels that is very similar to a sticky milk. 
> And Pliny says: the juice flows from the wound, which they call
> opobalsam, of extraordinary sweetness, but it is collected in
> small amounts from the "tears" of the droplets using wool in a
> horn; from these it is stored in a new earthenware vessel, 
> similar to a thick oil, and white when in the must <note>original: "musto"; the juice of freshly crushed grapes, here likely referring to the resin's appearance or a mixture</note>. 
> And Simeon Sethus, a man who should not be placed behind
> others in the history of balsam, said: the juice flowed from it, 
> appearing similar to any oil, which is why it is named the oil of
> balsam; the juice gathered in these little vessels was received, 
> and indeed, that which floated on top was white and thin,

<meta>This page continues the dialogue on the properties of opobalsam, specifically addressing the contradictions in ancient descriptions by explaining how the resin’s color, consistency, and scent change as it ages.</meta>

# DIALOGUE

...since they say it is not thin, but thick, like those who said that when it is collected in shells it thickens <note>original: "coagulari"; to curdle or set</note>. Since truly the opobalsam <note>original: "opobalsamum"; the precious sap or "juice" of the balsam tree</note>, which you described and we have often inspected, is observed to be either green, or golden, or the color of honey, and is seen sometimes to be cloudy, sometimes clear and thin, and at other times thick like turpentine—for there is none among us who has not seen that juice varied by all these aforementioned differences: namely oily, green, golden, honey-colored, clear, cloudy, thin, and thick—it will not be surprising that there are various and doubtful opinions about it.

->ABDACH<-

Our friend Abdella will easily drive these ambiguities from our minds, especially since the explanation of these things depends on what he himself has recently brought forward. For he said that opobalsam itself varies according to its different ages in terms of its color, substance, transparency, scent, and lightness. When it has just trickled from the trunk and been collected, it is seen to be white in color—especially the part that floats on top, unlike that which sinks—which color in a short moment changes into an oily greenish hue. After five years, as we remember was also said above, this becomes golden, and finally, having reached old age and thickened, it takes on a darker honey color. 

It was said elsewhere that its various ages cause just as many changes in transparency, substance, scent, and lightness; for recently extracted opobalsam remains cloudy for five years, from which time it begins to clear, and for nearly ten years it is observed to be very clear and shining like gold; afterwards, as it grows old, it becomes cloudy again. Also, when immediately taken from the trunk, it is very thin, and a little while later it thickens slightly; hence Strabo <note>Greek geographer (c. 64 BC – 24 AD) who described the collection of balsam in the Levant</note> said that when it is received in shells it thickens. When five years have passed—which time is assigned to its "childhood"—it is purified, it deposits its dregs at the bottom of the vessel, and becomes very thin. When twice as many years have passed, as it ages it becomes thick, and it grows thicker the older it gets. 

The scent and taste are no less potent in the freshest sap; in the middle period the scent becomes sweeter, smelling of frankincense and turpentine <note>original: "terebinthum"; resin from the terebinth tree, often used as a point of comparison for high-quality resins</note> though with a sweeter fragrance; the taste is bitter, somewhat sharp, and astringent. Finally, in old age, all these qualities are perceived as greatly diminished. And for this reason, I think Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), Roman author of "Natural History"</note> was deceived, as he thought that the droplet <note>original: "lachrymam"; literally a "tear," the common term for a bead of resin</note> of this finest juice is perceived as fragrant only by rubbing it. In truth, opobalsam becomes odorless only when it is worn out by old age, so that only then can it be recognized by rubbing. In the beginning, this juice is also very light, which with the greatest difficulty in water, or another liquid...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the physical properties of opobalsam—specifically its buoyancy and solubility in water—change as the resin ages and thickens. It also begins a critique of ancient authorities like Pliny the Elder based on these observations.</meta>

# ON BALSAM

...dropped in, it does not settle, but is immediately carried to the surface of the water, and is quickly dissolved within the water. However, in its middle years of consistency, it behaves differently; as it grows old and its color fades, it thickens, with the result that if it is suddenly cast into water, it sinks. It is then carried back up to the surface of the water only very slowly, and dissolves with it with the greatest difficulty and very slowly. Our friend Abdella will judge these things—though perhaps not expressed in the most elegant speech—to be sufficient for the explanation of your difficulties.

**ABD.** I truly judge these things to be so true that all who, in writing or speaking about opobalsam <note>original: "opobalsamo"; the resinous juice or "tears" of the balsam tree</note>, have disagreed with these points, are utterly ignorant of the matter. From this, it is clearly evident that Pliny erred by creating various differences or species of this juice based on the various colors and substances he observed in opobalsam. For he had said:

<margin>>>
"
"</margin>
The test of the "tear" <note>the sap or resin</note> is that it be fatty, thin, and moderately reddish, and fragrant when rubbed; the second grade is a bright color; worse is the green; and the thick, black kind is the worst.

No Egyptian or Arab has seen white opobalsam except when it has very recently trickled from the tree; yet this color vanishes within a very few days and, as has been said, turns into the color of oil, and from that color into golden, and finally into a darker honey-color. As for black, it has never been seen or heard of by any of our people or the Arabs—unless perhaps it was recently brought from the West Indies <note>The Americas; at this time, resins from the New World were often confused with ancient medicinal balsams</note> and accepted by some as legitimate opobalsam, as we also mentioned above. However, this is clearly shown to be false, since those who wrote its history affirm that the juice improperly called "opobalsam" in the West Indies comes from a tall and large tree which differs very greatly in size, shape, branches, leaves, and fruit from the true balsam tree. It will not be difficult to persuade those who know both trees of this.

**ALPIN.** Simeon Seth <note>An 11th-century Byzantine physician known for his work on the properties of foods and medicines</note> also seems to confirm this himself: that the best opobalsam, very recently distilled from the trunk, has a white color; and so does Strabo <note>The ancient Greek geographer</note>, who writes that opobalsam is similar to milk. But although the white color of this juice vanishes within a few days of its trickling and changes into another color, nonetheless—and this will seem truly wonderful to many—opobalsam that appears oily in color, when dropped into water or milk, appears white again. The same happens with the golden and honey-colored kinds. Always, true opobalsam, whatever its color may be, will be seen as white when dropped and dissolved in water or milk. On this point, Dioscorides <note>The primary Roman-era authority on herbal medicine</note> spoke most learnedly:

<summary>Abdella and Alpinus argue that Pliny the Elder was mistaken in classifying balsam into different species, explaining that the different colors and thicknesses are merely stages of the same resin aging. They also distinguish true Middle Eastern balsam from the "balsam" being imported from the Americas.</summary>

# DIALOGUE.

...said: Furthermore, when pure, it dissolves very quickly in water or milk, and turns a milky color. No one would judge opobalsam <note>original: "opobalsamum"; the resinous juice or "tears" of the balsam tree</note> to be anything but white by sight—which experience will prove to be true if one allows it to drip into water, to dissolve, and to coagulate. For once dissolved upon the water, it is rendered so white that it can hardly be distinguished from the water itself; yet in a brief moment it coagulates, and is seen resting upon the water like a white web and floating on the surface. Its white color becomes even more manifest if the coagulated substance is removed or extracted from the water with some kind of stylus <note>a pointed tool or rod</note>. However, this experiment with the juice must be performed during the summer, or if in winter, near a fire, with the opobalsam first properly thinned by heat. But why does it change so quickly from white into another color? 

**ABDELLA.** Is it because the substance of that milky color is soft and very thin, so that it is quickly resolved and dissipated? Or is it because its internal heat, not yet powerful enough, has disturbed the juice while acting upon it? The same is observed in civet <term>Zebet: a musk-like substance secreted by the civet cat, used in perfumes and medicine</term>; for as soon as it is extracted from the scent glands <note>original: "testibus"</note>, it is tinged with a milky color, but as it begins to bubble slightly, it turns green, and finally becomes blackish. 

**ALPINUS.** All things have been correctly explained and recounted by you thus far. Now, therefore, I desire the same from you regarding the investigation and teaching of the marks or signs by which the true may be correctly distinguished from the false, and the pure from the adulterated.

---

### On the signs by which true opobalsam is safely known and discerned from the false, and the pure from the adulterated. Chapter V.

-> ABDELLA. <-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap initial 'H' featuring floral and leafy scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>

**B**Y these signs, everyone will accurately and most precisely distinguish this true juice from the false, and the legitimate from the adulterated, nor can anyone be so easily deceived. For that juice which has not yet begun to decline toward old age appears white in color (which is very rare, and only happens to be seen while it is dripping from the trunk or when very recently drawn out), or it is green, oily, and somewhat turbid—very similar in substance and color to oil—or it is a most shining golden, very thin, and very clear. Whosoever holds a sample with all these marks will find it has a powerful odor; and the more fresh it is, the sharper the scent, resembling turpentine <note>original: "terebinthum"; resin from the terebinth tree, often used as an adulterant</note> mixed with another sweeter...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from the previous section concerning the sensory characteristics of pure opobalsam and moves into a discussion on how to detect fraudulent or "adulterated" versions of the substance using various physical tests.</meta>

# ON BALSAM

<note>original: "DE BALSAMO"</note>

...[with] a stronger fragrance, and it will be most pleasant to the sense of smell. In taste, it will also be perceived as somewhat bitter, astringent, and moderately biting; it will be smooth and easy to dissolve. This characteristic can be recognized by dripping it into water or milk, for it will remain smooth, and it will stay beneath the water either not at all or for a very short time, and will rise up above it almost immediately. In the water, it is quickly dissolved or melted, retaining nothing oily or greasy, yet it is tenacious or endowed with a certain stickiness. By all these marks, true and pure opobalsam <note>original: "opobalsamum"; the resinous juice or "tears" of the balsam tree</note> will always be apparent to everyone. 

**ALPINUS.** Is it possible for someone to prepare a faked oil so similar to opobalsam that he might deceive many with it? 

**ABDELLA.** Without a doubt, since Galen <note>a prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note> said that even in his own time it was so heavily adulterated that it could scarcely be recognized as faked. However, when it is adulterated, it must necessarily be corrupted with some liquid, oil, or ointment. Dioscorides <note>author of "De Materia Medica," a foundational pharmacopeia</note> said opobalsam can be spoiled by mixing in ointments of turpentine, cypress, mastic, and balanos <term>Balanos: an oil made from the fruit of the Balanites aegyptiaca tree</term>, as well as lily-oil, metopium <term>Metopium: an Egyptian ointment made from bitter almonds and galbanum</term>, honey, and liquid Cyprian wax. Serapio <note>likely Serapion the Younger, an influential Arabic medical author</note> added to these rose oil, myrtle oil, and henna oil, which they call *cyprum*. Pliny states that a similar oil is extracted from the seeds of the true balsam—which they call carpobalsamum <term>Carpobalsamum: the fruit or seed of the balsam tree, used to produce a secondary oil</term>—and that many extract oil from these seeds in a way that is very similar to true opobalsam. However, it is distinguished from the true resin by a more bitter flavor and a much fainter scent of opobalsam. It is also reported that it can be adulterated with oils of rose, cyperus, mastic, balanos, turpentine, and myrtle, as well as resin, galbanum <note>a bitter aromatic gum resin</note>, and Cyprian wax—which indeed are the same substances that it was recently said Dioscorides mentioned under the name of ointments. But perhaps he would have better said they could be adulterated with oils rather than ointments, because that juice is thin just like an oil and not at all thick, unless it has been aged. 

Therefore, opobalsam can be corrupted by these things, the fraud of which is easily detected. Adulteration with oil is revealed in three ways: first, because when touched with the fingers, it does not stick with any tenacity or viscosity, whereas we have said elsewhere—and it is confirmed by many ancient writers—that true balsam juice is not without a certain tenacity and viscosity. Indeed, it is manifest to everyone that the stickiness of all resins is removed by oil. Furthermore, no one of us is unaware that when dripped into water, it spreads over its surface in the form of small circles <note>referring to the way oil forms "eyes" or slicks on water</note>. That pure opobalsam does not do this will be taught later. Finally, a stain is left on cloth infected with oil, which is both difficult to remove and daily [becomes] even greater...

->### DIALOGUE<-

...it expands into a circle, which does not happen with pure opobalsam <note>original: "opobalsamo"; the resinous juice of the balsam tree</note>. By these signs, therefore, opobalsam corrupted with oil can be revealed to anyone. By these marks, the defect will also be discovered if ointments composed of the same oils are mixed in, but there will be this further sign in them: that in the opobalsam, it is necessary for the ointments to sink to the bottom of the vessel within a short space of time. 

Indeed, a mixture containing wax will be detected by its cloudy color, which never becomes clear. If it is tainted with honey, a sweet taste reveals it, and as Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, the Roman naturalist</note> also testifies, it will attract flies to it. If it is adulterated with resin and dripped onto coals, it produces a blacker flame, and it will be seen to have a thicker substance than is found in the pure variety. 

Pure balsam, however, will become known by these signs; for besides those signs which we said it displays according to its various ages, we have also added these: namely, that besides having a powerful scent, when dripped into water—even if the drop is quite small—it expands over the entire surface of the water, and once dissolved, it floats on top and covers it all. Furthermore, it quickly thickens in the water and takes on the color of milk; and what is more, once it has entirely thickened, it can be picked up and removed from the water with a stylus <note>a small pointed tool or rod</note>. Also, when poured onto a woolen garment, it does not stain it as oil does, even though it sticks to it. It also curdles milk, though not in the same way as rennet <note>original: "coagulum"</note> does, because it does not curdle the milk perfectly; rather, it thickens along with the milk. 

**ALPINUS.** Many more signs than you have included regarding the marks of pure and corrupted opobalsam are read in many authors. Indeed, Dioscorides <note>the 1st-century Greek physician and author of "De Materia Medica"</note> had certainly related these things to us:

> The test of the juice is that it should be fresh, of a powerful scent, pure, not tending toward sourness, easy to dilute, smooth, astringent, and moderately biting to the taste.

And where he recalled those things by which opobalsam can be adulterated, he added these words: 

> For the pure juice poured onto a woolen garment neither leaves a spot nor, if it is washed out, leaves a mark behind. The corrupted kind, however, sticks, and when dripped into milk, it curdles it—which the adulterated version does not do. Furthermore, the pure kind dissolves very quickly in water or milk and turns milky in color, whereas the corrupted kind floats like oil, coiling upon itself or spreading out in the shape of a star... as pure balsam ages, it grows thick and becomes of poorer quality. Those are mistaken <note>original: "hallucinantur"; literally, they are wandering in their minds or hallucinating</note> who believe that when it is thickened by water it goes to the bottom and sinks, and afterwards, once dispersed, floats back to the top.

And Pliny says that the pure kind sticks, and a drop of it thickens in lukewarm water and sinks to the bottom; it curdles milk and does not leave a stain on a garment. And Avicenna <note>the Persian "Prince of Physicians," author of The Canon of Medicine</note> says: it curdles milk, mixes with water, and thickens within it; once thickened, it is preserved, and if caught on cotton <note>original: "gossypio"</note>, it can be washed away. Serapio <note>Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century medicinal writer</note> says the same things from...

<meta>This page continues a dialogue regarding the physical tests used to identify genuine opobalsam, moving from the observations of Pliny to the methods described by Dioscorides, Simeon Seth, and Avicenna.</meta>

->## ON BALSAM<-

,, Having already recited the teachings of Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, the 1st-century Greek physician and author of *De Materia Medica*</note>, he related these things to us. Simeon Seth <note>an 11th-century Byzantine Jewish physician known for his works on the properties of foods and medicines</note> also wrote the same himself. Some indeed prove its purity by this method: that iron smeared with it and placed in a fire will glow red-hot even when moved, a property which also occurs with oil of camphor and the other oils mentioned. Others claim that when placed in water, it does not float at all, but immediately seeks the bottom. This is true indeed, but when these two signs occur together—namely, that the iron glows and the substance easily settles in the water—seize it immediately. For then it is legitimate and not a forgery. 

,, Furthermore, one ought to know that while it is fresh and in its full strength, it floats upon water. This is tested in this way: a linen cloth is soaked in it, and when the cloth is later lifted, the substance entirely recedes from the linen, leaving no color behind, but only the sweetness of its scent. 

Having read all these things—which are quite diverse among themselves—in the works of these most serious writers on plants, and since I possess <term>opobalsam</term> <note>original: "opobalsamum"; the sap or "tears" of the balsam tree</note> which you affirm to be truly pure, not all the things taught by these writers represent it accurately. Indeed, there are some signs to which it clearly does not seem to correspond. For although my sample is fresh and shows no tendency toward acidity, possesses a powerful scent, an oily color, and was observed to be cloudy in its first years, it soon became clear, golden, slightly bitter, astringent, and moderately biting to the taste. It is tenacious, light, easily diluted, and curdles milk. It does not stain linen, and as Avicenna <note>the famous 11th-century Persian polymath and physician</note> says, even when dripped into water, it mixes and coagulates within it, and once coagulated, it floats on the surface. Furthermore, when iron is smeared with it and placed in the fire, as Seth wished it to be tested, it glows well. 

However, there is one claim affirmed by all the aforementioned ancient writers: that when it is dripped onto a woolen garment and washed with water, it can be entirely driven away so that nothing adheres to it. This I have never been able to observe. For no <term>opobalsam</term> is found which, when dripped onto a cloth, does not adhere to it to some degree. Because of this, I believe that no true <term>opobalsam</term> can pass that specific test, because—as is observed in all other resins—it is not devoid of viscosity and stickiness, since it is a resinous juice and can be counted among the resins without error. 

Based on these findings, I am very doubtful and uncertain regarding this particular sign of the juice noted by almost all the ancients. I desire to hear your opinion on this matter so that I may finally find rest in it. 

**ABDEL:** You are worthy of no small reproach for this hesitation of yours, you who value a single sign more than the many signs by which your <term>opobalsam</term> is proven...

<sig>V</sig>

<summary>A student questions the accuracy of ancient botanical authorities like Dioscorides and Avicenna, noting that while his balsam passes most purity tests, it fails the ancient claim that it leaves no residue on wool. He concludes that because balsam is a resin, it must naturally be sticky, contradicting the old texts.</summary>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on identifying genuine opobalsam. The speaker is currently critiquing the traditional "cloth test" mentioned by ancient authorities like Dioscorides, arguing that because balsam is a resin, it must inherently possess some stickiness.</meta>

->### DIALOGUE ###<-

You know well how to respond, if you wish to consider the matter, that I think this should not be called a true sign of this juice. Indeed, in what way would this juice—which is not lacking in toughness and <term>viscosity</term> <note>thickness or "stickiness" of a fluid</note> (since it is itself nothing other than a certain resin)—not stick at all when a little is dripped onto linen, or woolen cloth, or cotton? For not only is that juice tenacious, but even the green branches, if touched with the fingers, undoubtedly stick to them. This is something known for certain not only by us, but even by some of the ancients; did not **Strabo** <note>a Greek geographer and philosopher from the 1st century BCE</note> say: *Cutting its bark, they receive the juice in vessels, very much like a tenacious milk*? And what of **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, author of Natural History</note>? Did he not say, when teaching the marks of pure <term>opobalsam</term> <note>original: "opobalsami"; the purest sap harvested from the balsam tree</note>: *The pure juice of balsam ought to cling*?

But so that I do not delay your desire any longer, I will say in a few words what I think about this mark proposed by **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, the primary authority on ancient medicine</note>. For I think that Dioscorides, and others along with him, said that pure opobalsam does not stick to clothes or cloth for no other reason than to teach us that a mixture with some oil or ointment can be detected this way. For as I recently taught you (since it was often adulterated with oil in that time), balsam spoiled with oil and dripped onto a cloth—especially a woolen one, which is easier to stain—infects it just like oil does. Not only does it stick to the cloth forever, but the spot received in it grows and spreads more every day. 

By contrast, although pure opobalsam may stick a little when dripped onto cloth, it does not infect it like oil, nor does it leave a permanent stain. Rather, unlike oil, the amount adhering to the cloth decreases daily, until at last, after some time, it dissolves and vanishes of its own accord. For this reason, pure opobalsam does indeed stick to cloth, but not in the manner of oil, nor does it leave a stain. This is what my own opobalsam has often shown you when dripped onto my own clothing. 

However, one should not test this on those cloths that you prepare in Italy, which are far thicker than ours. Perhaps since your thick woolen cloths were not imported here from Italy in the past, and our people did not use them as they do now, Dioscorides did not intend for the test to be performed on them, but rather on our much thinner fabrics. Indeed, Pliny seems to have taught this sign more clearly when he said, *and it should not make stains on clothing*, rather than saying *it does not stick to clothing*, as is found in Dioscorides. Perhaps **Sethus** <note>Simeon Seth, an 11th-century physician</note> recorded the knowledge of opobalsam more truly through this sign, for it is read in his works in these words: *Upon the linen...*

# ON BALSAM
<meta>catchword: tos</meta>

...when [the cloth] is moistened by it, and afterwards the substance is removed from the linen, it leaves entirely, leaving behind not color, but only the sweetness of the scent: which clearly can be observed exactly <note>original: "ad unguem"; a Latin idiom meaning "to the fingernail," implying perfect precision</note> in our own [balsam]. Furthermore, from these various things read in different ancient writers concerning the signs distinguishing true **opobalsam** <note>original: "opobalsamum"; the most precious resin harvested from the balsam tree</note> from the false, I believe we should not without reason doubt that these authors perhaps never encountered the living plant anywhere, and much less inspected its pure juice, and that everything put forth by them was based on the reports of others rather than true observation. For this reason, the truth should not be sought from ancient writers who disagree among themselves on the history of balsam, but from those peoples, and especially the physicians, who have lived perpetually in the places where balsam (by the consensus of all writers) grew, and who have observed it very often. Indeed, for these reasons, a firmer trust should rightly be placed in them. These are the Egyptians and the Arabs, who all with one accord assert that, except for this opobalsam—which you do not believe to be true—no other has ever been known or used anywhere in all of Egypt, Arabia, or Syria. 

The clearest and most certain sign of this truth will be that no other kind of opobalsam, different from this one, was ever found at the court of the **Sultan** <note>original: "Turcarum Regem"; literally the King of the Turks</note> ruling those places, nor among the many Turkish officials who at various times governed all these aforementioned regions, especially Egypt; rather, what was found among them is plainly the same as ours, and differs in no way from that which is commonly accepted. For the Sultan receives every year from the **Sharif of Mecca** <note>original: "Seriph Mechae"; the hereditary religious and political leader of the holy city</note> and the lord of Medina two or more pounds of opobalsam sent as a gift, and he has continually received opobalsam from him ever since he began ruling Egypt. This is certainly plainly the same as that used by all Egyptians, Arabs, Syrians, and others. 

**ALPINI** <note>Prospero Alpini (1553–1617), the Venetian physician and botanist who wrote this dialogue based on his travels</note>: You speak the truth; for I know for certain that the opobalsam commonly received is the same as that held by many Turkish lords and **Venetian Consuls**, to whom it was given as a gift by the Governor of Egypt and other Turkish nobles; and the same as that which is seen today in the possession of the **Most Serene Francesco de' Medici, Duke of Florence** <note>Francesco I de' Medici (1541–1587), a famous patron of the sciences and alchemy</note>, which was formerly given to him as a gift by the Sultan of the Turks. It is certain that this, when correctly compared by the Duke himself with my own opobalsam sent from Egypt to the aforementioned Most Serene Duke last year, was found to be plainly the same. Wherefore all those who deny that the commonly accepted [balsam] is the true one, if they were to compare it with the opobalsam which is [found] among many...

...[the Vene]tians exist, formerly the most Illustrious Consuls of this place, and many others who once traveled through these regions and brought back **opobalsam** <note>original: "opobalsamū"; the highly prized resin of the balsam tree</note> obtained from certain Turkish lords; they will rightly recognize if it is the true substance. ABDEL. Yet I greatly wonder at **Avicenna** <note>original: "Auicenam"; the influential Persian physician and philosopher (c. 980–1037)</note>, who said that this juice can be washed out of **cotton** <note>original: "gossypio"</note> with water, even though he stated earlier that it coagulates and thickens when dripped into water. Since that is true, how can it be washed away with water once it has been received and soaked into cotton? It will be obvious to anyone that this is impossible. Finally, there is another opobalsam expressed from the seeds of the balsam, which we recently listed among the adulterated versions; it is sometimes sold as the true juice of opobalsam, yet it is easily understood to be different from the true one by its faint scent and more bitter taste. Let what we have said about opobalsam suffice. Now it remains for us to speak of the fruits and twigs of the balsam, since many people still contend that neither true **carpobalsam** <note>original: "carpobalsamum"; the fruit or seed of the balsam tree</note> nor **xilobalsam** <note>original: "xilobalsamum"; the wood or twigs of the balsam tree</note> actually exist.

## Identification and Consideration of True Carpobalsam and Xilobalsam. Chapter VI.

### ALPINUS.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'H' with floral and scrollwork designs.</image-desc>

HITHERTO, most learned man, you have dissolved all the ambiguities concerning the balsam plant and the juice drawn from it, and you have made the truth so clear that our people should no longer argue about the tree and its juice, but rather recognize it and restore it to medical use. Those who do so will be worthy of the highest praise, reviving and accepting such a great aid for use. Since none of us doubt any longer that true opobalsam is carried from Egypt and Syria to Italy, its purity will be easily recognized there from the signs recently provided. It remains now for you to pluck from the minds of our people any remaining doubt regarding the fruits and twigs of the same plant. While common seeds are accepted by many as the common (though false) carpobalsam—of which a great abundance is brought to us every year—in my judgment it should be affirmed that this is the true carpobalsam of the ancients. However, it must be noted that two kinds of carpobalsam are circulated...

<meta>This page continues Chapter VI, where Alpinus describes the physical characteristics of true carpobalsam (balsam fruit) to distinguish it from inferior substitutes. He specifically addresses the botanical differences and the pharmaceutical practices of the era.</meta>

-> ON BALSAM <-

...found, and both are accepted by some as the seeds of balsam. One of these seeds is the size of a **terebinth** <note>The terebinth or "turpentine tree" (Pistacia terebinthus) was a common point of comparison for ancient botanists due to its resinous nature.</note> fruit and is almost the same in shape—round and oblong, like an egg, sharp at the ends, and yellow, entirely covered by a blackish-red pod. This is the true <term>carpobalsamum</term> <note>original: "carpobalsamum"; literally "fruit-balsam," the seed of the balsam tree.</note> of the ancients. The other, a false <term>carpobalsamum</term>, is quite similar to the true one in size, color, and shape; however, it differs from the true variety because it is more oblong and is covered by its pod only halfway. Indeed, the end that is attached to its stem lacks a pod and is not entirely covered by it, but only up to the middle. Regarding this seed—which, in my judgment, is falsely accepted as the balsam seed—we shall not speak, even though it is fragrant and aromatic. This was perhaps the substance that, in the time of **Dioscorides** <note>original: "Dioscoridis"; the 1st-century author of De Materia Medica, the standard pharmacological text of the time.</note>, was exported from the city of Petra in Arabia as carpobalsamum, and with which the true <term>carpobalsamum</term> was adulterated, if it carried the flavor of pepper. We must speak, however, of the other seed, which differs from this false one by the aforementioned marks—most notably because it is entirely covered by its pod, whereas the false one, as has been said, appears to be covered by the pod only halfway.

I hear that now in Venice, all the pharmacists, on the advice of the most excellent college of physicians, substitute the fruit of the **Juniper** <note>original: "Iuniperi"</note> for the true balsam fruits when compounding <term>theriaca</term> <note>theriaca: a famous poly-pharmaceutical "universal antidote" containing dozens of ingredients, including opium and viper flesh.</note> and **mithridatium** <note>original: "mitridatio"; an ancient complex antidote named for Mithridates VI of Pontus.</note>. How worthy of reproach this is, and in how great an error they find themselves, has never been hidden from you, most learned men. And I wish that now, having considered the reasons, they would no longer allow <term>theriaca</term> and other medicines to be prepared without these true fruits, to the great disgrace of the art (since it is not difficult for them to find all these things); but I fear they would rather persist in ancient errors than embrace the truth of the matter.

**ABDEL.** What? Do they even deny that the fruits of the balsam—of which a great supply is brought to Italy and other places from here—are the true ones, and instead substitute Juniper berries for them in the preparation of <term>theriaca</term> and other medicines? What is this I hear from you? I am ashamed for such great physicians, who have neglected these true fruits until now and use them not at all. But by what reasons are they led to deny that these seeds—rightly accepted by you as the true <term>carpobalsamum</term> of the ancients—are the true fruits of the balsam? Will not their size, color, and shape be enough for them to recognize them? **Theophrastus** <note>original: "Theophrastus"; the "Father of Botany," a student of Aristotle.</note> said they are similar to the fruits of the terebinth in size, shape, and color. And **Serapio** <note>original: "Serapio"; likely referring to Serapion the Younger, an influential 12th-century Arabic medical author.</note> explained this more clearly according to the mind of Dioscorides...

<summary>Alpinus provides a botanical description of true carpobalsam compared to a false variety and strongly criticizes Venetian physicians for using juniper berries as a substitute in important medical antidotes. The dialogue highlights the tension between traditional medical authorities and the direct observation of botanical specimens in the Near East.</summary>

<keywords>carpobalsamum, theriaca, mithridatium, Venice, Juniper, Dioscorides, botanical substitution, pharmacology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Alpinus and Abdach regarding the botanical identification of true balsam fruit. The previous page established that Alpinus believes the seeds commonly found in Venice are the authentic carpobalsamum of antiquity, despite the skepticism of contemporary Italian pharmacists.</meta>

-> DIALOGUE <-

...he described them in this manner:

> And its grain, which is called <term>carpobalsamum</term> <note>original: "carpobalsamum"; the name for the fruit of the balsam tree, derived from the Greek *karpos* (fruit) and *balsamon*.</note>, is a round seed tending somewhat toward an oblong shape, with a slight sharpness at both ends, but thicker in the middle. Its color tends toward redness; it is full, heavy, and bites the tongue with a mild stinging sensation; when it is broken, it has a honey-like moisture inside, and its scent is pleasant.

But **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician whose *De Materia Medica* was the primary botanical authority for 1,500 years.</note> added that this seed smells of <term>opobalsamum</term> <note>original: "opobalsamum"; the precious resin or sap of the balsam tree.</note>. Certainly, to tell the truth, the balsam fruits neglected by your Italian physicians and pharmacists are found to correspond entirely to the description by **Serapio** <note>original: "Serapionis"; likely referring to Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century author of an influential book on medicinal simples.</note>, and also to the notes provided by **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus (c. 371–287 BC), the "Father of Botany."</note>, since these are seen to be very close in color, shape, and size to the fruits of the **terebinth** <note>The turpentine tree (*Pistacia terebinthus*), often used as a reference point for resinous plants.</note>. Serapio, however, described them much more diligently and exquisitely; he established that they are round seeds, oblong, with sharp ends and a thicker middle, tending toward a reddish color, heavy and full, and moderately biting to the taste, having a substance like honey inside. From these marks, it is most clearly shown that the common carpobalsamum is entirely authentic. But why do those men deny that these are the true fruits of the balsam—namely, the carpobalsamum of the ancients? **ALPIN.** They all proclaim with one voice that the aforementioned seeds do not possess even one, but rather lack all of the marks that Dioscorides—easily the first of all the ancients in the description of plants—had once recorded for memory, specifically in these words:

> For which reason, let the yellow, full, large, heavy, biting to the taste, moderately burning in the mouth, and smelling of opobalsamum be chosen.

They say, therefore, that those seeds shown by you as carpobalsamum are not seen to be yellow, as Dioscorides taught, but blackish-red; they are empty and not full, light and not heavy, small and not large; they do not bite the taste in any way, nor do they smell of opobalsamum. For these reasons, they deny that those seeds are the true carpobalsamum of the ancients, but rather those which were once imported from the town of **Petra** <note>A major trading hub in Roman Arabia (modern Jordan).</note> in Arabia; Dioscorides noticed that the true carpobalsamum was adulterated with these, which he himself taught in these words, saying:

> It is adulterated with a seed similar to **hypericum** <note>original: "hyperico"; likely St. John's Wort (*Hypericum perforatum*), used here as a botanical comparison for the size and shape of the seeds.</note>, which is brought from the town of Petra, but it is exposed by its size, its emptiness, its lack of potency, and its flavor of pepper.

**Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the *Natural History*.</note> left nearly the same things attested; and these are the arguments disputed against the common balsam fruits by many physicians and pharmacists of Italy and other nations. **ABDACH.** Of these two opinions, I think one must be true: either those men mentioned by you...

<summary>Alpinus defends the authenticity of his balsam fruit samples by comparing them to descriptions by Serapio and Theophrastus, while acknowledging that Italian critics believe they are inferior substitutes from Petra.</summary>
<keywords>carpobalsamum, opobalsamum, Serapio, Dioscorides, Theophrastus, Pliny the Elder, Petra, plant morphology, medicinal adulteration, botany</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the physician Prospero Alpini and the Egyptian scholar Abdel, debating the authenticity of the balsam fruits (carpobalsamum) found in contemporary markets. Alpinus argues that the physical characteristics perfectly match the descriptions of ancient authorities, despite the doubts of European pharmacists.</meta>

# ON BALSAM
<meta>catchword: better</meta>

...that they never correctly knew the cited marks of <term>carpobalsamum</term> <note>original: "carpobalsami"; the fruit of the balsam tree, highly valued in ancient medicine.</note>, or never inspected these fruits or seeds which all these pharmacists have before their eyes every day. For if they had ever inspected them and correctly observed them, it would undoubtedly have been impossible for them not to recognize from all the marks that these are the true fruits of the balsam tree. It is not only false that they lack all the marks handed down by **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), the Greek author of the foundational pharmacopeia *De Materia Medica*.</note>, but not even one is missing from them. 

For these seeds are full of a moisture similar to honey; if they had inspected many of these broken open inside, unless they were altogether blind, it could in no way have happened that they did not notice it. They are also seen to be much larger than the seeds of <term>St. John’s wort</term> <note>original: "hyperici"; a common herb used here as a botanical reference for size comparison.</note>; they are heavy, with a certain stinging bite, and fragrant with the scent of <term>opobalsamum</term> <note>original: "opobalsami"; the precious resin or sap of the balsam tree.</note>. Anyone whose sense of taste and smell is not entirely lacking will be able to recognize their flavor and scent. In my opinion, there will be no one who, having first smelled and tasted the balsam sap, would not most clearly recognize that same flavor and scent within these broken seeds upon smelling and tasting them. 

-> ABDEL <-

You speak the absolute truth, and these things are worthy of being heard and noticed by those physicians. For those who have traveled through those regions truly testify that these common seeds received by us as balsam fruit are the same ones gathered from the balsam shrubs in many places of <term>Arabia Felix</term> <note>original: "Arabiae Foelix"; "Happy Arabia," a Roman term for the fertile southern parts of the Arabian Peninsula, modern-day Yemen and Oman.</note>. Nor did the Egyptian pharmacists ever use any others as balsam fruits to compose medicines. Nor does it stand in the memory of other Egyptians that any fruits other than these were ever brought to us as balsam fruit from the town of <term>Petra</term> <note>The historic city in modern Jordan, which Alpinus here notes was being associated with Mecca in contemporary trade descriptions.</note>, which is now called **Mecca**. 

Nor does **Galen** <note>Aelius Galenus (129–216 AD), the most famous physician of the Roman Empire.</note> think that men can wander or be deceived in the knowledge of those seeds, since he said in his book *On Antidotes* <note>original: "libro de Antid."; referring to Galen's work *De Antidotis*.</note> that balsam fruit cannot be adulterated—and rightly so, since it seems hardly possible that someone who has seen the true seeds of balsam just once could be deceived by others. But even if it were granted that it could be adulterated, it would still be recognized, at least as Dioscorides had taught, by its emptiness, smallness, and the flavor of pepper. Indeed, I cannot help but strongly wonder at those physicians who say that the marks of balsam fruit were handed down to us by Dioscorides so that these seeds might be distinguished from others; for he did not hand down those marks for that reason, but rather so that well-learned men might select from the balsam seeds those that are yellow, full, larger, heavy, and endowed with the taste and scent of the balsam sap, since these are better than the others...

<summary>Alpinus and Abdel defend the authenticity of the balsam seeds found in Egypt, arguing that their physical traits—honey-like interior, scent, and taste—perfectly align with the descriptions of Galen and Dioscorides. They dismiss the ignorance of European pharmacists who fail to recognize the genuine product imported from Arabia.</summary>

<keywords>carpobalsamum, opobalsamum, Dioscorides, Galen, Arabia Felix, Mecca, Petra, pharmacology, botanical identification, balsam fruit</keywords>

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 76, where Alpinus and Abdel discuss the sensory characteristics of the true balsam fruit (carpobalsamum) to prove its identity against the skepticism of European pharmacists.</meta>

->### DIALOGUE<-

...better ones exist. Who does not know that even farmers themselves, when buying any seeds—whether it be wheat or barley—select those that are full, large, heavy, and of the best taste and smell, and reject those that are empty, small, and light? Why is that? Because the former are useless, while the latter are the best.

**ALPINUS**: They say that the yellow color, by which **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a Greek physician of the 1st century AD whose work was the standard botanical reference for centuries.</note> taught that the true seeds of balsam are to be recognized, is not seen in these seeds, since they are observed to be reddish or even blackish instead.

**ABDEL**: They say rightly, but from this it is inferred that they are very little practiced in the observation of these seeds, and have never inspected them diligently; since if they had observed many of them often, diligently, and in various ways, they would have undoubtedly known that they are filled with a double color: namely, reddish-black when clothed in their husks, and yellow when stripped and bared of them. From this observation, it is clear that neither **Dioscorides** (teaching that the seed is yellow) nor **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus (c. 371–287 BC), known as the "father of botany."</note> along with **Serapion** <note>Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century author who synthesized Greek and Arabic medical knowledge.</note> (describing it as reddish-black) gave a false account. For the latter understood the seed as clothed in its husk, which is seen as blackish-red, while Dioscorides understood it as stripped of its husk, which is yellow. 

And let these things we have said about <term>carpobalsamum</term> <note>original: "carpobalsamo"; the fruit of the balsam tree.</note> be sufficient. Concerning <term>xilobalsamum</term> <note>original: "xilobalsamo"; the wood or twigs of the balsam tree, used in medicinal preparations.</note>, however, I do not think many things need to be said by us, since it does not seem so unknown to everyone as the fruit does. That these twigs are commonly accepted by all pharmacists as <term>xilobalsamum</term>, even if they do not use them, will be the most certain proof; for they give off the scent of <term>opobalsamum</term> <note>original: "opobalsami"; the precious sap or "juice" of the balsam tree.</note> and the fruit, and they even offer the same flavor to the taste. The best are those that are twiggy, thin, tawny, and recently cut, breathing forth a most manifest scent of the balsam resin. It was recorded by **Theophrastus** and **Serapion** that they breathe this extraordinary scent. Pharmacists should use these while they are fresh, since in a short time they are deprived of all scent and flavor; for so great is the thinness of that moist substance in which the scent and flavor are grounded, that it quickly evaporates, and the flavor and scent vanish along with it.

**ALPINUS**: Our pharmacist, **Domenico Rege** <note>Dominicus à Rege; a contemporary apothecary from the prominent Rege family in Venice.</note>, had a bundle of very fresh twigs from his Turkish friend at the town of **Suez** <note>original: "Sues"; the port city on the Red Sea.</note>, brought there from **Mecca** <note>original: "Mecha"; the balsam tree was famously cultivated in the gardens of Matariyya in Egypt, but also grew in the Arabian Peninsula.</note>; it breathed a strong scent of balsam resin, yet in a few months it was almost entirely spent. I have had many fresh twigs, which are still kept with me; when compared with those that are in the shops of the **Venetian pharmacists**, they are certainly recognized without a doubt to be the same, which, if they were kept fresh, the truth...
