<meta>This page transitions from Alpini’s original dialogue into a supplementary section. Alpini explains why he is including the observations of the French naturalist Pierre Belon, whose earlier travels in Egypt corroborate Alpini's own findings on the balsam plant.</meta>

<page-num>85</page-num>
<header>ON BALSAM</header>

## Why the observations of Pierre Belon, made in Egypt concerning the balsam plant, are recorded here.
### Chapter VII.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'C' containing foliate and ornamental patterns.</image-desc>
Since in these days I was about to publish the book I once wrote in Egypt about balsam, behold, there came into my hands the most learned travel observations by Pierre Belon <note>original: "Petro Bellonio"; Pierre Belon (1517–1564) was a pioneering French explorer and naturalist who traveled through the Levant and Egypt decades before Alpini.</note> of Le Mans, a man most learned both in other matters and especially in the study of plants <term>materia plantarum</term> <note>often referred to as *materia medica*, the study of the medicinal properties of natural substances.</note>. These were committed to writing forty years ago, and only published this year. When I read in these his most diligent observation of certain balsam plants formerly living in Egypt—most accurately drafted and described—I thought it would be best to add it at the end of this little book. Indeed, I hoped that from the reading of such a man, who was most practiced in the study of plants, those things which were said by us and committed to writing concerning the history of balsam might greatly confirm the minds of the readers, because he included nearly everything that is read in my own book. It pleased me to preface these things before I added Belon's observation of balsam here, so that they would not seem to the honest reader to have been inserted here too abruptly.

## Observation of Egyptian balsam collected from Pierre Belon in Book II of his travel observations, Chapter XXXIX.
### Chapter VIII.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'I' with floral motifs.</image-desc>
In a certain village, we went to see a garden planted with balsam shrubs, which is no further from Cairo <note>original: "Cayro"</note> than Lendit <note>original: "Lendit"; Belon uses a landmark familiar to his French readers: the Lendit plain or fair, located between Paris and Saint-Denis.</note> is from Paris <note>original: "Lutetia"</note>. Furthermore, since the balsam plant is quite famous, precious, and rare, it pleased me to write here whatever we judged to pertain to its history. We know there are some who think the balsam of Matarea <note>original: "Matareæ"; Al-Matariyyah, a site near Cairo famous for its balsam gardens and the "Virgin's Tree."</note> was brought from Judea; however, we shall show later that the matter is not so. They are in a certain large garden, but enclosed by walls in a narrow space, which they say were built after the Turk <note>The Ottoman Sultan Selim I, who conquered Egypt in 1517.</note> took Egypt from the Sultan <note>The Mamluk Sultan.</note>. The Pasha <note>original: "Bacha"; the Ottoman governor.</note>, however, [appointed] by the Emperor of the Turks to this region...

<meta>The text cuts off mid-sentence. The catchword "ni" likely begins the word "nisi" or a similar continuation regarding the governor's administration of the garden.</meta>

<summary>Alpini introduces and begins quoting the 16th-century French naturalist Pierre Belon to validate his own botanical findings, specifically describing a balsam garden in Matarea, Egypt.</summary>
<keywords>Pierre Belon, Cairo, Matarea, balsam garden, botanical history, Ottoman Egypt, Saint-Denis</keywords>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the observation of Pierre Belon regarding the balsam grove at Matarea (Al-Matariyyah) near Cairo. The previous page introduced Belon as a source whose findings corroborate Alpini’s own research.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>
->DIALOGUE<-

...the governor <note>original: "præfectum"</note> deemed those shrubs worthy of being enclosed in a special place. When we were there, only nine or ten of its shrubs were present, which yielded no liquid <note>referring to the precious resin or "balm" for which the plant was famous</note>. Among the signs passed down by the ancients by which balsam can be recognized, one is that it remains perpetually green; yet there at Matarea, near Cairo, it had very few leaves in the month of September, which seemed strange to us. For other trees that are usually green in winter only shed their leaves in the early spring when they have produced new shoots, and they tend to be greener in autumn than in early spring. But other trees that shed their leaves usually do so toward winter, so that they may acquire new ones in the summer. Therefore, it seemed contrary to nature to us that the balsam should shed its leaves so that it might produce new shoots in winter; for as we observed it, whatever leaves it had were newly born.

We cannot report its true size; for as many of its shrubs as were present in the garden had only a trunk of a foot in length and a thumb’s thickness, furnished with thin little branches and covered with sparse leaves. However, wherever they may eventually grow, they do not exceed a height of two or three cubits <term>cubit</term> <note>An ancient unit of length, roughly the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger, about 18 inches.</note>; and when they are a foot high, they split into many slender branches no thicker than the quill of a goose feather. The balsam shrubs of Matarea had just recently been pruned, so that only the main stems remained, which were producing the beginnings of little branches. For balsam is of the same nature as the vine, which must be pruned every year; otherwise, it degenerates. 

Those new shoots of the balsam were hidden under a reddish bark and had green leaves, shaped in the manner of lentisk <term>lentiscus</term> <note>The Mastic tree (Pistacia lentiscus), known for its resin and pinnate leaves.</note> leaves, growing on a stem <note>original: "nervo"</note> on both sides, as can be seen in the leaves of roses, or ash, or walnut. They did not, however, exceed the size of chickpea leaves, and the series of them was so constructed that the final leaflet, occupying the very tip, created an odd number; for when the leaflets of the whole wing <note>the botanical "pinna" or leaf structure</note> are counted, they are found in threes, fives, or sevens. We certainly hardly noticed them exceeding the number of seven. The end leaflet is larger than the others; indeed, moving away from it, the rest become gradually smaller, as in the leaves of rue <term>ruta</term> <note>A hardy, bitter herb (Ruta graveolens) with distinctively shaped blue-green leaves.</note>. Pliny, in his history, followed Theophrastus in all things, just as Dioscorides also did; and following in their footsteps, they compared the leaves of the balsam to the leaves of rue, which we found to be consistent with the truth. However, because we had not observed it exactly the first time at Matarea, we went to look at it again, and having obtained a branch of it, we [studied] it...

<meta>catchword: of it [illius]</meta>

<summary>This page describes Pierre Belon's firsthand botanical observations of the balsam plant in Egypt, noting its unusual shedding cycle, its physical dimensions, and the specific shape and arrangement of its leaflets, which he compares to rue and lentisk.</summary>
<keywords>balsam, Matarea, Cairo, botany, Pliny, Theophrastus, Dioscorides, plant morphology, pruning, evergreen</keywords>

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's detailed physical description of the balsam shrub, following the previous page's discussion of its pruning and growth habits in the gardens of Al-Matariyyah.</meta>

# ON BALSAM

We tasted its leaves, which we found to possess a somewhat astringent quality, a slightly oily flavor, and an aromatic one; however, the bark of the branches is more fragrant than these. The small branches are endowed with a double bark: the outermost is reddish on the exterior, covering the inner green bark like a membrane. When tasted, this bark has a flavor somewhere between frankincense <note>original: "Thus"</note> and the leaf of the turpentine tree <note>original: "Terebinthi"</note>, with a flavor very similar to wild savory <note>original: "satureiæ syluestri"</note> and quite pleasant; when crushed between the fingers, it smells of cardamom. The wood itself is white and devoid of smell or taste, no different from any other useless wood. Its stems are straight and slender, covered with leaf clusters growing at intervals in a scattered and jumbled order, which (as we said) consist of three, five, or seven leaflets.

The opinions of authors who have written about balsam are varied; and if we ourselves had not seen the plant, we would never have dared to write anything about it after them. We might have even leaned toward the opinion that it was never cultivated in the plains of *Jericho* <note>an ancient city in the Jordan Valley famous for its historic balsam groves</note>. However, because we saw the shrub itself and observed it diligently, we chose to discuss it as a matter worthy of careful study. By experience we learned that the wood commonly called *wood-balsam* <term>xilobalsamum</term>, which is brought from *Arabia Felix* <note>Latin for "Happy Arabia," referring to the fertile southern region of the Arabian Peninsula</note> and sold by merchants, matches the *Egyptian* variety cultivated in *Matarea* <note>Al-Matariyyah, the site of the famous balsam garden near Cairo</note>. For we compared our dried balsam branch with that which is found for sale in the apothecary shops and discovered that it corresponds in every detail. One of two things is necessarily true: either the *wood-balsam* <term>xilobalsamum</term> and *fruit-balsam* <term>carpobalsamum</term> sold in the shops is counterfeit, or that *Egyptian* variety grown in the garden at *Matarea* is judged to be the legitimate balsam. Since they agree in all respects and we are certain they are the same, we assert and conclude that what is found for sale under this name is similar to that which has been in use since antiquity.

At the present time, balsam is cultivated only in Egypt near *Cairo*. And although *Theophrastus* <note>an ancient Greek philosopher and the "father of botany"</note> is of the opinion that it is not found growing wild or spontaneously, we nevertheless dare to assert that it has been in *Arabia Felix* in every age, and even now grows there, and that its wood and fruit were brought to us even in olden times along with other Arabian wares. We wish to demonstrate that these were no less known to merchants than other spices, as is apparent from the compositions of medicines into which they were constantly added. Did not *Mithridates* <note>Mithridates VI, a king of Pontus famous for developing a complex "universal antidote" against poison</note> include it in his medicine? Were they not found for sale in apothecary shops? This can be proved by the authority of *Dioscorides* <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, the most influential ancient Greek physician and pharmacologist</note> alone, who in his time [found] *fruit-balsam* <term>carpobalsamum</term>...

<summary>Alpini provides a sensory analysis of the balsam plant's bark and leaves, concluding that the cultivated Egyptian variety is identical to the balsam products imported from Arabia. He argues against ancient authorities who claimed the plant did not grow wild, asserting its long-standing presence in Arabia Felix.</summary>
<keywords>Balsam, Wood-balsam, Fruit-balsam, Arabia Felix, Cairo, Al-Matariyyah, Botanical description, Pharmacy, Theophrastus, Dioscorides</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's discussion of the balsam plant, moving from a physical description to a survey of classical authorities and modern testimony regarding its geographical origins and the history of its transplantation to Egypt.</meta>

# DIALOGUE.

Pliny complains that it is first adulterated. <term>Fruit-balsam</term> <note>original: "Carpobalsamū"</note> (he says) is adulterated with a seed similar to Saint John's wort <note>original: "hyperico"</note>, which is brought from the town of Petra. By the "town of Petra," we understand Mecca. Regarding <term>wood-balsam</term> <note>original: "xilobalsamo"</note>: from the type of wood they call wood-balsam, the best is proven to be fresh, with a slender branch, tawny and fragrant, inasmuch as it smells of the <term>sap-balsam</term> <note>original: "opobalsamum"</note>. From these facts it is clearly apparent that its common use was established among other spices. But it is also manifest from the words of Diodorus Siculus, a very ancient writer, who enumerates the riches of <term>Arabia Felix</term> <note>Latin for "Happy Arabia," referring to the fertile southern region of the Arabian Peninsula; original: "Arabiæ fęlicis"</note>. For he says that balsam is produced in maritime places. Therefore, he does not understand it to be cultivated, but growing spontaneously. Pausanias also relates that the balsam of Arabia is a shrub. However, most authors treating balsam do not agree among themselves. For Strabo claims it grows in Syria near Lake Gennesaret, between Mount Lebanon and Antilibanus. Others maintain that the region of Judea alone produces it, and that its branches must be wounded only with a bone knife or glass to draw out the liquid; otherwise, if they are cut with iron, they immediately die. Cornelius Tacitus writes that when iron is brought near, it shudders with fear, and therefore it must be wounded with instruments other than iron if one desires to elicit the liquid. When we inquired of the merchants of Cairo about balsam while comparing it with our own small branch, they affirmed to us that whatever wood-balsam or fruit-balsam they had ever sold, they had received it all from Mecca along with other spices;

->and they remembered that in their time the balsam shrubs, which<-
->are now cultivated in Matarea, had been<-
->brought from Arabia Felix at the great expense of<-
->the Sultan. Because indeed so<-
->many affirmed this to us, we<-
->thought that without any<-
->scruple or concealment<-
->we were permitted<-
->to write<-
->it.<-

->Praise to God, the Best and Greatest.<-

# THE END.
